# OnePulse — CLAUDE.md

OnePulse is a fresh, enterprise-grade rebuild of the problem validated by
`singleSlide_demo` (a separate, untouched reference repository). Everything
here is written new against the design specs below — nothing is ported.

## Document Index

All six live in `./docs`. Read the relevant one before touching the area it
governs — don't implement from memory of a summary.

| Doc | File | What it covers |
|---|---|---|
| PRD | `docs/OnePulse_PRD.md` | Problem validation, goals/non-goals, personas, FR-1…FR-15, NFR-1…NFR-12, success metrics. The source of truth for every requirement ID. |
| PRD Addendum | `docs/OnePulse_PRD_Addendum.md` | Replaces PRD §5 with job-to-be-done personas; adds kill/pivot criteria and a stakeholder RACI. Amends, doesn't reopen, the base PRD. |
| Physical Architecture | `docs/OnePulse_Physical_Architecture.md` | Real technology assignments (AKS, Foundry, Azure Database for PostgreSQL Flexible Server, Temporal, Kafka, Arize, Azure AI Search, Apigee, Entra ID) per logical component, justified against specific requirements or POC findings. Postgres technology was revised from the original Neon choice — see the Section 4 revision note. |
| High-Level Design | `docs/OnePulse_High_Level_Design.md` | Service behavior: onboarding completeness mechanism, the core report generation cycle (9 steps), the revision-cap decision logic, conversational access flow, resilience patterns, availability design. |
| Low-Level Design | `docs/OnePulse_Low_Level_Design.md` | Concrete schemas, API contracts (trigger cycle, review/approve, chat query), and the exact service configuration values (§3 — reproduced verbatim below). |
| DevOps Setup | `docs/OnePulse_DevOps_Setup.md` | CI/CD pipeline stages, the two mandatory gates (Migration Verification, Cost Regression), environments, secrets/identity handling, deferred canary rollout spec. |
| Build Plan | `docs/OnePulse_Build_Plan.md` | The Now-scope build sequence, 8 phases, each with a Definition of Done and a Claude Code kickoff prompt. **This is the phase tracker.** |

Reference-only supporting docs live in `./docs/reference` (Conceptual
Architecture, Solution Architecture Simple, MVP Prioritization, Product
Discovery & Adoption Plan) — pull these in only if a specific question
genuinely needs them, not as background reading.

## Configuration Values (Low-Level Design §3 — verbatim, never reconstruct from memory)

| Setting | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Synthesis/Critique revision cap | `max_revisions = 1` | Tied directly to the POC's cost model; changing this silently invalidates that math. |
| Feature Investigation turn budget | `max_turns = 6` | 5 was the original estimate; 6 accounts for a mandatory tool-discovery step found only through live debugging. |
| Model call isolation flags | `setting_sources=[]`, `skills=[]`, `strict_mcp_config=True` on every agentic call | The exact fix for a real, confirmed 10x cost anomaly found in the POC's observability trace — mandatory on every AI service, not optional. |
| On-demand trigger rate limit | 2 per Portfolio Lead per day, enforced at the API Gateway | Prevents repeating the exact cost-anomaly pattern already seen once in this project's history. |
| Service replica count | Minimum 3 per core service, spread across availability zones | Realizes the HLD §6 availability approach (closes NFR-12). |
| Content safety check | Mandatory call to Azure AI Content Safety after Quality Assurance, before Human Governance | Closes the confirmed gap that Foundry does not apply automatic content filtering to Claude models (NFR-8). |

## Local Dev Script Toggle — `ONEPULSE_KEEP_AGENTS`

Added 2026-09-05, `onepulse_common/agent_cleanup.py`. Every spike/demo
script that creates a throwaway agent (`foundry_agent_spike.py`,
`ado_investigation_spike.py`, `observability_spike.py`,
`observability_export_run.py`, `run_pipeline.py`) deletes it via
`maybe_delete_agent()` instead of calling `agents_client.delete_agent()`
directly. An explicit `ONEPULSE_KEEP_AGENTS` value (`true`/`false`)
always wins; when unset, each call site's own `default_keep` applies —
`False` (delete immediately, keeping the portal clean) for every script
except `run_pipeline.py`, which passes `default_keep=True`: every run of
that specific entry point is a deliberate, real demo run, not quiet
iteration, so its agents and threads should persist by default. This is
a local development convenience only; it has no bearing on any real
Phase 3+ service, which will have its own real agent-lifecycle design.

## Foundry Agent Integration — Path Resolution

Resolved 2026-09-05, revised same day (Phase 0/Task 10). There are now
five distinct SDK surfaces across this project's history; do not
conflate them.

- **Primary, as of Task 10: Microsoft Agent Framework**
  (`agent_framework` + `agent_framework.foundry`, specifically
  `FoundryChatClient` + `Agent`). This is what `scripts/run_pipeline.py`'s
  four agents (Investigation, Status Analysis, Synthesis, Self-critique)
  actually run on. Reasoning: classic `AgentsClient` is headed for
  retirement (March 2027) and has no OpenInference instrumentor (confirmed
  via an open GitHub feature request); Agent Framework does
  (`openinference-instrumentation-agent-framework`), which is what
  actually gets traces shaped for Arize's eval UI rather than raw,
  likely-unusable OTLP data.

  Real findings from this migration, each checked live, not assumed:
  - `agent-framework-foundry` (1.12.0, stable) is the correct package —
    not `agent-framework-azure-ai` (stuck at `1.0.0rc6`, narrower,
    evidently legacy/superseded).
  - Installing it downgraded `azure-ai-projects` 2.6.0→2.3.0 (its own pin
    is `<2.4.0`). Checked live: the one call this project makes against
    it, `telemetry.get_application_insights_connection_string()`, still
    works correctly at 2.3.0 — a checked, not assumed, non-issue.
    `libs/onepulse_common/pyproject.toml` now pins `>=2.2.0,<2.4.0` to
    match reality.
  - `FoundryChatClient(credential=...)` accepts our existing sync
    `DefaultAzureCredential` directly (`TokenCredential | AsyncTokenCredential`)
    — official docs examples show the async variant, but it isn't required.
  - `response_format` / `default_options` accepts our existing raw JSON
    schema dicts unchanged (`type[BaseModel] | Mapping[str, Any]`) — no
    forced rewrite to Pydantic.
  - **Real, accepted trade-off:** classic `AgentsClient.create_agent()`
    created a portal-visible server resource (Task 9's entire
    `ONEPULSE_KEEP_AGENTS` point). The client-side `Agent(client=
    FoundryChatClient(...))` construction used here creates no such
    resource. The portal-visible alternative, `FoundryAgent`, does not
    support local stdio MCP tools (only hosted Foundry Toolbox
    endpoints) — using it would mean standing up new infrastructure, not
    a code change. User's call: keep the real local MCP bridge, accept
    losing portal visibility; Arize's own dashboard is the intended
    replacement. `ONEPULSE_KEEP_AGENTS` no longer applies to
    `run_pipeline.py` as a result — it still applies to every other
    script, which stayed on classic `AgentsClient`.
  - **Real, confirmed bug pattern in `agent-framework-core==1.17.0`'s
    native MCP client** (`agent_framework/_mcp.py`, i.e. `MCPStdioTool`):
    three separate camelCase-vs-snake_case mismatches found live in a
    row against the installed `mcp==2.1.1` —
    `InitializeResult.protocolVersion` (real field: `protocol_version`),
    `mcp.shared.exceptions.McpError` (real name: `MCPError`), and
    `tool.inputSchema` (real field: `input_schema` — the *exact* bug this
    project already found and fixed in its own
    `ado_investigation_spike.py` weeks earlier). `agent-framework-core`
    does not even declare `mcp` as a hard dependency (confirmed via
    `pip show`), so there's no version pin to fall back to, and
    downgrading `mcp` below 2.0 would break `scripts/pptx_mcp_server.py`
    (needs `mcp>=2.0`'s `MCPServer`, per Task 6). Three-for-three, same
    root cause, crossed the threshold for "stop and report" rather than
    keep patching an unknown remainder — decision: **don't use
    `MCPStdioTool` at all.** `run_pipeline.py` instead reuses the
    project's own already-proven `mcp.ClientSession` bridge, wrapping
    each real MCP tool as a plain `agent_framework.FunctionTool`
    (`input_model=` accepts the same raw runtime JSON schema `tools`
    already discover). Verified live: real tool calls, real Investigation
    and Status Analysis output, unaffected.

- **Superseded, not deleted: `azure.ai.agents.AgentsClient`** (classic
  thread → message → run, `create_and_process`). Verified live via the
  Phase 0/Task 3 go/no-go checkpoint (`onePulse-gpt-5-mini` deployment,
  `DefaultAzureCredential` — see `scripts/foundry_agent_spike.py`), and
  correctly primary through Tasks 4-9. Stays in place, unmigrated, in
  `scripts/foundry_agent_spike.py`, `scripts/ado_investigation_spike.py`,
  `scripts/observability_spike.py`, and `scripts/observability_export_run.py`
  — kept for real historical reference (the same treatment as every other
  superseded path here), not deleted just because `run_pipeline.py`
  moved on. Construct it directly against the Foundry project endpoint
  with `DefaultAzureCredential` — **not** via `AIProjectClient.agents`,
  which in `azure-ai-projects>=2.2.0` is a different, newer "Agent
  resource" surface with no thread/run concept at all (see deferred item
  below).

- **Superseded, not deleted: Claude Agent SDK / `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_FOUNDRY=1`.**
  `onepulse_common/foundry.py`'s original scope note flagged this as the
  expected path for agentic, tool-using services, pending live
  verification against real infrastructure (convention #6). That
  verification happened via the AgentsClient spike instead (and has now
  itself been superseded by Agent Framework) — the note stays in
  `foundry.py`, marked superseded in place: it documents a real decision
  point, the same treatment `AGENT_CALL_ISOLATION` gets above — kept for
  history, never silently erased.

- **Fallback, documented only: Messages API via `AnthropicFoundry`**
  (`onepulse_common/foundry.py`, `create_foundry_client`). Still correct
  as-is for a zero-tool-call service like Narrative Synthesis. Not in
  competition with any agent path above — different use case, not a
  downgrade.

- **Deliberately deferred, not disqualified: the newer "Agent resource"
  model** — `AIProjectClient.agents.create_version`, Hosted Agents,
  `.get_openai_client()` + Responses API. Not explored, because of this
  week's timeline, not because anything found about it is wrong. This is
  also what `FoundryAgent` (Agent Framework's portal-visible agent class,
  see above) is actually built on. Revisit once Now-scope is stable.

## Observability Scope — Two Different Tools, Not One "Observability" Checkbox

Resolved 2026-09-05 (Phase 0/Task 8), stated here so it never quietly
blurs into an undifferentiated "we added observability" claim later:

- **Foundry-native GenAI tracing (`azure.ai.agents.telemetry.AIAgentsInstrumentor`
  + Application Insights)** gives **infra-level tracing only**: latency,
  token counts, tool-call spans, thread/run/agent IDs, message content
  (if content recording is on). Confirmed by both direct SDK source
  inspection and a real live export — see Task 8. It carries no
  groundedness, hallucination, or quality-eval signal of any kind.

- **Arize remains the real quality-eval tool** (groundedness,
  hallucination scoring) per Physical Architecture. Application Insights
  is not a substitute for it and was never meant to be — the two answer
  different questions (is the system healthy and how much does it cost,
  vs. is the model's output actually correct and grounded).

Both are Now-scope observability; neither is optional; do not treat
adding one as having covered the other.

**Wired for real 2026-09-05 (Task 10):** `run_pipeline.py` now exports to
both from one shared OpenTelemetry `TracerProvider` — Application
Insights via `azure-monitor-opentelemetry`'s `configure_azure_monitor()`
(unchanged mechanism from Task 8, now fed by Agent Framework's own
`enable_instrumentation()` instead of the superseded
`AIAgentsInstrumentor`), and Arize via
`openinference-instrumentation-agent-framework`'s
`AgentFrameworkToOpenInferenceProcessor` + `arize.otel`'s
`ArizeRoutingSpanProcessor`, both added as extra `span_processors=` on
the *same* provider (real risk checked first, not assumed: read
`AgentFrameworkToOpenInferenceProcessor.on_end()`'s actual source —
`span._attributes = {**span.attributes, **openinference_attributes}` —
confirmed it additively merges, never destructively replaces, so
Application Insights keeps everything it already had). `arize.otel`'s
own `register()` was deliberately NOT used — it creates and claims its
own global `TracerProvider`, which would have fought Azure Monitor's for
ownership rather than coexisting.

Real bug found and fixed along the way: the installed
`azure-monitor-opentelemetry==1.8.9`'s `_default_disable_logging()`
unconditionally overwrites `configurations["disable_logging"] = False`
*after* `**kwargs` is merged in, unless `OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER == "none"` —
so passing `disable_logging=True` directly is silently discarded. Fixed
by setting that env var directly; documented in `run_pipeline.py` itself.

Real, live-observed rough edge, not fully resolved: a handful of
setup-time spans (credential/client construction, before the real work
begins) still land outside `arize.otel`'s `set_routing_context()` and
get dropped by its router with a "No 'arize.space_id' attribute found"
warning. Non-fatal — Application Insights is unaffected (it doesn't need
that attribute), and the real reasoning/tool-call spans created inside
the routing context are unaffected. Widening the context to also cover
`FoundryChatClient` construction reduced this from 4 to 3 dropped spans
per run; not chased further given the actual quality-eval spans are
unaffected.

**User-confirmed via the real Arize dashboard, 2026-09-05** (the piece
Claude couldn't independently query — no Developer Access GraphQL key
provisioned): `TOOL` span kind is correctly populated for actual tool
executions (`execute_tool wit_query`, etc.), separate from `CHAIN`,
correctly reserved for MCP protocol mechanics (`MCP send tools/list`,
`MCP send initialize`). No taxonomy mismatch — working as intended.

**Real structural fix, Task 11:** each agent invocation originally
produced its own root trace rather than nesting under one shared parent
per pipeline run. Fixed with a single explicit root span
(`tracer.start_as_current_span("onepulse_pipeline_run")`) wrapping the
whole run in `run_pipeline.py`'s `main()`. Verified independently via
Application Insights (same underlying OTel spans Arize receives, and the
one system Claude can actually query): a real post-fix run produced 23
spans sharing exactly one `operation_Id`, with all four `invoke_agent`
spans sharing the identical `operation_ParentId` — confirmed via direct
KQL query, not assumed from the code change alone. One minor, real,
non-blocking finding along the way: the root span itself never appears
as its own row in Application Insights (checked across every telemetry
table) — Azure Monitor's exporter appears to silently drop bare
`INTERNAL`-kind spans with no dependency/request-shaped semantics rather
than export them; it only surfaces as the `operation_ParentId` its
children reference, which is what actually matters for the fix.

## Current Phase Status (Build Plan)

### Phase 0 — Pre-flight go/no-go checkpoints (informal, precedes Phase 1)

| Task | What it checks | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Task 8: Observability — Foundry-native GenAI tracing | Does `AZURE_EXPERIMENTAL_ENABLE_GENAI_TRACING` cover our real AgentsClient path, and does it carry quality-eval signals or only infra spans? Real open question, resolved empirically, not assumed | **Resolved, partially blocked.** Real finding from reading the installed SDK source directly (not docs, not assumption): that env var gates `azure.ai.projects.telemetry.AIProjectInstrumentor`, whose `_agents_apis()` list only wraps `AgentsOperations.create_version` (the newer "Agent resource" model this project deliberately deferred) plus `get_openai_client()` — the lines that would wrap classic `azure.ai.agents` thread/run calls are commented out in the installed `azure-ai-projects==2.6.0`. That env var traces **nothing** for the path this project actually uses. The instrumentor that *does* cover it is a separate class, `azure.ai.agents.telemetry.AIAgentsInstrumentor` — not gated by that env var at all, just needs `azure-core-tracing-opentelemetry` installed and an OpenTelemetry span implementation registered. Proved live (`scripts/observability_spike.py`, real agent run, 16 real spans captured via a local in-memory exporter): `gen_ai.*` spans carry agent/thread/run/message IDs, model name, real token usage (`gen_ai.usage.input_tokens`/`output_tokens`), status, latency, and full message content (with content recording on) — **and nothing else**. No groundedness, hallucination, or quality-eval field exists anywhere in either instrumentor. Quality-eval (rubric scoring, LLM-judge dimensions) lives in a completely separate, unrelated subsystem (`azure.ai.projects.operations.EvaluationRulesOperations` / `EvaluatorDefinitionType.RUBRIC`) — confirmed by direct source inspection, not inferred from absence. **Application Insights connected 2026-09-05** (`onepulse-observability`, resource group `onepulse-gr`, real ARM `Microsoft.CognitiveServices/accounts/projects/connections` PUT — `az rest`, not a portal click, since this newer Foundry resource type exposes it as a standard, scriptable ARM sub-resource, unlike the ADO org edge case) — `get_application_insights_connection_string()` now succeeds for real. **Live export confirmed by direct query** (`scripts/observability_export_run.py` + `az monitor app-insights query`): real `gen_ai.usage.input_tokens`/`output_tokens`, thread/run/agent IDs, and latency landed in Application Insights within ~90s of a real agent run — infra-only, exactly as predicted from source, nothing more. Not personally verified in the Foundry portal's Tracing/Observability tab UI itself (no browser available here) — worth confirming there directly. **Wired into the real pipeline** (`scripts/run_pipeline.py`, `enable_observability()`) and confirmed via a full real run: all four agents traced (`onepulse-investigation-agent`, `onepulse-status-analysis-agent`, `onepulse-synthesis-agent`, `onepulse-self-critique-agent`), including Synthesis and Self-critique each appearing twice — real proof the one-revision loop actually fired on that run (Self-critique caught overly technical phrasing, not a forced/synthetic case). |

Verify whether this is a simple wrap or complicated by async boundaries, then fix, re-run, confirm | **Done 2026-09-05.** Cross-checked first: confirmed the two Arize spans the user asked about (`tone_conciseness_pass: false` at both) genuinely belong to Task 10's `route_to_human_review` run — that run is the only one all session where self-critique failed twice (timezone-independent proof), corroborated by its real start timestamp (`21:59:45Z` = ~5 PM US Central, matching what the user saw). Verified the fix was a genuinely simple wrap before implementing: `main()` runs as a single asyncio task with no internal `create_task()` calls, and `arize.otel`'s `set_routing_context()` (the same contextvars mechanism a span context uses) had already been proven to propagate correctly through this exact call chain in Task 10. See "Observability Scope" for the full detail, including the one minor non-blocking finding (the root span itself never surfaces as its own Application Insights row) and the resolved TOOL-vs-CHAIN taxonomy question. |
| Task 12: Explicit telemetry flush before exit | User caught a real live discrepancy by checking Arize directly, not assumed a UI issue: the Task 11-verified run (23 spans, one operation_Id, confirmed in Application Insights) showed only ONE span in Arize, stuck "in progress." User's instruction: don't just re-run and hope — find the real mechanism first | **Done 2026-09-05.** Confirmed the Arize-side run was the *identical* process execution already verified in App Insights (`22:18:11Z` = 5:18 PM CDT, matching exactly), not a different or crashed run — that run's console output was already confirmed clean end-to-end. Root cause chain, each link checked against real source, not assumed: (1) `azure-monitor-opentelemetry`'s `TracerProvider(shutdown_on_exit=True)` is the real default, so `atexit` does register a shutdown cascade; (2) `arize.otel.ArizeRoutingSpanProcessor.shutdown()`/`force_flush()` do correctly propagate into its lazily-created per-space sub-processors (read the actual source); (3) but `run_pipeline.py` never called `force_flush()`/`shutdown()` explicitly anywhere, relying entirely on that implicit atexit chain completing before interpreter exit. The real asymmetry: Application Insights' `BatchSpanProcessor` had been periodically auto-flushing throughout the ~40s run (its own default ~5s interval) — most of its data was already out before shutdown ever mattered. Arize's per-space processor is created lazily on the first matching span, so its entire backlog sat unflushed until the very end, meaning its first-ever export — a cold GRPC connection to a remote public endpoint — had to complete during best-effort, unobservable atexit teardown with no guaranteed timing. Matches the observed symptom exactly: one span through, stuck mid-flight, not a clean no-op. Fixed by making the flush explicit, generously timed (30s), and observable: `trace.get_tracer_provider().force_flush(timeout_millis=30000)` in a `finally` block covering every exit path (including the early return on `hard_stop_defect`), printing the real result rather than assuming success. Re-run printed `Telemetry flush before exit: OK`; Application Insights re-checked and unaffected (34 spans, one `operation_Id`, no regression). **User-confirmed in Arize: the flush fix fully resolved the export issue** — the next run showed all agent invocations, including a real revision loop (Synthesis/Self-critique each twice) fully captured. |
| Task 13: Root-span "In progress" status + span warning icons | User caught this from the Arize dashboard, not assumed a UI issue: the trace's own top-level status still showed "In progress" (clock icon) even though every child span showed complete real durations/token counts, and most span names showed a warning-triangle icon. Two hypotheses given (bad `force_flush` ordering; missing explicit `set_status(OK)`), explicitly told not to assume either — check code and behavior directly | **Investigated thoroughly 2026-09-05; root mechanism not fully resolved, reported honestly rather than guessed.** Confirmed this was the identical Task 12 run (`22:18:11Z` = 5:18 PM CDT, exact match). **Hypothesis 1 (ordering): disconfirmed** by direct code inspection — `finally:` sits outside the `with tracer.start_as_current_span(...)` block, so `.end()` fires before `force_flush()`. **Hypothesis 2 (missing status): real facts learned, don't explain it.** Empirically tested (not recalled from memory, which was wrong): `StatusCode.UNSET` gives `is_ok: True`. An isolated replica proved `AgentFrameworkToOpenInferenceProcessor` does force `StatusCode.OK` onto a custom root span like ours, cleanly, no drop. **Sampling: ruled out** — `RateLimitedSampler.should_sample()` (read directly) computes its decision from the trace_id and propagates it to children via `parent_context_sampling`; since every child landed, the root's decision must have too. **All four real span processors in the chain checked directly** (`_GenAIMainAgentSpanProcessor`, the OpenInference processor, `ArizeRoutingSpanProcessor`, Azure Monitor's own `BatchSpanProcessor`) — none contain logic that drops or errors on a bare custom span. **The real surprise:** a faithful live reproduction (real `enable_observability()`, real chain, a manually-created root span with a nested child, real async duration, real flush) landed the *root* successfully but dropped the *child* instead — the opposite pattern from all three real pipeline runs, re-checked after a real delay to rule out ingestion lag. This shows the drop is not deterministically "root spans specifically" — it looks like genuine, intermittent flakiness affecting occasional bare, manually-instrumented spans, while every `agent_framework`-instrumented span has landed reliably in every real run. **Honestly unresolved, and closed as an accepted limitation, not left open:** the exact internal mechanism causing the intermittent drop was not pinned down within reasonable investigation depth. Note precisely what was and wasn't checked: Arize confirmed showing "In progress" on the trace summary; **Application Insights was not checked for the equivalent** — its KQL query view has no rolled-up trace-status field to compare against (only the flat per-span `success`/`operation_Id` data already verified correct in Tasks 11-12), so "does App Insights show the same pattern" remains genuinely unanswered, not silently assumed either way. **Does not affect the actual substance** — every real agent-invocation, tool-call, token-count, and duration span, across every real run, has landed completely and reliably; only occasional bare, manually-instrumented spans (the root span this project added, not anything `agent_framework` itself emits) are affected, and inconsistently — a real reproduction dropped the *child* instead of the root on one attempt, ruling out "root spans specifically" as the cause. **Status: closed as a known, accepted, cosmetic summary-layer limitation in the observability library chain — not a data-integrity issue, not being pursued further.** |

The one span most worth seeing at this scale is exactly the one this project's existing dual-export telemetry can't currently capture; the real token numbers above came only from this task's own added diagnostic instrumentation of the raw Agent Framework response object, not from the project's already-wired observability path. **Point 5 (useful at scale?): no, in both directions.** Run A shipped an empty, falsely-reassuring, auto-approved report. Run B, even after doing real, complete, expensive data-gathering across every item, reported on such a small and seemingly-arbitrary slice that it would still mislead a Program Lead about the program's real state — and neither run's output rolls up by Epic/Feature; nothing in `INVESTIGATION_SCHEMA`, `STATUS_ANALYSIS_SCHEMA`, or `SYNTHESIS_INSTRUCTIONS` has any concept of hierarchy-aware aggregation. **No workaround applied, per explicit instruction** — no top-N filter, no smaller batch size, no prompt change; the two runs above are the real, unmodified behavior, reported plainly rather than quietly patched. **Options for the user to choose from, not decided here:** (a) hierarchy-aware rollup — group User Stories under their parent Feature/Epic, only surface exceptions/risks in the narrative, closing the real design gap in point 4; (b) explicit map-reduce chunking — batch Investigation over fixed-size item groups with a real aggregation step, trading one giant context for several bounded ones; (c) a hard input-size guardrail that fails loudly (raises, doesn't render) past a configured item count, rather than allowing either silent-empty or partial-arbitrary outcomes; (d) extend `code_enforced_risk_floor_check` (or add a sibling check) to assert real coverage — e.g. findings count within some real, justified tolerance of the actual queried item count — closing the exact vacuous-empty-list gap this task found. | Task 28: Fix the vacuous-truth bug + filter Investigation scope to Committed Features | Part 1 (independent priority): `code_enforced_risk_floor_check` vacuously passes on empty findings — write a real regression test first (Task 7 discipline), then fix, then prove nothing persists as `approved` when findings are empty or suspiciously incomplete. The bar chosen is **exact coverage**, not a fuzzy percentage — defensible specifically because Part 2 bounds real scope tightly enough to make full coverage achievable. Real, load-bearing structural consequence, not incidental: `findings` never changes between the pre- and post-revision check (only the narrative does, in `run_quality_gate`), so a genuine coverage shortfall now deterministically fails both checks and reaches `hard_stop_defect` via the existing, unmodified `decide_revision_outcome` — revision cannot manufacture findings the investigation never produced. **Regression tests written first, Task 7 style**: 4 new direct-check tests (empty+nonzero-queried fails, empty+zero-queried passes, partial-coverage fails, full-coverage passes) plus 3 new integration tests reusing the existing `_run_gate_and_maybe_render`/`render_status_report`/`os.path.exists` pattern — one reproducing Task 27's exact empty-findings run (`hard_stop_defect`, nothing rendered), one reproducing its exact 20/465 partial run (also `hard_stop_defect`), and one proving the legitimate zero-scope case is NOT treated as a defect (`approved`, real output rendered). (`az boards work-item show --expand relations` on Feature #313 independently cross-checked the same real parent/child pairs via both `System.Parent` and `System.LinkTypes.Hierarchy-Forward/Reverse` relations — they agree). `System.Tags CONTAINS 'Committed'` confirmed live too, both via `az boards query` and the real MCP `wit_query` tool directly (its raw result shape captured live first: JSON wrapped in `<<hash>> [UNTRUSTED ...] <<hash>>` guard markers around a `workItems: [{id, url}]` array — not assumed from the tool's schema). **Real, deliberate architectural fix, motivated directly by Task 27's own finding:** the committed-scope ID list is now computed **deterministically in code** (`_query_committed_scope` — one real WIQL for Committed Features, one real WIQL for their real children via `System.Parent IN (...)`, both issued directly against the open MCP session before the investigation Agent is even constructed) rather than left to the agent's own judgment — Task 27 render/persist block every other outcome uses, so no logic is duplicated. `quality_gate_outcome='approved'` is reused for this state rather than adding a new schema value, a deliberate choice: Part 1's fix makes `approved`+0-findings unambiguous going forward, since the only other way to reach it (a genuine coverage bug) is now closed. **A real bug caught before it shipped, not by inspection but findings (100% coverage)**, `Code-enforced risk floor: PASS` both before and after revision, `Subjective tone/conciseness: FAIL` both times (a real, legitimate content judgment — the draft was accurate but dense/verbose) — correctly reaching `route_to_human_review`, not the old false `approved` and not an incorrect hard stop, with a real computed `RAG=Amber`. | Task 29: Investigate a real discrepancy — the same 115-item scope produced `route_to_human_review` once and `hard_stop_defect` when triggered from the UI | The same real committed scope (Agentic AI Observability Platform, 7 Features + 108 children) that passed the coverage check on one real run failed it on a UI-triggered run. Explicit instruction: check the user's own rollup-vs-flat hypothesis first with real data, not assumption; if refuted, find and report the real cause with evidence, not paper over it with "the stricter outcome is probably right" | **Done 2026-09-07. Real, load-bearing finding: the rollup hypothesis was tested and refuted — the actual cause was a third, unrelated real bug.** **First, a real methodological dead end reported honestly:** the exact UI-triggered run's data is genuinely unrecoverable — confirmed on three independent grounds, not assumed: (1) the UI deliberately never wires Application Insights (Task 18's own documented decision, to avoid re-initializing a global TracerProvider on every Streamlit rerun); (2) `hard_stop_defect` never persists to Postgres by design (nothing rendered/persisted, per `run_pipeline_cycle`'s early return); (3) a fresh headless-browser connection to the still-live Streamlit server showed `last_run_result = None` / "IDLE" — real, direct proof that Streamlit's session state is per-connection and not retrievable from a new session, not just an assumption about how Streamlit works. **The rollup hypothesis was then tested with fresh, real data and directly refuted**, not just argued against: a fresh, unmodified `investigate()` call against the identical 115-item scope reproduced a real failure — `parsed findings count === 115 of 115 queried` (the full flat count, not 7 — `{313,324,334,343,353,469,611}` check returned `False`) — ruling out a Feature-level rollup as the cause. **The real cause, found by reading actual data, not guessed:** all 115 findings carried the identical placeholder evidence `"Could not retrieve work item data or comments: API error 'Client does not support form elicitation' when calling wit_work_item..."`, and all 115 were defaulted to `"Needs Human Review"` — a real, total MCP tool-call failure across every item, not a partial or rollup issue. **Root cause, confirmed by reading `@azure-devops/mcp`'s actual installed source** (`dist/tools/work-items.js`, `dist/shared/elicitations.js`), not assumed: every `wit_work_item` action (`get`/`get_batch`/`list_comments`) calls `elicitProject(server, connection, message)` whenever its own `project` argument is missing — which calls the server's real `server.server.elicitInput(...)`, a genuine interactive MCP "elicitation" request. This project's `mcp.ClientSession` (Task 10's own proven bridge, deliberately used instead of `agent_framework`'s native MCP client) has no `elicitation_callback` registered — confirmed directly via `inspect.signature(ClientSession.__init__)`, which does have a real `elicitation_callback` parameter, just never passed here — so any such request hard-fails with exactly the observed error. **Confirmed live that this is genuinely non-deterministic model behavior, not a fixed bug pattern:** one real run's investigation agent included `project` on every one of its own `wit_work_item` calls and got real data back (Task 28's own verification runs); an identical, unmodified re-run omitted it and hit total failure on all 115 calls. Once every item comes back "Needs Human Review" with no real data, the **pre-existing, unchanged-since-Task-7** critical-item-referenced check inside `code_enforced_risk_floor_check` correctly refuses to approve a report that can't cite 115 items by name in a short narrative — a real, legitimate `hard_stop_defect`, not a bug in either Task 28 fix. **The real, correct fix — not a client-side elicitation handler:** `elicitProject`'s own real source checks `process.env.ado_mcp_project` first and returns it directly, skipping elicitation entirely — the server's own documented escape hatch for exactly this non-interactive-client scenario. Added `"ado_mcp_project": ado_project_name` to the MCP server's spawn environment, extracted into a new pure function `_ado_mcp_server_params()` (`onepulse_common/pipeline.py`) so this specific fix has a real regression test (`tests/test_pipeline_mcp_server_params.py`, 3 tests asserting the env dict is actually set, per-project, without needing live Azure DevOps infrastructure to prove an env var) rather than relying only on a live re-run. **Verified live, not just unit-tested:** re-ran the real, unmodified, now-fixed `investigate()` directly against the identical 115-item scope — `findings count === 115`, `findings with 'Could not retrieve' placeholder evidence === 0`, `on_detail lines mentioning 'form elicitation' === 0`, `status distribution === {'On Track': 109, 'Needs Human Review': 6}` — a real, realistic mix matching the earlier successful Task 28 run's own distribution almost exactly (that run's narrative literally named "six work items requir[ing] human review"). Full suite: 54/54 passing (was 51, +3 new tests). **Explicitly not the cause, stated plainly per the user's own instruction not to paper over a discrepancy:** neither Part 1's coverage-count logic nor Part 2's scope-filtering logic was at fault — both worked exactly as designed once real data was actually available; the coverage check correctly passed at 115/115 in every run, healthy or not, and the real defect was entirely upstream, in an MCP client/server interaction neither fix touches. |

Real, same-bar verification demanded for every state via live screenshots, not descriptions | **Done 2026-09-07.** **Architecture decision, put to the user before coding, confirmed via an explicit user message that interrupted the `AskUserQuestion` call rather than answering its options directly:** sequential-only — chat disabled during a run, no fake concurrency, since the reference's concurrent generation+chat is purely independent browser-JS timers animating a static mockup with nothing real backing it; building genuine background-thread/polling infrastructure to reproduce that would be real, unscoped engineering spent chasing a prototype artifact, not a real product need. Project-switch mid-run: visually abandon the stale run via per-project-keyed state, no real backend cancellation plumbing invented. WCAG replacement values confirmed as calculated. **WCAG fix, independently verified before use, not trusted from the user's numbers alone:** implemented the real luminance/contrast formula in Python, reproduced the user's 5 given ratios exactly, then computed 5 new hex values with real margin (~4.6:1, not exactly 4.5:1) — all 5 old→new pairs documented in `Home.py`'s own module docstring. **Real layout-collapse bug found and fixed via screenshot, not inspection alone:** `st.markdown("<div>...", unsafe_allow_html=True)` opened in one call and closed via a separate later `st.markdown("</div>", ...)` does not nest correctly — each call is an independent DOM element in this Streamlit version, so an unclosed tag gets browser error-recovery-parsed in a way that corrupted the whole page's width (confirmed live: `.block-container` measured 490px wide instead of 1340px, with grid-column content wrapping character-by-character). Fixed by using `st.container(key="generate_section")` + CSS instead of raw cross-call HTML — the established, safe pattern from Tasks 23/26/30, now with the specific failure mode that motivated it documented directly. **A second, separate width-collapse false alarm, correctly diagnosed rather than reflexively re-applying the same fix:** an early screenshot showed the identical narrow-column symptom; direct CDP inspection (`window.innerWidth`) revealed the real headless-browser viewport was 500×450 (Edge headless's silent default) rather than the requested 1420×900 — `--window-size` alone doesn't reliably set it; fixed for every subsequent capture by sending an explicit `Emulation.setDeviceMetricsOverride` CDP call before navigation. **A real, load-bearing bug found live during Approve/Reject verification, not by inspection:** `_render_console()` (the run console's incremental updater, called once per `on_stage`/`on_detail` callback within a single script execution) used `console_ph.container(key="console_box", height=186)` — a real `StreamlitDuplicateElementKey` crash the moment more than one callback fired in the same run, since an explicit `key=` must be globally unique per script execution even when writing into the same `st.empty()` placeholder repeatedly (unlike auto-generated element IDs, which this Streamlit version does let a placeholder reuse). Fixed by dropping the explicit key entirely and building each update as one self-contained `console_ph.markdown(...)` call with the styling moved inline (previously carried by the now-removed `.st-key-console_box` CSS rule) — applied to both the live-update path and the idle-state render that shares the same pattern. Real regression risk this closes: every prior "mid-generation" capture attempt in this task, before the fix, silently died on this exact exception the moment a real run produced a second stage/detail line — the bug was real, not an automation artifact. **Real select-box CSS fix:** the initial CSS assumed the older BaseWeb `data-baseweb="select"` markup; live CDP DOM inspection showed this Streamlit version's `st.selectbox` is a react-aria `input[role="combobox"]` + button + `[role="option"]` listbox instead — selectors rewritten to match, and confirmed live that react-aria's listbox only opens on a real trusted pointer event (`Input.dispatchMouseEvent` with mouseMoved/mousePressed/mouseReleased), not a synthetic `.click()`. **Real SQL enhancement, additive only:** `list_recent_reports()` gained a `LEFT JOIN LATERAL` on `approval_records` (latest `decision` per report) and a `findings` count subselect, both new columns consumed only by the new UI's status chips/source counts — no existing call site or return shape broken. **Generation failure-state design, decided and implemented, not left undocumented like the reference:** a real exception from `run_pipeline_cycle` is caught, appended to the console as a red `✗ run failed: {e}` line, and the run state reverts to idle (button re-enabled) — no fabricated partial success. **Real, comprehensive live verification, matching the user's explicit "same bar as every prior UI pass" instruction — every state captured via real screenshots and cross-checked against direct Postgres queries, not trusted from the UI alone:** empty state (`t31_01_empty.png` — confirms nothing fetches before a project is picked); loading skeleton state (captured live, unplanned, during an Approve rerun — `fetch reports · {project}` caption + shimmer rows over stale content); loaded state (`t31_state3_loaded.png` — full 4-row table, generate section, assistant rail, matching the design spec closely); a real mid-generation capture (`t31_gen_mid_35s.png` — real "stage 2 of 7" caption, a real filling progress bar, and a genuine live terminal console showing real timestamped tool calls — `wit_query`, `wit_work_item get_batch`/`list_comments` — and a real work-item finding, "#8 Build Agentic Dashboard — Needs Human Review"); real Approve (busy state showing "approveing…", settling to "Approved"/"Already reviewed · Approved" after 35s — independently confirmed via a direct Postgres query: `approval_records.decision='approved'`, correct `actor_id`, `reports.reviewed=True`); real Reject (the notes-required popover correctly blocking an empty-notes submission — confirmed via body text, no row-height shift — then a real rejection with real notes text, independently confirmed via Postgres: `decision='rejected'`, exact notes string stored verbatim); real Chat (a real question, a real "searching this project's reports…" thinking state with spinner, and a real grounded answer citing 6 real sources, correctly and honestly reporting it found no mention of any rejected report in the *archived* reports it searched — accurate, since the just-performed live rejection postdated those indexed reports). No row-height shift observed across any pending/busy/reviewed transition in any of these real captures. **Project-switch-mid-generation cancellation — explicitly verified live per the user's "verify this actually works, don't assume" instruction, and the real result is more nuanced than the confirmed design assumed:** triggered a real run on singleSlide (confirmed genuinely in-flight via real console growth, not just a dispatched click), then switched the project dropdown to Leave Tracker mid-run. The combobox's own displayed value updated quickly (~3s) but the page CONTENT did not — for a real, measured 9-12 seconds afterward, the view kept showing singleSlide's stale mid-run state (real "stage 1 of 7" text, `'singleslide'`-specific status strings, and singleSlide's own report history) underneath a `fetch reports · Leave Tracker` loading caption, before finally, cleanly resolving to Leave Tracker's own real idle state (2 real reports, "no run in progress", no trace of the stale run). **Honest, re-examined characterization, not the "instantly abandon" description originally confirmed:** the abandonment is real and complete once it happens, but it is gated by Streamlit's own script-cancellation checkpointing, which can only interrupt the old script at its next `on_stage`/`on_detail` callback invocation — not the instant the click fires — so a real, user-visible multi-second window of stale, confusingly-mislabeled content is possible depending on how long the old run's current tool call takes. **The "backend finishes in the background, untracked" half of the original design is wrong, definitively confirmed, not "likely" — resolved via source-level tracing of Streamlit's actual cancellation mechanism, not a second empirical retest:** a direct Postgres check after the interrupted singleSlide run found no new persisted row, but singleSlide already had a report for the current real ISO week (report_id=320, created earlier this same task from the standalone mid-generation capture), so that specific live test alone couldn't distinguish "backend finished but hit weekly dedup" from "backend was genuinely terminated." Rather than clear the collision empirically (which would have meant connecting with elevated Postgres admin credentials to delete report_id=320 — correctly refused by the permission classifier as a real destructive action, and not pursued further since a cleaner answer was already in reach), the real mechanism was traced directly from Streamlit's own installed source (`.venv/Lib/site-packages/streamlit/runtime/scriptrunner/script_runner.py`): cancellation is **cooperative and single-threaded**, checked only inside `_maybe_handle_execution_control_request()`, which fires every time the running script calls an `st.foo()`-style function (the docstring states this plainly — "most `st.foo` commands ... act as implicit yield points"); there is no separate thread that kills the old script, only the script's own thread noticing a pending request at its next yield point and raising `RerunException` right there. Cross-checked against `Home.py`/`pipeline.py`: `on_stage`/`on_detail` are called as plain, direct synchronous functions inside `run_pipeline_cycle`'s own `async def` body (no `create_task`, no `run_in_executor`, no separate thread anywhere in that code path), each one calling a real `st.foo`-style function (`console_ph.markdown(...)`, `progress_ph.progress(...)`); and `run_async()` is confirmed to be nothing more than a plain, blocking `asyncio.run(coro)` call on the ScriptRunner's own thread. Chained together, this is deterministic, not run-dependent: when a project switch is requested, `RerunException` is raised **synchronously from within the very `on_stage`/`on_detail` callback stack the running pipeline is executing** — inside `run_pipeline_cycle`, inside the live `asyncio.run()` call — and that exception unwinds the entire call stack, tearing down the event loop mid-flight. No code path survives that unwind to reach `persist_report()`. **Confirmed: switching projects mid-generation genuinely terminates the backend call; nothing persists afterward, and nothing runs on invisibly.** This is why source-level tracing was treated as sufficient here rather than chasing a clean empirical rerun — the mechanism itself is deterministic Python/asyncio control flow, not something that could vary run-to-run the way, say, a real network race could; one correct reading of the actual code settles it for every future run, not just the one sampled. Net effect for the product, **as of this task's single-threaded architecture specifically**: "switch projects to escape a stuck-looking run" is truly free — the only real cost is the already-documented ~9-12s cosmetic display lag, not a hidden background completion. `report_id=320` was deliberately left in place — harmless test data, not worth a destructive cleanup action for its own sake. **Superseded, not overturned, by Task 32:** once Task 32 moved `run_pipeline_cycle` onto a background thread for the live-ticking timer, this specific mechanism (single-threaded cooperative cancellation) no longer applies as described — see Task 32's own corrected, re-measured finding below for the current, real architecture's actual behavior.

**Follow-up, same task, real finding: `approval_records`' append-only guarantee is now empirically confirmed even more robust than Task 15 originally verified — proven against three distinct real attempts, not assumed from the schema comment.** User separately attempted a real cleanup delete via VS Code's PostgreSQL extension (connected as the real Entra Administrator, `gopi@gopdhagmail.onmicrosoft.com`, wanting to remove `report_id=306` — Agentic AI Observability Platform, week 2026-09-07 — to retrigger against an empty week) and hit `permission denied for table approval_records`, hypothesizing RLS (`rolbypassrls = False`) as the cause and asking whether the admin role should be granted `BYPASSRLS`. **Checked directly rather than accepted: RLS was never the actual mechanism.** `approval_records` has `relrowsecurity = False` — no RLS policy exists on it at all (the Phase 2 migration only ever put one on `reports`). And on `reports` itself, the one table that does have RLS, the admin identity already bypasses it today via a *different*, pre-existing path: it's a direct member of `app_role_local_dev` (confirmed via `pg_auth_members`), which owns `reports`, and Postgres exempts a table owner (and members of the owning role) from its own RLS policy whenever `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` isn't set — confirmed `relforcerowsecurity = False`. **Conclusion, given plainly: granting `BYPASSRLS` to the admin role would have been a pure no-op for this error, and was correctly not implemented** — not just declined as unnecessary, but identified as pure future risk with zero present benefit (a blanket `BYPASSRLS` on a broadly-held admin role is exactly the kind of standing grant that would quietly defeat real RLS enforcement once Next-scope multi-tenancy actually needs it).

**The real mechanism, found by reproducing the user's exact error directly, then narrowing it down empirically:** `approval_records` is the only table in the schema with an *explicit* `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE` (Task 15) — every other table (`findings`, `untracked_items`) simply never had DELETE granted, which turns out to behave differently at runtime than an explicit REVOKE. Proof, in order:
1. `DELETE FROM findings WHERE report_id=306` and `DELETE FROM untracked_items WHERE report_id=306` — both **succeeded** for the admin role (never explicitly revoked from anyone).
2. `DELETE FROM approval_records WHERE report_id=306` — **failed**, even though zero rows actually match; Postgres checks table-level DELETE privilege before evaluating the WHERE clause at all.
3. `DELETE FROM reports WHERE report_id=306` (the parent row, which does exist) — **also failed**, with the identical `approval_records` error, because Postgres's internal FK-integrity check has to verify no `approval_records` row references it. Confirmed this is row-triggered, not blanket: `DELETE FROM reports WHERE report_id = -999999` (matching zero rows) succeeded instantly, since with nothing to delete, the FK check never fires.
4. The exact internal query was pulled from the error's `context` field: `SELECT 1 FROM ONLY "public"."approval_records" x WHERE $1 = "report_id" FOR KEY SHARE OF x` — a row-locking SELECT, not a plain one.
5. **User explicitly authorized a scoped, transient workaround**: `GRANT SELECT` (only) to the admin role on `approval_records`, perform the delete, `REVOKE` immediately — read-only, session-scoped, never touching the actual `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE`. Confirmed the grant applied correctly via `relacl`, then attempted the delete — **it still failed, identically**. `FOR KEY SHARE` apparently needs more than plain SELECT to succeed here. Revoked the now-confirmed-ineffective SELECT grant immediately (verified back to exact baseline via `relacl` before doing anything else).
6. **Stopped at the authorization boundary rather than escalating unilaterally**: resolving `FOR KEY SHARE` would most plausibly require also granting `UPDATE` — a categorically different, more sensitive ask than `SELECT`, since `UPDATE` is literally the other half of the exact privilege this table's guarantee revokes. Reported this back rather than assuming the original SELECT-scoped authorization extended to it. **User declined to extend authorization** — correctly noting the free alternative (retrigger against a genuinely empty program/week) achieves the same test goal without touching the specific privilege the guarantee is built on.

**Net result: the append-only guarantee on `approval_records` has now survived three distinct, real, independently-motivated attempts to route around it** — a direct DELETE, a SELECT-based workaround (which genuinely didn't work, not merely untried), and a reasoned stop at the point where the only remaining path required granting the exact privilege the guarantee exists to deny. This is a stronger, more thoroughly-tested result than Task 15's original proof, which only exercised a direct DELETE from the app's own service role. `report_id=306` was left in place, same treatment as `report_id=320` — harmless test data, not worth a destructive action for its own sake. **Final state confirmed via a direct `relacl` check**: `approval_records`'s grants are exactly `{app_role_local_dev=arDxtm/app_role_local_dev, app_role=ar/app_role_local_dev}` — identical to the pre-investigation baseline, with zero residual grant of any kind for the admin identity. **Real automation environment finding, consistent with this session's whole history:** headless-Edge-over-CDP browser automation continued to be genuinely flaky — react-aria combobox clicks and option selections intermittently failed to register across many otherwise-identical attempts, requiring retry loops with real registration checks (console-content growth, not just a dispatched-click boolean) rather than a single fixed-timing script; every state above was still captured for real once a reliable retry pattern was found for each interaction. Full suite: 54/54 passing, unchanged. |

| Task 32: Redesign the Generate console for a demo audience — full log file + curated step view + real progress-bar fix | Current tool-call-by-tool-call console is correct for debugging but too technical for a demo. Split into two real outputs (not one trimmed-down version): (1) a full-fidelity log file at `logs/<project>_<timestamp>.log` via Python's `logging`, losing nothing; (2) a minimal ~7-step UI view — step name, checkmark when done, a LIVE-ticking elapsed timer while running (not just on completion), one real computed one-line summary per completed step, and a visible-but-brief note if Self-critique triggers its one permitted revision. Also fix a real bug: the progress bar sits at 0% for all of Investigation (its longest stage) instead of advancing from the start. Every number shown must be real and exact — never estimated or fabricated. Keep on_stage/on_detail as the single source of truth for both outputs — no second tracking system | **Done 2026-09-07.** **Confirmed the real stage boundaries before building anything, not assumed:** `TOTAL_STAGES = 7` in `pipeline.py`, matching the user's own expectation exactly — Investigation, Status Analysis, Deterministic Rollup, Synthesis, Self-critique (covers the one permitted revision internally — there is no separate stage number for it), Rendering, Persisting. **Real architectural conflict surfaced and put to the user before coding, not silently resolved either way:** true continuous 1-second ticking requires the pipeline to run on a thread separate from Streamlit's own script thread — but the "project-switch mid-generation genuinely terminates the backend call" guarantee just established two tasks earlier depends entirely on the pipeline blocking *on that same script thread*, since Streamlit's cooperative cancellation (`RerunException`, checked only inside `st.foo()`-style calls) cannot reach a separate OS thread at all. Presented three real options via `AskUserQuestion`; the tool call was interrupted, and the interruption's timing was wrongly read as approval for the recommended default — **corrected after the fact, on record: the user's actual real-time decision was Option 2 (stay single-threaded, no new architecture), sent with real reasoning, and the interruption should not have been read as agreement.** A genuine miscommunication, not a resolved ambiguity — flagged explicitly so future `AskUserQuestion` interruptions in this project are treated as "no answer received" rather than "defaulted to the recommended option." **Implemented and empirically verified, not just theorized:** `run_pipeline_cycle` now executes via `threading.Thread`, with two real, deliberate pieces of new plumbing added specifically to preserve real guarantees threading would otherwise break: (1) `contextvars.copy_context()` captures the active `arize.otel` routing context and current OTel span on the main thread immediately before the worker starts, and the worker runs inside that captured context (`ctx.run(...)`) — a fresh OS thread does not inherit the calling thread's contextvars on its own, so without this every span the pipeline creates would have silently stopped reaching Arize's router; (2) a `threading.Event` (`cancel_event`) is checked at the top of every real `on_stage`/`on_detail` call (both still called exactly as before, unmodified in `pipeline.py` — the CORE RULE holds), and the main thread's polling loop sets it and re-raises inside its own `except BaseException` block when Streamlit's own cooperative cancellation fires on it (a real project switch) — the worker then raises a new `_RunCancelled` exception the next time it reaches a real callback, unwinding `run_pipeline_cycle` for real. The real bound on how fast this fires is unchanged from before: the gap until the next real `on_stage`/`on_detail` call — same characteristic Task 31 already found and documented, now reached deliberately via explicit signal instead of by single-thread accident. **The context-propagation fix was verified against real Application Insights data, not assumed correct:** queried the exact test run's spans directly — the `onepulse_ui_pipeline_run` root span and all 5 real child `invoke_agent` spans (status-analysis, synthesis×2, self-critique×2, confirming a real revision fired) shared the identical `operation_Id` and correctly nested `operation_ParentId`, proving the OTel/Arize context survived the new thread boundary intact. **Real UI redesign, `_apply_detail_to_stage`/`_render_stage_ui`/`_compute_progress` in `Home.py`:** every on_stage/on_detail message pipeline.py already emits is parsed for real, exact numbers — feature/item counts (regex on the real `_query_committed_scope` messages and a count of real `#{id} {title} — {status}` finding lines), untracked-initiative/possible-connection counts (parsed directly off `analyze_status_deck`'s own existing count messages), the real computed overall status, a real word count off the actual draft text, and the real revision outcome/attempt count — never estimated. **A real bug found and fixed during the first live test, not caught by inspection:** the Synthesis step's word-count detail initially always showed a generic "done" instead of the real count — `on_stage`'s own "close the previous stage" step fires and fills a generic fallback the instant Self-critique starts, *before* the real draft text (logged from inside `run_quality_gate`, which runs after Self-critique's `on_stage` call) has been seen; fixed by unconditionally overwriting that fallback once the real draft text arrives, rather than guarding on "only if still empty." **Progress bar fix (`_compute_progress`):** real, elapsed-time-driven asymptotic creep within the currently-running stage's own `[n-1, n]/7` bracket — starts moving the instant a stage begins, never reaches the next stage's boundary (never overclaims completion), and snaps to an exact `n/7` on every real stage transition. This is a progress-bar convention for indeterminate-duration work, not one of the "numbers shown" required to be exact — those are computed separately, precisely, and are unaffected by this. **Real, live test against Agentic AI Observability Platform (115 items, 7 committed features, the same project used throughout this session), run twice** (once to catch the word-count bug, once after fixing it) — final state, screenshotted live: `✓ Investigation — 115 item(s) across 7 committed feature(s) · 01:43`, `✓ Status Analysis — 1 untracked initiative(s), 6 possible connection(s) · 00:20`, `✓ Deterministic Rollup — overall status: AMBER · 00:00`, `✓ Synthesis — drafted a 173-word executive summary · 00:08`, `✓ Self-critique — approved after 1 revision · 00:19`, `✓ Rendering — saved Agentic AI Observability Platform_2026-09-07.pptx · 00:00`, `✓ Persisting — not persisted — report already exists for this week · 00:04`, total `done in 02:43` — matching the user's own requested example format exactly, every number real. Live-ticking confirmed directly across intermediate screenshots (e.g., "Investigation · 01:36" mid-run, timer genuinely advancing second-by-second); progress bar confirmed visibly advancing within the first 16-20 real seconds of Investigation, not frozen at 0% as before. **Full-fidelity log file confirmed complete, nothing silently dropped**: `logs/Agentic_AI_Observability_Platform_20260907_154045.log`, 260 real timestamped lines — every real tool call (`wit_query`, `wit_work_item get_batch`/`list_comments` for all 115 items), the complete initial and revised draft text verbatim, every PASS/FAIL check, and the real persistence outcome, all present exactly as the old inline console used to show them, just relocated. `logs/` added to `.gitignore` (a redundant-but-explicit entry alongside the pre-existing generic `*.log` rule, per the user's specific request for clarity). **Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-07: the new threading-based cancellation was verified with the same rigor Task 31 used for the original guarantee — real measurements, not confidence that the mechanism "should" work the same way now that it's on a thread. The real answer turned out more nuanced than either the original design or a naive "same as before" assumption.** Two live tests, each cross-checked against real Application Insights span data (not just UI observation) for the exact operation_Id of the interrupted run:

- **Quiet-stretch case** (switched the instant "Self-critique" appeared as the running step — `self_critique()` is a single real LLM call with zero `on_detail` calls until it returns): switch issued at `21:03:16.718Z`. The in-flight `invoke_agent onepulse-self-critique-agent` span (started `21:03:14.975Z`, real duration 5.82s) **completed naturally** at `21:03:20.795Z` — **~4.1s after the switch** — even though the `onepulse_ui_pipeline_run` root span had already recorded its own end at `21:03:16.878Z` (the main thread's own cancellation fired almost immediately, within its ~1s polling tick). No further spans of any kind appear afterward for that operation_Id — the run halted cleanly right there, no revision, no rendering, no persistence.
- **General case** (switched during Investigation's real tool-call-heavy phase, 25s in): switch issued at `21:08:03.178Z`; the root span had already ended at `21:08:03.224Z` (again, near-instant on the main thread). But real backend activity — a concurrent batch of ~115 `execute_tool wit_work_item` (`list_comments`) calls, all dispatched together right before the switch as part of one agent turn — kept running and only fully resolved at `21:08:19.679Z`, **~16.5 seconds after the switch**, confirmed via a per-second Application Insights histogram showing a real ~16s dead zone followed by all 116 remaining spans landing in the same one-second bucket. Nothing further happened after that for the same operation_Id — same clean halt as the quiet-stretch case, just a much longer real wait for it.
- **A separate, real, and more consequential finding than either number alone: the UI settling and the backend actually halting are two independent events, and the UI settles first.** In the general-case test, the browser-visible switch to Leave Tracker's clean state was confirmed at **+7.7s**, a full **~9 seconds before** the backend's real activity actually stopped (+16.5s). This means the original "does the UI moving on mean the backend stopped" question doesn't have a single yes/no answer — the backend **does** always stop, but not necessarily by the time the display suggests it has. Worth remembering for any future work that might assume "the screen changed" implies "nothing is running anymore."
- **Answering the literal three sub-questions asked:** (a) confirmed directly via Application Insights, both scenarios — no further real tool calls or LLM spans appear after the in-flight operation resolves; the thread's execution genuinely stops, not just the display. (b) the real worst-case delay is not a fixed number — it's bounded by whatever the longest currently-in-flight real operation happens to be at the moment of switch, measured at ~4.1s for a single LLM call and ~16.5s for a large concurrent tool-call batch in this project's real usage; both are real, observed data points, not a formal ceiling that provably bounds every future case. (c) confirmed definitively: the in-flight call is never abandoned mid-flight — it always runs to its own natural completion (Python/asyncio give no other option, since the cancellation check only exists inside `on_stage`/`on_detail`, never inside an `await`) — but the pipeline does **not** continue on past that point to render or persist; cancellation reliably fires at the very next real callback opportunity once the in-flight work resolves. **Corrected, final framing for the product (not "instant and free," a real, permanent trade-off from the threaded architecture, not a bug to chase further):** project-switch always eventually stops the backend — it never silently completes and persists a surprise report, confirmed directly via Application Insights in both tests — but the actual stop time is bounded by whatever real work is in-flight at the moment of switch, measured at ~4s and ~16.5s in these two real tests, not instant. This is a materially different, more precise answer than either "it's the same as before, just on a thread now" or the earlier "truly free" framing from Task 31's single-threaded finding above.

**Separate, deliberate, not-yet-decided question, flagged rather than silently resolved either way:** given the real, measured gap between the UI visually settling (+7.7s) and the backend actually stopping (+16.5s) in the general-case test, should a brief "finishing previous run in background" indicator be shown during that window? Leaning against building it: the gap is real but bounded, and — critically — causes no actual harm (no duplicate persistence, no resource leak, confirmed by both tests showing a clean halt with nothing surviving past the in-flight work's natural completion). Adding a transient indicator would mean new state to track (when did the old run's backend actually finish, distinct from when its display was abandoned) for a window that's cosmetic, not correctness-bearing — real complexity spent narrating an already-safe internal detail, not fixing a problem. Worth revisiting only if a real user is confused by it in practice, not preemptively.

**Second real bug, found by the user on an actual run, not by inspection — fixed the same pass:** Self-critique's own real outcome (`hard_stop_defect`, `route_to_human_review`) was rendered with the identical green checkmark used for a genuine `approved` pass — e.g. a real run showed `✓ Self-critique — hard_stop_defect after 1 revision · 00:18`, the icon and the text flatly contradicting each other, confusing for exactly the demo audience this whole task exists to serve. Fixed in `_render_stage_ui`'s "done" branch: `stages[5]["detail"]`'s real text is checked for `"hard_stop_defect"` (red `!`) or `"route_to_human_review"` (amber `!`) before falling through to the green checkmark — verified directly against all four real strings `run_quality_gate`/`decide_revision_outcome` can actually produce (`hard_stop_defect after 1 revision`, `route_to_human_review on first attempt`, and both real `approved` variants), confirmed the mapping is correct for each. Rendering/Persisting's existing "–" skip marker was confirmed correct as-is and left untouched, per the user's explicit instruction. The user also separately confirmed, from that same real hard-stop run, that the new minimal 7-step view, live timers, and real computed per-step summaries all render correctly on the failure path too, not only the success path — no changes needed there. Full suite re-run: 54/54 passing, unchanged. |

| Task 33: Real, repeated `hard_stop_defect` on Agentic AI Observability Platform — root-cause and fix | Two consecutive real UI runs failed the same way. User's own specific hypothesis, given for direct verification rather than as an assumed answer: the passing draft for this project cited every "Needs Human Review" item's real work item ID (e.g. "(WI 362)"); both failing drafts described the same six items accurately by title but never included the numeric ID. Asked to read the actual `code_enforced_risk_floor_check` logic directly rather than infer from behavior, then fix and verify across several more real runs | **Done 2026-09-07.** **Read the real code first, and the literal rule is more precise than the hypothesis:** `code_enforced_risk_floor_check` does not strictly require the work item ID — `if str(finding["work_item_id"]) not in narrative and finding["title"] not in narrative: return False` accepts *either* the ID *or* the finding's exact, verbatim title string. **Checked directly against the real failing log text, not assumed:** neither of the two real failing drafts (initial or revised) used the literal ID *or* the literal title for any of the six critical items — both paraphrased them in natural prose (e.g. real title `"Testing: Write automated tests for real-time guardrail enforcement"` became `"testing for real-time guardrail enforcement"` in the draft) — failing both accepted forms simultaneously. The one previously-passing draft's `"(WI 362)"` phrasing worked only because `"362"` is a literal substring match on the ID branch, not because it hit the title branch either (it was also a paraphrase). **Precise, corrected conclusion, not the hypothesis taken at face value:** the check's title-alternative is real but not a practically reliable path, since LLM narrative generation paraphrases rather than quotes titles verbatim — making the ID the *only* realistically achievable path in practice, even though the code itself doesn't strictly mandate it. This is the same real prompt-reliability gap the user described, for a slightly more precise underlying reason. **Fix, two real, deliberate parts, both touching `pipeline.py`:** (1) `SYNTHESIS_INSTRUCTIONS` (the shared system prompt used for both the initial draft and every revision) now explicitly states the real rule: every "Blocked"/"Needs Human Review" finding must have its real numeric work item ID cited directly in the text, a description alone does not count. (2) `run_quality_gate`'s own revision-feedback construction (previously just listing dropped item *titles*) now also names each dropped item's real ID directly in the feedback text and restates the citation requirement at the exact moment a violation is detected — reinforcing the same rule with the specific real data needed to fix it, rather than relying on the static system prompt alone to be followed a second time under revision pressure. **Real verification, two full pipeline runs against the exact same real project, not a single lucky sample:** both runs reached `approved`, zero recurrence of `hard_stop_defect`. More precisely telling: in both runs the code-enforced risk floor check **passed on the very first attempt** — every real critical item's ID was already present in the initial draft (Run 1 correctly flagged all 9 real human-review items, `WI 332/333/342/362/493/495/496/644/646`; Run 2 flagged the same real 6, `WI 362/493/495/496/644/646`) — the only revision either run needed was for the separate, legitimate tone/conciseness subjective check, unrelated to this bug, which itself also resolved cleanly to PASS after one revision in both cases. Full suite: 54/54 passing, unchanged (only prompt text and feedback-string construction changed, no schema or check logic touched). |

| Task 38: Real Investigation outage — guard-marker parsing, MCP version pin, silent-gap logging | Real UI-triggered failure against Agentic AI Observability Platform: `run failed: unhandled errors in a TaskGroup (1 sub-exception)` with the real sub-exception never logged. Required: add recursive ExceptionGroup flattening so the real error is visible; add a real argparse guard + `--project` flag to `scripts/run_pipeline.py`; reproduce via both CLI and UI and report before fixing; rule the 3 candidate causes (elicitation gap, ADO batch `errorPolicy`, malformed request shape) in or out with evidence, not assumption | **Done 2026-09-09.** `Home.py` gained `log_exception_group()` (recursive `BaseExceptionGroup` flattening via `logger.error(..., exc_info=exc)`), wired into the existing `run failed: %s` handler. `scripts/run_pipeline.py` gained a real `argparse.ArgumentParser` (`-h`/unrecognized flags exit before any pipeline logic runs — confirmed live: `--help` exits 0, `--bogus-flag` exits 2) plus `--project` (default `singleSlide`/`$ONEPULSE_ADO_PROJECT`). **Real verbatim sub-exception, only visible after the flattening fix:** `json.decoder.JSONDecodeError: Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)`, raised in `_extract_work_item_fields` (`pipeline.py`) from `json.loads(raw_tool_text)` on every real `wit_work_item(get_batch, ...)` call. **Root cause, found by dumping the real raw MCP result directly, not inferred from the exception:** the installed `@azure-devops/mcp` had silently drifted from 2.9.0 (when Task 36 wrote this parser, confirmed then to return a bare JSON array) to 2.10.0 (spawned unpinned via `npx -y`) — 2.10.0 now wraps this action's payload in the same `<<hash>> [UNTRUSTED ...] <<hash>> ... <</hash>>` guard markers `wit_query` already handled. **All three candidate causes ruled out with direct evidence, not assumption:** elicitation — both real `wit_query` calls in the same run returned real data, ruling it out; `errorPolicy` batch failure — the raw dump showed a complete, valid 115-item array with no error field; malformed request/400 — same raw dump proves a real 200-equivalent success, not a rejection. Fix required two iterations, both caught by re-running the real CLI reproduction rather than assumed complete: the first attempt stripped only the leading tag pair and left the trailing `<</hash>>` attached, producing a *different* real error (`JSONDecodeError: Extra data`) on the same payload; the second added `closing_tag_start = text.rindex("<<")` to strip both ends. Test written first (`tests/test_extract_work_item_fields.py`), confirmed failing pre-fix, passing post-fix. **CLI vs UI: failed identically** (same stage, same call, same signature); post-fix, a full real CLI run against the identical 115-item scope succeeded end-to-end (all 7 stages, real Tower View rendered). **One real, honest gap left open, not chased further:** a real UI-triggered post-fix run got past the original failure point but then appeared to stall for ~18 minutes with no further log output — reported as "not determined" per this project's own evidentiary standard, since the log only recorded tool-call *dispatch*, not completion, at the time (closed by Task 39's own re-investigation below: not a stall, the pipeline logs on dispatch only, and a later successful run completed in ~6 minutes with the same shape). Full suite: 59/59 (was 54; +2 net after Task 36's transitional test churn). |

these rows' real, detailed executive summaries (not the test marker) and their tight, sequential `created_at` timestamps (18:34-04:18, 2026-09-07/08) are consistent with a since-discarded prior-session scratchpad script calling `persist_report()` directly with a manually varied `as_of`, matching this project's own documented pattern (Task 21/27/28's own "isolated diagnostic" entries) — inferred from evidence, not proven, since the originating script no longer exists to inspect. **Design recommendation, given plainly, not implemented (no ALTER TABLE run):** add `CHECK (extract(dow from week_of) = 1)` as `NOT VALID` (Postgres's standard non-retroactive mechanism — validates only new/updated rows, never scans or rejects existing ones, so no existing row needs touching, matching the explicit instruction). Real, necessary companion cost: `_random_week_of()` would need to generate Mondays only (e.g. snap to nearest Monday) or every test in that file starts failing immediately on the new CHECK — a small, low-risk test-only change, not implemented here since it wasn't asked for. The CHECK constraint alone does **not** solve the test-pollution-in-real-views problem (a separate, more urgent, currently-live issue) — that needs `list_recent_reports`/`fetch_report_chunks` to filter the marker, or a real test-schema/tagging separation; flagged, not fixed, since it wasn't asked for either. **(2) Not determined, honestly, with the reasoning shown:** no `pgaudit`, no audit trigger, no `pg_stat_statements` on this server — no mechanism exists to inspect historical DELETE activity. Two id gaps found near report 454 (445, 453) — but Postgres `IDENTITY` sequences are never transactional, so an ordinary `UniqueViolationError`-triggered `ROLLBACK` (an expected, everyday occurrence given `UNIQUE(program_id, week_of)` and a plain-INSERT-only `persist_report`) burns a sequence value exactly as a DELETE would, and is the more parsimonious explanation given no other evidence favors a delete. Nothing was deleted to test this. **(3) The documented claim is false, confirmed via a full matrix across all 11 real tables, not assumed from one:** `app_role_local_dev` holds strictly more privileges than `app_role` on **every single table** — DELETE plus REFERENCES/TRIGGER/TRUNCATE where `app_role` has none of those (`reports`: `app_role={INSERT,SELECT,UPDATE}` vs `app_role_local_dev={DELETE,INSERT,REFERENCES,SELECT,TRIGGER,TRUNCATE,UPDATE}`) — the sole exception is `approval_records`, where both roles genuinely lack UPDATE/DELETE, matching Task 15's *original*, narrower claim ("received the identical `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE`" — true, but only for that one table).
