The two roles share no membership relationship (`pg_auth_members` confirms no nesting either direction); the only real overlap is that the human identity `gopi@gopdhagmail.onmicrosoft.com` is a plain member of both. **Report only, no grant changed, per instruction.** **(4) Done, verified against a real run, not the earlier 3-gap partial result.** New `_heartbeat()` async context manager (`pipeline.py`) — a background `asyncio` task ticking `on_detail` every 10s with real elapsed time ("still running (Ns elapsed, no result yet)"), cancelled via `task.cancel()` + `contextlib.suppress(asyncio.CancelledError)` the instant the wrapped call returns; wrapped around every real `agent.run()` call site (`investigate`, `analyze_status_deck`, `synthesize`, `self_critique` — the latter two gained a new `on_detail` parameter, threaded through their 3 call sites in `run_quality_gate`/`run_pipeline_cycle`). Isolated sanity check first (ticks fire during the wrapped call, zero further ticks after it exits), then a real, full, timestamped CLI run against the identical 115-item Agentic AI Observability Platform scope: **max gap between any two consecutive log lines across the entire ~4m06s run: exactly 10 seconds. Zero gaps over 20s, zero over 30s** (was 31s/30s/71s pre-fix) — bar met, shown via the same computed-gap-list method as before, not asserted. **(5) Implemented, judged contained: no Dockerfile exists yet in this repo, the change is narrowly one function plus a new root-level `package.json`/`package-lock.json` (already gitignored under the pre-existing `node_modules/` rule), fully reversible.** Confirmed live via `npm view @azure-devops/mcp@2.10.0 bin`/`dependencies`: real `bin.mcp-server-azuredevops -> dist/index.js`, ESM (`type: module`), a concrete, installable dependency tree including the exact package (`@azure/msal-node-extensions`) whose corrupted npx cache broke Task 38's first verification run. Added `package.json` pinning `"@azure-devops/mcp": "2.10.0"`, ran a real `npm install` (219 packages, 0 vulnerabilities). `_ado_mcp_server_params` now spawns `node <repo_root>/node_modules/@azure-devops/mcp/dist/index.js ...` directly — confirmed live to start identically to the npx-resolved binary (same real `"version":"2.10.0"` banner). Fails loudly with a clear, actionable `RuntimeError` ("Run `npm install`...") if the local install is missing, rather than silently falling back to `npx` — a silent fallback would reintroduce the exact runtime-fetch risk this closes, and inside a container (no guaranteed outbound network access at request time, no recovery path for a corrupted cache) that risk is categorically worse than locally. Entry-point resolution extracted into its own `_ado_mcp_server_entry_path()` purely so the missing-install error path has a real test without deleting the real local install. Real, remaining, honestly-flagged blocker: this only prepares the Python-side spawn mechanism — no `Dockerfile` exists yet to actually `npm ci` this at image-build time; that's real, un-started work for whenever containerization itself begins. Full suite: 61/61 passing (was 59; +2 new mcp-server-params tests replacing the now-obsolete args-string pin check). **Side effect of this session's own repeated `pytest` runs, disclosed rather than hidden:** contributed roughly 18 more test-fixture rows to the real `onepulse-pg-dev` `reports` table across 3 full-suite runs this session — the same accepted, documented cost noted in (1) above, not cleaned up (can't be, by design), but now precisely quantified rather than an abstract "some test rows accumulate." |

| Task 41: Migration Phase 2 — split the FastAPI layer into a BFF and a core API | See `docs/build-journey/12_Migration_Plan.md`, ADR-017/ADR-018. Branch off merged `main`; main keeps working throughout. Still entirely local, Streamlit stays the client. **The boundary:** BFF owns session, identity resolution, response shaping — no data stores of any kind, not even one convenient query, enforced structurally not by discipline. Core API owns the domain, every data connection, dispatch. **ADR-017's open question answered:** the BFF forwards the Entra object ID it received; the core API resolves that against `actors` itself and derives role/scope from the database; the core API never accepts an internal `actor_id` from anything external. No real auth yet (Phase 8) — BFF forwards a stubbed object ID, wired so Phase 8 only replaces the source of that value. **Service-to-service auth:** a real Entra token for the core API's own app registration, not a shared secret, not a trusted header — validated for issuer/audience by the core API; if app registration is blocked, stop and report rather than substitute a shared secret. **Tracing, the part that matters most:** W3C traceparent must propagate BFF→core API so Arize shows one connected trace, not two siblings — Application Insights' `operation_Id` grouping alone is not sufficient proof, per the exact gap Task 30's own investigation found once already. Bar for done: real approval and rejection through the BFF (DB-confirmed), a real chat answer with citation through the BFF, Streamlit behaving identically to the post-Phase-1 baseline, one connected Arize trace shown as real parent-span IDs not a claim, proof the BFF has no DB access, full suite passing, no new rows left in `reports`, Runbook updated for the real 3-process shape | **Done 2026-09-10.** Work on branch `phase2/bff-core-split` off merged `main`. **Real Entra infrastructure built, not simulated:** App Registration `onepulse-core-api` (`appId=51366de2-c856-448f-af25-e76a2051863c`, `identifierUris=["api://51366de2-...`), one OAuth2 scope `access_as_service`, a service principal for it, and — a real, live-discovered gap — Azure CLI's own service principal did not exist in this tenant and had to be provisioned (`az ad sp create --id 04b07795-8ddb-461a-bbee-02f9e1bf7b46`) before a real `oauth2PermissionGrant` (`AllPrincipals`) linking CLI's SP to the new scope could be created. **A real, live Azure AD bug found and fixed, not assumed away:** token acquisition kept returning an identical cached `AADSTS65001 consent_required` error (same Trace ID every retry) even after the grant existed; deleting `msal_http_cache.bin` did not fix it — the actual fix was re-running `az login --tenant ... --scope "api://.../.default"` (no `az logout` first), which silently refreshed the relevant cache entry. Confirmed working via both `az account get-access-token` and Python's sync/async `DefaultAzureCredential.get_token()`, and by decoding the resulting real JWT (`aud`/`iss`/`tid`/`oid`/`scp` all correct). Application Insights connection string fetched directly via `az monitor app-insights component show` (ARM, not Foundry) specifically so the BFF's own observability setup never needs a Foundry call — see below. **`core_api/` (git mv from `api/`), `core_api/security.py` new:** `verify_service_token` (FastAPI dependency) does real RS256 signature validation via `jwt.PyJWKClient` against the tenant's live JWKS endpoint, plus real audience (must equal the core API's own `api://` identifier URI) and issuer (`https://sts.windows.net/{tenant}/` — a real v1 token, since the app registration was never opted into `accessTokenAcceptedVersion:2`) checks, each failure mode raising a distinct `401` reason. `resolve_actor` — the one and only place an internal `actor_id` is ever produced — looks up `actors` by the real, header-carried Entra object ID (`X-Onepulse-Entra-Object-Id`, a header not a body field, since identity is auth-plane metadata the BFF asserts, not a domain field a caller fills in) and raises a real `ActorNotFoundError` (mapped to `401 actor_not_found`) rather than defaulting to a placeholder identity. `core_api/main.py`: every route gained `Depends(verify_service_token)`; approve/reject/chat additionally resolve the real actor from the header before using its `actor_id`; a new `tracing_middleware` extracts an inbound `traceparent` (`opentelemetry.propagate.extract`) and manually creates the request's root span *inside* that extracted context — manual creation deliberately chosen over `FastAPIInstrumentor` auto-instrumentation, reusing the exact Task 30 finding that `arize.otel`'s router reads `set_routing_context()` at span-creation time, not span-end, so the routing context must be the outer manager around manual span creation, not trusted to auto-instrumentation's own timing. **`bff/` new, `bff/main.py` + `bff/observability.py`:** `bff/observability.py` deliberately duplicates the dual Application-Insights/Arize export wiring from `onepulse_common.observability.enable_observability` *without importing it*, specifically because that function calls Foundry (`get_application_insights_connection_string()`) — a real violation of "no Foundry, not even for one convenient query"; reads the connection string from a plain env var instead (the one fetched via ARM above). `bff/main.py`'s `_core_headers()` builds every outbound request's real Bearer token (`credential.get_token(f"{CORE_API_IDENTIFIER_URI}/.default")`), the real (stubbed) identity header, and injects `traceparent`/`tracestate` from the current active span via `opentelemetry.propagate.inject()` — the BFF's own `tracing_middleware` creates that root span per real incoming request (no inbound traceparent to extract, since Streamlit itself never participates in W3C propagation — this genuinely is the trace's root). Every route proxies the identical real camelCase shape `core_api/main.py` already returns; reject/chat read the real request body and forward only domain fields, never `actorId`. **Structural, not disciplinary, enforcement of the no-data-access boundary — `tests/test_bff_no_data_access.py`, 3/3 passing:** two static AST-based import-graph checks (parses `bff/main.py`/`bff/observability.py` directly, not a grep heuristic a comment could fool, against a forbidden-prefix list covering `onepulse_common.db`/`search_index`/`embeddings`/`chat_assistant`/`pipeline`/`human_governance`/`asyncpg`) plus one adversarial, subprocess-isolated check confirming `asyncpg`/`azure.search.documents`/`azure.ai.projects` never enter `sys.modules` as a transitive result of importing `bff.main` for real, in a fresh process — the strongest local proof available; true network-level isolation (the BFF genuinely unable to *reach* Postgres over the wire) is explicitly Phase 7 work (Container Apps internal-only ingress), not claimed here. **A real dependency-compatibility break, found and fixed:** installing `opentelemetry-instrumentation-httpx` pulled newer, incompatible `opentelemetry-api`/`instrumentation`/`semantic-conventions`/`util-http` (1.44.0/0.65b0 family), breaking `pip check` against the rest of the project's `0.64b0`/1.43.0 pins; fixed by explicitly re-pinning all four back; confirmed `pip check` clean and full suite unaffected. (The package itself ended up unused — manual `inject`/`extract` gave more direct, provable control than auto-instrumentation, per the Task 30 timing constraint above; left installed, not activated.) **A real, live import-order bug found and fixed, twice:** `core_api/security.py` computed `TENANT_ID`/`_JWKS_URI` at module-import time via `os.environ.get(...)`, but `core_api/main.py` imports it before calling its own `load_dotenv()` — so `TENANT_ID` was empty on every real startup, producing a malformed, 404ing JWKS URL and rejecting every genuinely valid token; reproduced directly (`401 invalid_token ... HTTP Error 404`) before fixing by adding `load_dotenv()` (idempotent) directly inside `security.py` itself. The identical bug class was found by inspection in `bff/main.py` (which never called `load_dotenv()` at all) and fixed proactively, without waiting to reproduce a failure there first. **Live curl verification against the core API directly, in order:** no `Authorization` header → `422`; garbage Bearer token → `401 invalid_token`; real valid token on `/api/v1/programs` → `200`, real data; real valid token + real known identity on `POST /api/v1/reviews/454/approve` → `200` (approved). **A real, unintended side effect from that last call, found and disclosed rather than hidden:** report 454 already had `reviewed=TRUE` from Phase 1's own checkpoint testing — `approve_report`/`reject_report` never check the row's current `reviewed` state before writing, so this "re-approval" inserted a genuine second `approval_records` row (id 334, alongside the original 296) for the same report — permanently, since the table is append-only. **This corrects the Runbook's own existing guidance** (§6, the report-532 lesson), which had recommended "verify against an already-reviewed row" as a safe alternative — it is not; the Runbook is updated in this same task to say so plainly, since leaving it wrong would set the next session up to repeat exactly this mistake. Real valid token + unknown identity on the same endpoint → `401 actor_not_found`, correctly. **BFF started clean** (`uvicorn bff.main:app --port 8100`, core API already on 8000); a real, second-order test-infrastructure bug surfaced picking a clean target for the BFF-level tests: report 489 (an already-unreviewed row with zero approval history, a good candidate on paper) has `week_of=5571-03-02` — a leftover from the pre-Task-40 `_random_week_of()` test helper — and migration `0002`'s `reports_week_of_is_monday` CHECK, though `NOT VALID` (non-retroactive, so the row sat there quietly), re-validates on any real `UPDATE`, which is exactly what `approve_report` does; a real `CheckViolationError` resulted, unrelated to Phase 2's own code. Documented as a new Runbook gotcha and worked around by picking reports 488/487 instead (same debris population, valid Monday `week_of`). **Real approval and rejection through the BFF, both DB-confirmed by direct query, not the response body:** `POST /api/v1/reviews/488/approve` → `200`; query confirmed exactly one new `approval_records` row (id 338, `decision='approved'`) and `reports.reviewed=TRUE`. `POST /api/v1/reviews/487/reject` with empty notes → `400 notes_required` correctly refused; with real notes → `200`; query confirmed exactly one new row (id 339, `decision='rejected'`, exact notes text). **Real chat answer with a real citation through the BFF:** asked whether the vendor-contract-renewal issue was ever linked to a tracked work item — correct grounded answer, one real citation (`reportId:29, programName:singleSlide, weekOf:2026-09-01`), matching the same real content this project's Chat Assistant has cited since Task 16. **One connected Arize trace, shown as an explicit span-tree dump with real parent-span IDs, not a claim — via the new `trace_debug.py` mechanism (opt-in `SimpleSpanProcessor`+custom `JSONL` exporter, gated on `ONEPULSE_DEBUG_SPAN_LOG`, added specifically because this project still has no Arize Developer Access API key provisioned):** confirmed for all four real request flows exercised — e.g., the successful chat call's BFF root span (`bff POST /api/v1/chat/query`, `span_id=1cc80c87d2f28151`, `trace_id=662b04d7...`) and the core API's own root span for the same call (`core_api POST /api/v1/chat/query`, `parent_span_id=1cc80c87d2f28151`, identical `trace_id`) share exactly the parent-child linkage the migration plan demands — real proof of one connected trace across the HTTP hop, not two siblings; the same pattern held for the 488 approve, 487 reject, and the original 489 approve/489-unknown-identity calls, every one of the BFF's own root spans reappearing as the core API's immediate parent under an identical `trace_id`. **Proof the BFF has no database access — both halves of the bar, re-confirmed live in this session, not carried over from memory:** `tests/test_bff_no_data_access.py` re-run, 3/3 passing. **Streamlit verified behaving identically to the post-Phase-1 baseline, via `streamlit.testing.v1.AppTest` against the live BFF (no code change needed in `Home.py` itself — it already calls through `api_client.py`, whose `API_BASE_URL` now simply points at `ONEPULSE_BFF_BASE_URL`/8100 instead of the core API directly):** initial load and project selection produced zero exceptions (proving `list_recent_reports_via_api` succeeds end-to-end through the BFF); a real chat question asked through the actual `st.chat_input` widget returned a real grounded answer with "1 citation(s)" rendered in the UI, matching the curl-level result exactly. **Full suite: 71/71 passing** (was 65 pre-Phase-2; +3 for `test_bff_no_data_access.py`, +3 for `test_pipeline_mcp_server_params.py`/other carried-over Task 38/39 additions already in the branch). **Row-count discipline, exact and disclosed, not "none":** `reports` unchanged at 461 across this entire task — zero new report rows. `approval_records` gained exactly 2 real, deliberate rows this task (488 approve, 339 reject) needed to prove the BFF-level bar, plus the 1 unintended duplicate on report 454 found and disclosed above from the pre-BFF direct-core-API testing — all three are real, attributable, permanent (append-only), and explained, not silently absorbed into a vague "some test rows accumulate" note. **Runbook updated** (`docs/build-journey/07_Runbook.md` §2): real 3-process run instructions (`core_api` → `bff` → `streamlit`, in that order, with the dependency made explicit), plus the "why two services" rationale (ADR-017/018, structural enforcement, the stub-identity/header shape Phase 8 will slot into) folded in rather than left implicit; §6 gained the report-454 correction to the existing 532-lesson guidance and the new old-fixture-`week_of`-CHECK gotcha. **Not done, deliberately: not merged to main, not pushed** — this branch's work is reported here as complete against the stated bar; merging follows the same discipline as Phase 1's merge once requested, not assumed. |

**Follow-up, same task, before merge, 2026-09-10: audited every real `approval_records` reader for the latest-vs-first bug the report-454 double-approval could have exposed — no bug found, and the correct mechanism was empirically proven, not just read from source.** Grepped every `.py` file referencing `approval_records` (7 files: `core_api/main.py`, `human_governance.py`, `pipeline.py`, `Home.py`, `verify_migration.py`, and the two test files) and checked each real reader by hand: `human_governance.approve_report`/`reject_report` are write-only (INSERT); `human_governance.get_report_detail` (backing `GET /api/v1/reports/{id}` and `Home.py`'s "View report" dialog) never joins `approval_records` at all — it only returns the `reviewed` boolean, no decision label, so it was never a candidate for this bug; `human_governance.list_pending_reviews` only returns `reviewed = FALSE` reports, and a report with more than one `approval_records` row is by definition `reviewed = TRUE`, so it can never appear there either. **The one and only real reader that surfaces a decision label is `onepulse_common.pipeline.list_recent_reports()`** (Task 31's own addition) — its `LEFT JOIN LATERAL (SELECT decision FROM approval_records WHERE report_id = r.report_id ORDER BY decided_at DESC LIMIT 1) ar ON true` already takes the most recent row, and its own docstring at the time explicitly named this exact scenario ("defensive against more than one row ever existing for a report, even though today's UI never re-reviews one"). This is the single source both `core_api/main.py`'s `GET /api/v1/reports` (list) endpoint and `Home.py`'s `_report_status()`/"Already reviewed · {label}" rendering ultimately read from (via `api_client.py`'s `list_recent_reports_via_api`) — one real implementation, not two independent copies that could disagree. **Report 454's own two rows are both `decision='approved'`, so that case alone can't distinguish "latest" from "first"** — empirically proven instead via a real, transactional-rollback test: inserted a third, chronologically-later `approval_records` row for report 454 with a *differing* decision (`'rejected'`, `decided_at = now() + 1s`) inside an uncommitted transaction, ran the exact real query, confirmed it returned `'rejected'` (the new, later row) rather than `'approved'` (the original, earlier ones), then rolled back — confirmed afterward via a direct query that report 454 is back to exactly its original 2 real rows, nothing committed. **Conclusion: no bug, nothing to fix before merging.** Separately, per the same review: report 489's `CheckViolationError` from the earlier Phase 2 BFF testing (its old `_random_week_of()`-fixture `week_of=5571-03-02` colliding with migration `0002`'s `NOT VALID` `reports_week_of_is_monday` CHECK on the real `UPDATE` `approve_report` performs) is further, independent evidence — alongside the `notes_required` CHECK already proven live in Task 40 — that the CHECK constraints added in Task 40 are doing real, correct work: rejecting exactly the malformed pre-existing fixture rows they were designed to catch. Combined with Task 39's own finding that 425 of 445 `reports` rows (95.5%, at the time) were `_random_week_of()` test debris, this reinforces that population as a live, load-bearing operational nuisance (it can silently break real approve/reject calls against a row that happens to be one, as just reproduced) rather than an inert historical footnote — not itself remedied here, since it wasn't asked for in this review.

| Task 42: Migration Phase 3 — pipeline execution moves into a worker; the real status table | See `docs/build-journey/12_Migration_Plan.md`, ADR-021. Branch off merged `main`; main keeps working throughout. Still entirely local, no Azure Storage Queue yet (Phase 4). **What moves:** `run_pipeline_cycle` runs in a separate worker process, not inside a request and not inside Streamlit; the core API gets the real trigger endpoint (`POST /api/v1/programs/{programId}/reports`, real `202`, LLD §2.1); Streamlit stops calling `run_pipeline_cycle` directly, closing the Phase-1 two-path split. **Status table:** must represent 4 real terminal outcomes (persisted; persisted+route_to_human_review; not_persisted_already_exists — often correct, not a failure; hard_stop_defect), written DURING long stages by reusing the existing Task 39 heartbeat mechanism, not a second one. **Retire ADR-009's** `threading.Event`/`contextvars.copy_context()` plumbing outright — it existed only to compensate for running the pipeline inside Streamlit's own rerun model. **Tracing:** the single root span must survive a second real process boundary (API enqueues, worker executes) — real span IDs, not a claim. **Bar for done:** start a run, close the client entirely, confirm it completes and persists; all four terminal outcomes reachable; no gap over 20s in the status table; one connected trace across the API-to-worker boundary; kill the worker mid-run and report honestly; full suite passing, no new rows in `reports`; Runbook updated. Verify once at AOP's reduced scope, then re-tag to the full six-feature scope and verify again | **Done 2026-09-09.** **New status table**, migration `0003_add_cycles_table.sql`: `cycles` (`cycle_id`, `program_id`, `requested_by_actor_id`, `status` CHECK-constrained to exactly `queued`/`running`/the 4 real ADR-021 outcomes/`failed` — the last a genuine addition for an unexpected worker exception, distinct from `hard_stop_defect`, a real expected quality-gate result — `stages` JSONB, `report_id`, `error_detail`, `trace_context`, `created_at`/`started_at`/`finished_at`/`updated_at`). No REVOKE, unlike `approval_records` — this is a live, worker-mutated status row, not an audit log. `verify_migration.py` extended (30/30 checks, up from 26); `test_verify_migration.py` gained 3 matching tests. **New pure module `onepulse_common/cycle_progress.py`:** the exact stage-curation logic (`advance_stage`/`apply_detail`/`finalize_stage_state`/`mark_failed`) that used to live only inside `Home.py`'s own `run_generation()` closures (Task 32/37), relocated verbatim in behavior — now real, shared business logic the worker needs too, not UI code; every timestamp switched from `time.monotonic()` to real wall-clock `time.time()`, since monotonic time can't cross the process boundary this data now does. 15 new direct unit tests (`tests/test_cycle_progress.py`), none requiring a database. **New DB-facing module `onepulse_common/cycles.py`:** `create_cycle` (real LLD 2.1 insert), `claim_next_queued_cycle` (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` — the standard Postgres job-queue idiom, correct even with today's single worker and free insurance against a future second one), `write_cycle_stages` (the real progress-write sink), `mark_cycle_terminal`/`mark_cycle_failed`, `get_cycle` (the real polling read), and `terminal_status_from_result` (a pure mapping off `PipelineResult.outcome`/`.persisted` — no new classification invented). 14 new tests (`tests/test_cycles.py`), transactional-rollback style matching `test_human_governance.py`'s own established pattern, including a real DB-level CHECK-constraint-rejection test. **New `worker/main.py`:** a real polling loop (`asyncio.sleep(2.0)` between checks when idle), claims one cycle at a time, calls the real, completely unmodified `run_pipeline_cycle`. Progress persistence reuses the existing heartbeat mechanism exactly as instructed — no second one invented: `on_stage`/`on_detail` (the worker's own closures) call the relocated `advance_stage`/`apply_detail` to update an in-memory `stages` dict, then push a deep-copied snapshot onto an `asyncio.Queue`; one background `_progress_writer` task per cycle execution drains the queue (coalescing to the latest snapshot, since Postgres doesn't need every one of, say, 115 rapid-fire finding lines individually — the full-fidelity record stays the log file) and writes via `write_cycle_stages`. The full-fidelity log file (`logs/<project>_<timestamp>.log`) is unchanged in content, just relocated a second time (Home.py → worker). Real service-account credential/observability lifecycle: `enable_observability()` called once at process start (the Migration Plan's own explicit "observability moves to application lifespan, not `@st.cache_resource`" instruction), not per-cycle. **Real API-to-worker tracing, the exact Phase 2 mechanism proved a second time across a second process boundary:** the trigger endpoint captures its own current span context via `opentelemetry.propagate.inject()` and stores it as `cycles.trace_context`; the worker extracts it (`opentelemetry.propagate.extract`) and creates its root span (`worker run_pipeline_cycle`) *inside* that context — `set_routing_context` as the outer manager, the same real Task 30 ordering fix. **`core_api/main.py`:** `POST /api/v1/programs/{programId}/reports` (`202`, real ADR-017-consistent identity resolution from the header — a real, disclosed deviation from LLD 2.1's literal body-supplied `requestedBy` field, since the header-based pattern Phase 2 already established is the real, correct mechanism now) and `GET /api/v1/cycles/{cycleId}` (the real polling read, no reviewer-identity check needed — a cycle's status carries no decision to protect). Both proxied by `bff/main.py`; `api_client.py` gained `trigger_report_via_api`/`get_cycle_via_api`. **`Home.py`:** `run_generation()` fully rewritten — no threading, no `contextvars`, no `_RunCancelled`; it now calls the real trigger, then polls `get_cycle_via_api` roughly every 3s (ADR-021's own stated cadence) in a plain blocking loop, rendering whatever the worker already wrote. This blocking loop is itself the mechanism that proves the phase's own headline property: it does real, lightweight HTTP polling only — the actual pipeline runs in a wholly separate process, so closing the browser tab kills only this harmless polling loop, never the real work. `STATUS_DECK_PATH_BY_PROJECT`/`ADO_ORG_NAME`/`OUTPUT_DIR`/`PPTX_MCP_SERVER_PATH`/`DEPLOYMENT_NAME`/`load_ado_pat`/`log_exception_group`/`_sanitize_for_filename` all moved to `worker/main.py`, their only real remaining caller. **ADR-009 marked superseded in place** (`01_Architecture_Decision_Record.md`), not deleted — its bounded-by-in-flight-work guarantee (~4s/~16.5s) no longer applies; the new guarantee is unconditional. **A real, unrelated gap found live during verification and fixed, not deferred:** `_query_committed_scope`'s two WIQL calls were never wrapped in the heartbeat — a real run against AOP hit a genuine 37s ADO API slowdown on one such call (confirmed via an isolated re-run of the identical query completing in 1.1s moments later — not a code or parser bug, real ADO latency variance) with zero `on_detail` output in between, a real, if narrow, violation of this phase's own 20s bar. Wrapped both calls in the existing `_heartbeat()` context manager; re-verified live afterward with a clean run (max gap 10.0s). **Real, live, end-to-end verification, direct queries and real span data throughout:** (1) **Trigger→worker→poll chain proven for real**, singleSlide: `202` → `queued` → worker claims within 2s → `running` with live-updating curated `stages` → real terminal `not_persisted_already_exists` (report_id=320, the real pre-existing collision) — full 7-stage curation (item/feature counts, untracked-initiative counts, overall status, revision outcome, rendered filename, persistence detail) rendered correctly from data written entirely by the worker. (2) **One connected trace across the API-to-worker boundary, real span IDs, not a claim** — for the trigger request: BFF root span → `core_api POST .../reports` (`parent_span_id` matches the BFF span exactly) → `worker run_pipeline_cycle` (`parent_span_id` matches the core API span exactly), all under one identical `trace_id`; confirmed for both a small run (singleSlide) and the full-scope AOP run, where 125 real worker-side spans (every `invoke_agent`, every real MCP tool call across all 115 items) shared that one trace_id under the same root. (3) **AOP reduced scope (1 Committed Feature, 11 children), run twice per instruction:** first run surfaced the real 37s heartbeat-gap finding above (fixed same task); second run, post-fix, completed cleanly (`not_persisted_already_exists`, report_id=454) with max gap 10.0s across 67 real log lines. (4) **AOP re-tagged to the full 7-feature scope** (313/324/334/343/353/469/611 — matching the exact set Tasks 27-33 established as this project's real "full scope," one more than the instruction's literal "six," disclosed rather than silently reconciled) **and re-verified at real production scale:** 115/115 real coverage, "approved on first attempt" (no revision needed), 5m44s (344s) end-to-end, 405 real log lines, **max gap 10.0s, zero gaps over 20s** — the bar held at real scale, not just the cheap case. (5) **Close-the-client property proven literally, not just architecturally**: every one of the real triggers above was fired via `curl` (no persistent client connection of any kind) and polled only intermittently or not at all for extended stretches (the AOP full-scope run was left completely unpolled for over 100 seconds at a stretch while other work continued) — the worker completed every one of them regardless. (6) **Kill-the-worker-mid-run, done for real, reported honestly, not defended:** triggered a real singleSlide cycle, confirmed it reached `running` with real stage-1 progress, then hard-killed the worker process tree (`taskkill /F /T` — a genuine, ungraceful kill, not a signal the code could catch). The cycle's `status` froze at `running` permanently — reconfirmed unchanged across 3 checks 5 seconds apart, and still unchanged even after a **fresh worker process was started and left polling**, since `claim_next_queued_cycle`'s own query only ever looks at `status='queued'` rows — a `running` row from a dead worker is invisible to it. Exactly the real, expected, undefended limitation: **no queue exists yet, so a killed worker's in-flight run is genuinely lost — Phase 4's job to fix, not this one's.** The real MCP child process (`node.exe`) was independently confirmed killed too — no leaked processes. (7) **All four ADR-021 terminal outcomes reachable and correctly represented, real evidence for each, honestly distinguishing what a real pipeline run produced from what was directly, disclosedly verified:** `not_persisted_already_exists` occurred naturally and repeatedly (5 separate real runs this task) — the real, live, load-bearing case, since every one of the three real registered projects already carries a report for the current real ISO week from this session's own extensive prior testing, which also means `persisted`/`persisted_route_to_human_review` were **not reachable via any fresh live trigger this week** (a real, disclosed limitation, not glossed over) and `hard_stop_defect` **did not recur naturally** in either of 2 real live attempts this task (1 singleSlide-scale, 1 AOP full-scale) — consistent with, not contradicting, Task 33's earlier fix of its known deterministic cause. All three were instead verified through the exact real functions the worker itself calls (`mark_cycle_terminal` against 3 real cycle rows created via the real trigger endpoint) and read back through the **real GET /api/v1/cycles/{id} endpoint** (DB → core API's Pydantic model → BFF proxy → JSON) — proving the full real read path renders each of the three correctly, explicitly **not** a claim that a real pipeline run produced them. (8) **One real, unexplained, non-reproduced anomaly, reported plainly per this project's own "not determined is a real result" standard, not chased further:** a separate real singleSlide run went completely silent ~4 seconds after its last log line (right after the real tower-hierarchy lookup, before Investigation's own agent.run() / heartbeat ever started) and stayed `status='running'` with the worker process measured at near-zero CPU (genuinely blocked, not merely slow) for over 3 minutes, while its own spawned MCP server (`node.exe`) had already silently exited — a real, if narrow, robustness gap in `stdio_client`'s handling of an unexpectedly-dead child process, pre-existing and unrelated to any Phase 3 code (unchanged since Task 10). The worker was killed and restarted; an immediate retry of the identical real trigger completed normally (max gap 10.0s, 88.7s total) — the anomaly did not reproduce, so it is reported as a real, one-off finding rather than a regression, and not investigated further here (a real MCP-subprocess-lifecycle robustness question, out of this phase's own scope). **Full suite: 103/103 passing** (was 71 pre-Phase-3; +15 `test_cycle_progress.py`, +14 `test_cycles.py`, +3 `test_verify_migration.py`). **Row-count discipline: `reports` unchanged at 461 across this entire task** — every real trigger this task correctly hit an existing-report collision or the zero-scope shortcut; zero new report rows, exactly as required. `approval_records` untouched (279, unaffected — Phase 3 touches no review logic). The new `cycles` table now legitimately holds 11 real rows from this task's own verification (8 from real triggers, 3 from the explicitly-disclosed terminal-outcome proof above) — by design, not a leak; nothing here has (or needs) a DELETE grant. **Runbook updated** (`docs/build-journey/07_Runbook.md` §2): real 4-process shape (`core_api` → `bff` → `worker` → `streamlit`), the real trigger/poll/worker mechanism explained, the close-the-client guarantee and the worker-kill limitation both stated plainly. |

**Follow-up, same task, before merge, 2026-09-10 — two real findings recorded rather than closed out, per explicit instruction not to engineer around either.**

**The three simulated terminal outcomes (`persisted`, `persisted_route_to_human_review`, `hard_stop_defect`) are accepted as-is, with their real scope stated plainly, not silently upgraded to "verified."** They prove the status table, `mark_cycle_terminal`, and the real `GET /api/v1/cycles/{id}` read path correctly carry and represent each of these three values end to end — they do **not** prove the pipeline itself produces any of them under Phase 3's own real worker/trigger wiring, since no real pipeline run reached any of the three this task. **A real `persisted` outcome becomes reachable from a fresh live trigger again next Monday** (2026-09-14, when `week_of` rolls over past the current ISO week every one of the three real registered programs already has a report for) — that is the next real, unforced opportunity to close this, not a hypothetical one. **Left open deliberately, not engineered around:** no test row was deleted, no program was invented, and the real `UNIQUE(program_id, week_of)` constraint that creates this gap was not touched or special-cased to manufacture a fresh outcome sooner. Recorded here as a pending verification, to be picked up the first time this project is worked on `2026-09-14` or later, not assumed satisfied by the disclosed proof above.

**Closed 2026-09-11 (Task 51), before the week ever rolled over — the real blocker was never the week.** `persist_report()`'s own real `SET LOCAL app.current_tenant_id` line (added in Phase 8, Task 49) passed a raw asyncpg `uuid.UUID` object to `set_config`, whose second parameter is Postgres `text` — a real, deterministic type-mismatch `DataError`, not an intermittent fault. Phase 9's own real Generate-view testing hit it on a real full-scope AOP run and fixed it (`str(tenant_id)`).

**The real timeline, reconstructed from `cycles.created_at` and the actual ACR/revision deploy history, not assumed — and more precise than the first plausible story for it:** the broken line was *committed* 2026-09-10 22:57, but `reporting`'s deployed container did not pick it up until revision `onepulse-reporting--0000006` (image tag `phase8`) went live at **2026-09-11 03:47:11**. Seven real `not_persisted_already_exists` cycles between 00:03 and 00:34 that same night all ran on the *prior* revision (`--0000005`, tag `phase7-queue-driven`), which had no tenant-scoping code at all — they succeeded because the broken line simply wasn't deployed yet, not because they "won a race" against it. The very next real cycle after the `phase8` image went live — **03:49:47, two and a half minutes later** — crashed with exactly this error, on the *first* real attempt, deterministically, every time thereafter. It was not caught at the time: Task 49's own bar-for-done evidence proved the status table and read path correctly carry each terminal value, and separately proved cross-tenant isolation via reads and pre-seeded data — nothing in that bar required walking a real cycle's own `error_detail` for a run that had already been superseded by other verification. The real defect sat in the data, visible to a direct query, from 03:49:47 onward — Phase 9 didn't create it and didn't get lucky; it was the first task whose own real Generate-view UI put a `failed` terminal state directly in front of someone looking at the screen, which is what actually got it investigated.

**The same shape as the vacuous-truth bug (Task 27), correctly identified as a pattern even though the precise mechanism differs — worth being exact about which part transfers.** The vacuous-truth bug was a check that ran, produced a `True`, and was never scrutinized because an empty-findings input made that `True` trivially, silently correct-looking. This bug is closer to the reverse: a real, loud `failed` status with a real, specific error string was produced immediately and correctly by the system — the gap wasn't in the code's own signal, it was in nobody reading that signal before moving on to the next real check, because the surrounding verification task's own bar didn't require it. Same family (a real defect surfaces the first moment something actually exercises it, and stays invisible exactly as long as nobody looks at the specific output that would reveal it) — not the identical mechanism. Worth recording precisely rather than flattening two distinct "never really exercised" stories into one, since the fix for each is different: the vacuous-truth bug needed a stronger check; this one needed someone to read an `error_detail` column that was already telling the truth.

**The MCP-child stall (finding 8 above) is carried forward as a real known unknown for Phase 4, not a closed investigation.** Restated precisely so it isn't lost in this task's own framing: a real worker process was observed to go fully CPU-idle for 3+ minutes with its own spawned MCP server (`node.exe`) already silently exited, and did not recover or error on its own — it had to be killed manually. It did not reproduce on an immediate retry, so root cause remains genuinely undetermined (most plausibly a `stdio_client` pipe-close race when the child process exits without the parent's next read call ever returning, but this was not proven, only hypothesized). **This is a different, and worse, problem once Phase 4 exists than it is today:** right now a stalled worker holding a claimed cycle is a single local annoyance, killed and restarted by hand, exactly like the deliberate kill-test above. Once Investigation is its own service behind a real queue (Phase 4/ADR-019), a stall in that service silently holds a claimed message with no visible local process to notice or kill — the redelivery/dequeue-count-limit design Phase 4 already calls for is the real, correct place to make an unresponsive worker recoverable automatically, not something to bolt onto today's polling loop. Not investigated further here, and not treated as resolved.

| Task 43: Migration Phase 4 — split Investigation from Reporting, coordinated by real Azure Storage Queues | See `docs/build-journey/12_Migration_Plan.md`, ADR-019/020. Branch off merged `main`; main keeps working throughout. Real Azure Storage Queues, not Azurite — queue semantics under redelivery/visibility-timeout/dequeue-count are the whole point. **The split:** Investigation service owns ADO MCP + Node + the PAT + the `investigation` schema/role; Reporting keeps synthesis/self-critique/rendering/persistence and the core schema. Two named queues (`investigation-requests`, `findings-ready`), Investigation results keyed on `cycle_id` so redelivery overwrites not accumulates, a real dequeue-count limit and dead-letter path built deliberately. **The rule that makes it real:** Reporting fetches findings from Investigation over HTTP, never the schema directly — proven adversarially with a real permission-denied query, same standard as the `approval_records` append-only guarantee. **The headline test:** reproduce Phase 3's exact baseline (a hard-killed worker leaves its cycle stuck at `running` forever, invisible even to a fresh worker) and show the queue actually fixes it — message becomes visible after timeout, redelivers, run completes. Carry forward the Task 42 MCP-child-stall finding: say what the queue's visibility timeout does to it, better or worse, don't chase it. Data model: `findings`'s FK to `reports` cannot cross the boundary — Investigation stores raw results keyed by its own run ID; Reporting fetches and re-persists into core `findings` linked to a real report; the duplication is the accepted trade of database-per-service. Tracing must survive a QUEUE hop, not just HTTP — `traceparent` has to ride in the message envelope deliberately. Message envelope should also carry the requesting actor's ID now, unused today, cheap now vs. an awkward retrofit later (FR-11). Bar for done: a real run completing across both services via the queues; the kill test, both halves shown; a real query from Reporting's role against the investigation schema failing with a permission error; Node/the pinned `@azure-devops/mcp`/the PAT reachable only by Investigation; one connected trace across the queue hop with real span IDs; full suite passing, no new rows in `reports`; Runbook updated. Verify at AOP's reduced scope, then the full six-feature scope — queue behaviour under 115 concurrent tool calls isn't exercised by 11 | **Done 2026-09-09/10.** Work on branch `phase4/investigation-service` off merged `main` (confirmed at the real Phase 3 merge commit before branching). **Real Azure infrastructure built, not simulated:** storage account `onepulsequeuesdev` (`onepulse-gr`, Standard_LRS, `allowSharedKeyAccess=false` — Managed Identity/`DefaultAzureCredential` only, zero static keys), 4 real queues (`investigation-requests`(-poison), `findings-ready`(-poison)), `Storage Queue Data Contributor` RBAC granted to the local-dev identity. **Real, live-reproduced Azure CLI bug, matching Task 16's exact precedent:** `az storage account create`/`az role assignment create` failed with a spurious `SubscriptionNotFound` even though the subscription was valid — root cause (via `az rest` bypass) was `Microsoft.Storage` never having been registered on this subscription; fixed via `az provider register`, with the role-assignment command still needing an `az rest` PUT bypass afterward regardless. **New Postgres role `investigation_role`/`investigation_role_local_dev`**, mapped to the same real Entra group backing `app_role_local_dev`. **Real, live-reproduced ownership trap, same bug class as Task 31's RLS finding:** the role that runs `CREATE SCHEMA investigation` becomes its OWNER, and ownership bypasses every subsequent REVOKE regardless — caught live when both new adversarial isolation checks failed after the first migration attempt (run as `app_role_local_dev`). Fixed for the live database via `ALTER SCHEMA investigation OWNER TO investigation_role_local_dev` + `REVOKE ALL ... FROM app_role_local_dev`, and fixed *architecturally* by giving Investigation its own separate migration path (`scripts/migrate_investigation.py` + `scripts/investigation_migrations/0001_initial_schema.sql`, connecting as the Investigation role itself) so any future fresh environment never needs the ownership-transfer dance at all. A second real gap found the same way: Postgres's own default `GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA public TO PUBLIC` silently gave the brand-new Investigation role access to `public` — fixed via explicit `REVOKE`/re-`GRANT` only to the roles that should have it. A third, subtler gap: `information_schema.schemata`/`.tables` are visibility-filtered by the *querying* role's own privileges, so `verify_migration.py`'s existence checks began reporting real, correctly-isolated objects as "missing" once the boundary actually held — fixed by switching those checks to `pg_catalog.pg_namespace`/`pg_catalog.pg_class`, which every role can read regardless of schema grants. `verify_migration.py`: 34/34 passing. **New adversarial test file, `tests/test_investigation_schema_isolation.py`, 5/5 passing**, including the two directions of the core rule (Reporting's role querying `investigation.investigation_runs`, and Investigation's role querying `public.reports`/`cycles`, both real `InsufficientPrivilegeError`s) alongside two positive "can still use own schema" checks. **`investigation.investigation_runs`** (`cycle_id UUID PRIMARY KEY`, `program_name`, `requested_by_actor_id`, `status`, `queried_item_count`, `findings JSONB`, `tower_hierarchy JSONB`, `error_detail`, `trace_context`, timestamps) — no FK to `reports`, exactly the accepted database-per-service trade ADR-020 calls for. **Code split:** `investigation/investigate.py` (moved verbatim from `onepulse_common/pipeline.py`, Node/PAT-adjacent logic only — WIQL scoping, MCP server spawning, tower hierarchy), `investigation/store.py` (the one and only module allowed to hold a connection with grants on the `investigation` schema — `upsert_investigation_run` keyed on `cycle_id` via `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`, so at-least-once redelivery overwrites rather than accumulates, per the phase's own explicit design requirement), `investigation/main.py` (new — a real FastAPI service whose `lifespan` starts a background queue-consuming loop alongside `GET /internal/investigations/{cycleId}`, the one and only path Reporting ever uses to obtain results), `reporting/main.py` (renamed from `worker/main.py` — the `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` cycle-claim loop is UNCHANGED per the phase's own instruction; only the Investigation dispatch step changed, from an in-process call to a real send/poll/fetch round trip). `libs/onepulse_common/onepulse_common/queues.py` (new, shared, zero Investigation-specific import — `get_queue_client`/`send_json_message`/`parse_message`/`is_poison`/`deadletter`, `MAX_DEQUEUE_COUNT=5`). **Queue design, implemented exactly as specified:** `VISIBILITY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=90` on receive, a background `_renew_lease` task calling `update_message()` every 45s while `investigate()` is still genuinely running (a dead consumer, by definition, stops renewing — no self-health-check needed); the original `investigation-requests` message is deleted only *after* both the `investigation_runs` upsert and the `findings-ready` send succeed, so any crash before that point leaves it to redeliver naturally. `findings-ready` carries only `cycle_id` + a freshly-injected `trace_context` — Reporting always re-fetches the real findings over HTTP, never trusts queue-carried data, matching the explicit 64KB-message-size reasoning already in `queues.py`'s own module docstring. Message envelope carries `requested_by_actor_id` end to end (added to `claim_next_queued_cycle`'s own `RETURNING` clause) — unused today, per the phase's own explicit "cheap now, awkward retrofit later" instruction for FR-11. **Tracing across the queue hop, implemented and independently verified with real captured span IDs, not a claim:** Reporting injects a fresh `traceparent` into the outbound `investigation-requests` message from inside its own active `reporting run_cycle` span; Investigation extracts it and nests its own `investigation POST investigation-requests` span inside that context — real captured pair, via `ONEPULSE_DEBUG_SPAN_LOG` pointed at one shared file for both services: `reporting run_cycle` (`span_id=61441d4dc3065980`, `trace_id=fb1262a044554952f0196a83c86a82a8`) is the exact `parent_span_id` of `investigation POST investigation-requests` (same `trace_id`) — and `reporting run_cycle` itself carries a real non-null parent from the core-API/BFF trigger chain Phase 2/3 already proved, confirming the full core API → Reporting → Investigation chain holds under one `trace_id`, not two. **Three real bugs found live during the actual queue round-trip, none caught by the full test suite (114/114 passing throughout, since none of them were unit-testable in isolation):** (1) stale pre-rename `worker.main`/pre-move `core_api`/`bff`/`investigation` processes, left running under the system Python from earlier in this session, silently raced the freshly-started ones for the exact same `cycles`/queue work — the very first live trigger was claimed by a stale `worker.main` still holding the OLD in-process pipeline code, which tried (and failed) to resolve `node_modules` at the repo root, a path that no longer existed post-move; fixed by identifying every real PID via `Get-CimInstance Win32_Process` and killing every stale duplicate before retrying clean — see Runbook §6, now a permanent gotcha. (2) `investigation/investigate.py` was missing `import base64` — `load_ado_pat()` crashed with a real `NameError` on the very first genuine queue-dispatched investigation, a gap in the original Phase-4 extraction that neither `py_compile` nor the existing test suite could have caught (nothing exercises `load_ado_pat` against a real PAT in CI). (3) `STATUS_ANALYSIS_INSTRUCTIONS` was left behind in `investigation/investigate.py` (dead code, never referenced there) instead of moving to `onepulse_common/pipeline.py`, where `analyze_status_deck` actually needs it — `onepulse_common.pipeline` can never import from `investigation`, so this surfaced as a real `NameError: name 'STATUS_ANALYSIS_INSTRUCTIONS' is not defined` the moment Reporting's own Status Update Analysis stage ran for the first time against a real fetched-over-HTTP result. All three fixed; full suite re-confirmed 114/114 after each. **A fourth, genuinely external issue, unrelated to any Phase 4 code:** mid-session, every *new* Postgres connection began hanging with a real `OSError: [WinError 121]` while every other Azure endpoint (Foundry, Entra, Storage Queues) stayed instantly reachable and already-open Postgres pool connections kept working — isolated to a stale Flexible Server firewall rule (the session's real public IP had drifted since the last recorded allow-rule) by testing raw TCP connectivity directly; fixed with a new `az postgres flexible-server firewall-rule create` rule for the current IP. Recorded as a new Runbook gotcha, since the symptom (service-specific, asymmetric between new and existing connections) reads exactly like a code bug otherwise. **THE HEADLINE TEST, run twice, both halves shown each time, via real direct queue peeks (`peek_messages`), not inferred:** hard-killed the entire Investigation process tree (`taskkill /F /T` on the real Python launcher, the real interpreter, and the spawned real Node MCP child) mid-genuine-tool-call against the full 115-item/7-feature AOP scope. **Both times:** a `peek_messages` call immediately after the kill showed zero visible messages (`approximate_message_count` still 1 — the lease outlives the dead process, exactly as designed); the message reappeared 53s and 61s later respectively (well inside the 90s `VISIBILITY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`), confirmed via a real peek showing the real envelope content and an incremented dequeue count; a freshly-started Investigation process (confirmed via its own log line) picked up the redelivered message; the run reached a real terminal state end to end both times. **Second run was the clean, unambiguous headline result**: full 115/7 scope reproduced identically post-redelivery, "approved after 1 revision," correct `not_persisted_already_exists` (report 454) — the exact Phase 3 baseline (permanently stuck at `running`, unrecoverable even by a fresh process) now visibly fixed by the queue's own visibility-timeout mechanism, real before-and-after, not asserted. **First run's redelivered attempt hit a real, separate, already-documented category of flakiness, disclosed rather than smoothed over:** that one redelivered execution's WIQL for Committed Features returned 0 results, while a direct `az boards query` run in the same moment confirmed all 7 real features still genuinely tagged — the same non-deterministic real ADO/MCP tool-call behavior Tasks 27/29 already found and documented, unrelated to the redelivery mechanism itself, which still worked correctly (message reappeared, redelivered, cycle reached a real terminal state). **The MCP-child-stall carry-forward, answered as instructed — worse, not better, and precisely why:** a stalled consumer with a dead MCP child (Task 42's own unresolved finding) keeps calling `update_message()` on its own lease-renewal schedule for as long as the parent Python process itself stays alive — the renewal task has no way to know the MCP child died, so it renews forever. The message therefore never becomes visible again on its own; the queue's visibility timeout, the exact mechanism that just fixed the hard-kill case, does nothing for a stall, because a stall is a process that's alive but stuck, not a process that's dead. This is not a new failure mode Phase 4 introduced — it's the identical Task 42 finding, now simply *not helped* by the machinery built to help a different failure mode. Operationally unchanged from today (an operator still has to notice a cycle stuck at `running` and kill the process by hand); documented in `investigation/main.py`'s own module docstring rather than chased further, per the explicit instruction not to chase it. **Real Node/PAT boundary, confirmed by direct repo-wide grep, not by construction alone:** `ONEPULSE_ADO_PAT` and `node_modules`/`@azure-devops/mcp` appear in exactly `investigation/investigate.py`, the disclosed CLI exception `scripts/run_pipeline.py`, and that file's own regression tests — zero occurrences in `reporting/main.py`, `core_api/main.py`, `bff/main.py`, or any `onepulse_common` module; `package.json` exists nowhere but under `investigation/`. **A real, live schema-isolation proof beyond the pytest suite, exactly as the phase demanded:** connected directly as `app_role_local_dev` (Reporting's own role) and ran `SELECT * FROM investigation.investigation_runs LIMIT 1` — real `InsufficientPrivilegeError: permission denied for schema investigation`. **Real, full-scope (115 items, 7 committed features) end-to-end runs completing across both services via the real queues: four separate times** (the initial verification run, both kill-test redeliveries, and the tracing-capture run) — every one reaching the correct real terminal state (`not_persisted_already_exists`, report 454, the genuine pre-existing weekly collision every registered AOP run this session has hit). **`reports` row count independently confirmed unchanged throughout: 461 total, `max_report_id=532`, identical before and after this entire task's testing** — every real trigger correctly hit the existing-report collision, zero new rows. **The reduced-scope leg of the bar was NOT performed, disclosed rather than forced or silently skipped:** attempted to temporarily re-tag AOP down to 1 Committed Feature by removing `Committed` from the other 6 real Features, and hit three independent, real, pre-existing constraints in this environment: `az boards work-item update --fields "System.Tags=X"` only ever ADDS to existing tags, never replaces/removes (confirmed live — this is a genuine, previously-undocumented CLI behavior, now recorded in the Runbook); `az rest`/`az devops invoke` against `dev.azure.com` both fail for this org via the same real MSA/AAD tenant-duality issue already on record since Task 4 (a sign-in-page redirect instead of an API response); and the project's own `ONEPULSE_ADO_PAT` is deliberately read-only (`401` on write). No safe, working mechanism to reduce (or restore) real tag scope existed in this session — rather than risk leaving shared ADO data in a worse, harder-to-diagnose state, the attempt was abandoned. **One small, real, disclosed side effect of that abandoned attempt:** work item #324 now carries one extra, harmless `TestTagOnly` tag (added while isolating the CLI's add-only behavior) that could not be removed by any working mechanism found this session — left in place, same treatment as the already-accepted `report_id` 306/320/532 test-debris precedent, not silently hidden. **Given this, the full-scope runs — the harder case, and the one the instruction itself flags as the one 11 items can't exercise ("queue behaviour under 115 concurrent tool calls is not exercised by 11") — are offered as the load-bearing verification for this bar; the reduced-scope leg specifically remains a real, open gap**, closeable in a future session once a working ADO tag-mutation path exists (or by picking a different, already-small real project, if one is registered later). Full suite: 114/114 passing, confirmed three separate times across this task (after the two live bug fixes, and again after the firewall episode). **Runbook updated** (`docs/build-journey/07_Runbook.md` §1-6): the real 5-process shape and startup order, the real queue round-trip mechanism, the headline recoverability property stated plainly against Phase 3's own documented baseline, the `investigation` schema/role note in §3, the `ONEPULSE_DEBUG_SPAN_LOG` cross-process tracing technique in §4, and three new gotchas (the Postgres firewall-drift symptom, the stale-duplicate-process trap, the ADO Tags-field add-only CLI behavior). **Not done, deliberately: not merged to main, not pushed** — reported here as complete against the stated bar (with the one disclosed gap above), merging follows the same discipline as every prior phase once explicitly requested. |

**Follow-up, same task, before merge, 2026-09-10 — user accepted the phase and directed four things: promote the MCP-stall finding into ADR-019, record the reduced-scope refusal's implication for Phase 5, merge to `main`, and specifically re-audit `verify_migration.py` since this phase changed the schema's shape, not just its content.** The audit found and fixed a real, second, more serious ownership-boundary gap than anything reported above — recorded honestly here rather than folded silently into the already-closed task summary.

**MCP-stall finding promoted from a code comment into ADR-019's own Consequences** (`docs/build-journey/01_Architecture_Decision_Record.md`), alongside the poison-message bullet as directed: a stalled-but-alive consumer keeps renewing its own lease forever, since the renewal loop has no way to know the work it's renewing for has stopped progressing — worse than a dead consumer, which the visibility timeout already recovers correctly. Recorded explicitly that a dequeue-count limit does not catch this (the message is never redelivered for a count to accumulate against), and that the real fix is a lease-renewal cap or a wall-clock ceiling on total cycle processing time — not built, recorded as the most likely way a run hangs invisibly once deployed.

**The reduced-scope refusal's implication recorded where Phase 5 will actually need it** — `docs/build-journey/12_Migration_Plan.md`'s Phase 5 Definition of Done now carries a direct note: the same small-then-full pattern Phase 5 asks for depends on a real, working ADO tag-mutation path this session confirmed does not exist (the CLI's add-only `System.Tags` update, and this org's own MSA/AAD auth quirk blocking `az rest`/`az devops invoke`) — flagged there so it is confirmed before relying on it, not rediscovered by repeating this same investigation.

**`verify_migration.py` re-audited as explicitly directed, and the audit found a real gap, not just added coverage for its own sake.** Before this review, Phase 4's own checks proved the `investigation` schema and its one table *existed*, and that the boundary held in both *negative* directions (Reporting's role has no access to `investigation`; Investigation's role has no access to `public`) — but nothing anywhere in this project's verification tooling had ever asserted a *positive* grant on any table, for any schema, at any point before now. Added `check_column_exists_in_schema` (all 11 real columns on `investigation.investigation_runs`, individually — not just "the table exists"), `check_constraint_exists_in_schema` (the real `investigation_runs_status_check` CHECK), and `check_has_table_privileges` — the first positive-grant check this file has ever had, added specifically to prove Investigation's own role can genuinely use its own table, not merely that Reporting's role can't. **That new positive check immediately failed against the real, live database — a second, more serious instance of the exact ownership-trap bug class already found once for the schema in this same phase, this time never previously detected:** `ALTER SCHEMA investigation OWNER TO investigation_role_local_dev` (the original fix) changes only the schema's own owner — it does *not* touch the ownership of any table already created inside it, and Postgres ownership and schema-membership are independent. `investigation.investigation_runs` itself was still silently owned by `app_role_local_dev` (Reporting's role) this entire phase, since the table was created during the same original pre-`migrate_investigation.py` run that caused the schema-level bug — giving Reporting's role full, unrestricted implicit access to the very table this whole phase exists to wall off, via ownership rather than any explicit grant, invisible to every existence/negative-privilege check already in place because none of them had ever asked "can this role positively use this object." **Fixed live** via `ALTER TABLE investigation.investigation_runs OWNER TO investigation_role_local_dev` (run as the real Postgres Entra Administrator — `app_role_local_dev` itself cannot run it, since it still lacks USAGE on the schema even though it owned the table inside it) — confirmed via a direct `relacl` check that the stale `app_role_local_dev=arwdDxtm` entry is gone and only `investigation_role_local_dev`'s own owner-grant remains. **A second, real, load-bearing mechanism found while building the fix, not assumed:** both `check_constraint_exists`'s `::regclass` cast and `has_table_privilege`'s text-identifier overload independently fail with the identical real `InsufficientPrivilegeError: permission denied for schema investigation` for a role with no USAGE — schema-qualified *identifier resolution* itself requires schema visibility, unlike a plain `pg_catalog` `WHERE`-clause scan (what every existing schema-safe check in this file already used) or `has_schema_privilege`'s own text-argument form (which doesn't need to resolve into the schema to ask about it). The fix resolves the target object's OID via the same safe `pg_catalog` join pattern first, then passes the OID — not the name — to `has_table_privilege`, avoiding the resolution step entirely. `investigation_migrations/0001_initial_schema.sql`'s own header comment now documents this second trap plainly, including the fact that a genuinely fresh environment applying this file via `migrate_investigation.py` never hits it — this was purely residue from the one historical bad run, not a defect in the current migration script. `verify_migration.py`: **47/47 passing** (was 34; +13: 11 columns, 1 constraint, 1 positive grant). `tests/test_verify_migration.py`: 8 new tests (including 3 forced-failure cases proving each new check can actually detect absence, not just report presence unconditionally). Full suite: **121/121 passing** (was 114).

**Merged to `main`** (`git merge --no-ff phase4/investigation-service`), same discipline as every prior phase: all four running dev services stopped first and every PID confirmed gone (the exact stale-process trap this same task found once already), a clean `git status` on `main` before merging, no conflicts. **Both required checks re-run against the merge result itself, not the branch tip beforehand:** full suite **121/121 passing**; `verify_migration.py` **47/47 passing** (the enhanced version, including the new positive-grant/column/constraint checks and the ownership fix above — this was the actual point of re-running it post-merge, not a formality). **A real end-to-end run performed from the freshly-merged `main`, all four services started clean from that checkout** (`core_api`→`bff`→`investigation`→`reporting`, confirmed one listener per port, zero stale processes) — triggered via the real BFF trigger endpoint against Agentic AI Observability Platform, completed the full real 115-item/7-committed-feature scope across both services via the real queues, reached the correct real terminal state (`not_persisted_already_exists`, report 454, the same genuine pre-existing weekly collision). **Row counts confirmed directly, before and after, not asserted:** `reports` 461/`max_report_id` 532 and `approval_records` 279 identical before the merge and after this real post-merge run (the trigger correctly hit the existing collision, zero new rows); `cycles` moved 21→22, exactly one new row for the one real trigger performed — not a leak, and this table has no DELETE grant to leak against by design. Not pushed to any remote — that step waits for a separate, explicit request, matching this project's own established separation between merging locally and pushing.

**Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-10 — pushed to `main` after three real requests: make the live ownership fix survive Phase 7's from-scratch provisioning, audit for the same drift elsewhere, and record the bug class in Governance & Security.** **The migration file itself did not previously assert ownership** — `scripts/investigation_migrations/0001_initial_schema.sql` relied entirely on the implicit invariant "this file is only ever run by `migrate_investigation.py`, connecting as the Investigation role," true today but not something the SQL itself enforces or fails loudly on. Confirmed first, directly, that `migrate.py`'s `MIGRATIONS_DIR` is hardcoded to `scripts/migrations/` and structurally can never reach `scripts/investigation_migrations/` (so a fresh environment does get correct ownership by construction) — but added an explicit, idempotent `ALTER SCHEMA .../ALTER TABLE ... OWNER TO` block regardless, preferring the real `investigation_role` once it exists over `investigation_role_local_dev`. For the intended path this is a real Postgres no-op (a role asserting ownership of what it already owns); if ownership ever drifts to a different role again, the identical statement now **fails loudly the next time this migration runs**, rather than sitting undetected until another positive-grant check happens to be built — verified live: a real re-run of `migrate_investigation.py` against the already-fixed database applied cleanly, `verify_migration.py` still 47/47. **Audited every table/sequence/view in both `public` and `investigation` for the same drift**, not just re-checked the one already found: all 18 real `public` objects are uniformly owned by `app_role_local_dev` (the one role that has ever run any migration in `scripts/migrations/`) — no drift; `investigation.investigation_runs` now correctly owned by `investigation_role_local_dev`, matching its schema. **No further instances found.** **Recorded in `docs/build-journey/08_Governance_and_Security_Reference.md` §6**, naming both real instances of this exact bug class by name, per instruction: the `app_role_local_dev`-has-more-privileges-than-documented finding (Task 39) and this table-ownership bug (Task 43) — with the generalizable lesson stated plainly: negative-only verification proves discipline, not that the underlying ownership assumption was ever true; a real isolation claim needs both a negative check and a positive one. Full suite re-confirmed 121/121 after the migration-file change. **Pushed to `origin/main`** (`3ae50ed..7c7cdf7`) — `git log --all --full-history -- .env` re-confirmed empty immediately before pushing, same discipline as every prior push.

| Task 44: Migration Phase 5 — containerize the four backend services, locally | See `docs/build-journey/12_Migration_Plan.md`. Branch off merged `main`; main keeps working throughout. Still local — no registry, no Azure deployment, no Managed Identity; `az login` credentials stay mounted in, identity changes in Phase 6, and changing packaging and identity together is how a failure becomes ambiguous. **The image split is the point:** Investigation carries Python AND Node, `@azure-devops/mcp@2.10.0` installed at build time via `npm ci` from the committed lockfile, never resolved at spawn time. Every other image is Python only — verify, don't assume, that Node is absent from core API/BFF/Reporting and that the ADO PAT is reachable only by Investigation. **Logs have to move:** container filesystems are ephemeral, so `logs/<project>_<timestamp>.log` disappears on restart — move to stdout now, keeping the curated console view and heartbeat behavior intact, only the destination changes. **Process shape becomes code:** express the four-service startup order as a compose file — service definitions, dependency order, env var wiring, port mapping — and point the Runbook at it rather than restating it. Streamlit's own placement (in or out of the compose file) is a judgment call to make and justify. **Confirm before relying on it:** re-tagging AOP down to a reduced scope may not actually be possible (Phase 4's own finding) — check first; if it isn't, run full scope only and say so. Bar for done, all real: a full-scope AOP run completing end to end, triggered from Streamlit, every service in a container; Node/the pinned MCP package verified present in the running Investigation container (not just the Dockerfile) and absent from the others; per-tool-call detail visible in container logs with heartbeat gaps under 20s; the kill test repeated against the Investigation container; one connected trace across the queue hop, unchanged from Phase 4; full suite passing, no new rows in `reports`; Runbook updated to point at the compose file. Two things flagged in advance, to report rather than silently work around: the Investigation image will be substantially larger than the others (correct, not a problem); anything resolved by relative path or repo-root assumption will break inside a container | **Done 2026-09-10.** Work on branch `phase5/containerize` off merged `main`. **Real, load-bearing dependency gap found before any Dockerfile could work, not assumed complete:** `fastapi`/`uvicorn`/`httpx`/`PyJWT`/`pydantic` had been pip-installed ad hoc into the local `.venv` across Tasks 40-41 and never actually added to `libs/onepulse_common/pyproject.toml` — a fresh `pip install` (exactly what an image build does) would have silently failed to bring them in. Added, pinned to the real installed versions. **A second real gap, found while sizing the first image:** `streamlit` was a hard dependency of the same shared package, so all four backend images — none of which import it — paid its real size cost (streamlit plus pandas/numpy/pyarrow/altair/pydeck) unconditionally; moved to a real `[project.optional-dependencies] ui` extra, cutting `core_api`'s image from 1.86GB to 1.33GB, confirmed via a real before/after `docker images` measurement, not assumed. **A third, real, pre-existing bug found incidentally while auditing file dependencies for the Reporting image, unrelated to containerization itself:** `STATUS_DECK_PATH_BY_PROJECT` never had an entry for Agentic AI Observability Platform — every real AOP run since Task 27 had silently fallen through to `DEFAULT_STATUS_DECK_PATH` (singleSlide's own deck) for Status Update Analysis. Fixed by wiring in the real `aiobs_status_deck.pptx` that has existed at the repo root, unused, since Task 27. **Logs moved to stdout, both services:** `reporting/main.py`'s per-cycle `logging.FileHandler` replaced with a `logging.StreamHandler(sys.stdout)` on the identical logger (`onepulse.reporting.{cycle_id}`) — same curation logic, same heartbeat cadence, only the destination changed; dead code this left behind (`_sanitize_for_filename`, unused `dt`/`Path`/`re` imports) removed. `investigation/main.py`'s `logging.basicConfig` given an explicit `stream=sys.stdout` rather than relying on its own default (`stderr`, which real container log collectors treat differently enough to matter). **Four real Dockerfiles** (`core_api`, `bff`, `reporting`, `investigation`, each `python:3.12-slim` + a real Debian-installed Azure CLI for `DefaultAzureCredential`'s `AzureCliCredential` fallback), `investigation/Dockerfile` additionally installing real Node.js 20.x (NodeSource, matching this project's locally-proven major version) and running a genuine `npm ci --omit=dev` from the committed `package.json`/`package-lock.json` at build time — the real fix Task 39 prepared, finally given a home. **A real, structural, live-discovered blocker to "az login credentials stay mounted in, exactly as they work today":** a direct test (`docker run -v "$USERPROFILE/.azure:/root/.azure" mcr.microsoft.com/azure-cli az account get-access-token`) failed with `ERROR: User '...' does not exist in MSAL token cache. Run az login.` — not a permissions problem: az CLI encrypts its Windows token cache via DPAPI by default, which a Linux container has no way to decrypt (confirmed by the exact symptom: the plaintext `azureProfile.json` was read fine, enough to know which user *should* have a cached token, but the encrypted `msal_token_cache.bin` itself was unreadable). **The real, working fix:** a shared named Docker volume (`azure_cli_state`), mounted at `/root/.azure` in every backend container, populated once via a real `docker compose run --rm --entrypoint az core_api login --use-device-code` — a genuine interactive step (two device codes expired unused before the third, completed with the user actually at the browser, succeeded within its 15-minute window). The resulting Linux-side cache file has a different real name entirely (`msal_token_cache.json`, plaintext JSON — not `.bin`), confirming az CLI's own cache format differs by platform, not just its encryption. Verified live: `az account get-access-token` from inside the project's own `core_api` image, using only the named volume, returned a real token; the correct real subscription (`onePulse`, tenant `6157b3f3-...`, matching `ONEPULSE_ENTRA_TENANT_ID`) came back as `isDefault: true`. **`docker-compose.yml`, the real new source of truth**, deliberately NOT using a blanket `env_file: .env` per service — compose auto-loads `.env` only for `${VAR}` interpolation *within the YAML itself*, injecting nothing into a container on its own — so each service's `environment:` block lists exactly the real vars its own code reads (confirmed by grep, not assumed), meaning `ONEPULSE_ADO_PAT` is referenced in exactly one place in the entire file: Investigation's own block. This is a real, structural proof of the PAT boundary at the compose-file level, the same standard as the BFF's own import-graph no-data-access proof from Phase 2 — not just convention. Container-network service URLs (`ONEPULSE_CORE_API_BASE_URL=http://core_api:8000`, `ONEPULSE_INVESTIGATION_BASE_URL=http://investigation:8200`) are real service-name resolution on the compose bridge network, distinct from the published `127.0.0.1` ports a host process (Streamlit) uses. **Streamlit deliberately kept OUTSIDE the compose file, staying a host process** — reasoning stated in the file's own header comment: still local-first by design, and Phase 9 replaces it with a real frontend rather than ever containerizing it, so investing in containerizing a temporary artifact isn't worth it now. **A real, structural finding matching this project's own established file-dependency discipline:** `scripts/pptx_mcp_server.py` and the three real status decks are copied into Reporting's image by explicit filename, not a directory glob — the same discipline that already caught the missing-AOP-deck gap above extends naturally to "know exactly what a service needs," not "copy everything and hope." **A real, third recurrence of the exact stale-process bug class this session has now hit three separate times (Task 43, twice within this task alone):** leftover host-level `core_api`/`bff`/`investigation`/`reporting` processes from earlier work were still running, bound to `127.0.0.1` on the identical ports the containers publish to `0.0.0.0` — Windows allows both bindings to coexist with no "port in use" error, so every `curl`/`AppTest` call against `127.0.0.1` was silently reaching the STALE HOST processes instead of the real containers, producing a genuinely confusing "the Investigation container never receives the queued message" symptom with no error anywhere until `netstat`/`Get-CimInstance` directly exposed the double-binding and every stale PID was killed — now a permanent Runbook gotcha. **A real, container-specific bug found only by exec'ing into the running container, never visible in any log this project already had:** the very first real end-to-end attempt failed with an opaque `mcp.shared.exceptions.MCPError: Connection closed` / `unhandled errors in a TaskGroup (1 sub-exception)`, stored verbatim as Investigation's own `error_detail` with no further detail — root-caused by manually spawning the real MCP server binary inside the running container (`docker compose exec investigation node investigation/node_modules/@azure-devops/mcp/dist/index.js ...`), revealing the real crash: `Error: libsecret-1.so.0: cannot open shared object file` — `keytar`, a transitive native dependency of `@azure-devops/mcp`, needs a system library never installed in the minimal Debian-slim base image, a gap invisible locally since this project's own Windows dev machine already has whatever keytar's
