Windows binding needs. Fixed by adding `libsecret-1-0` to the image's `apt-get install` list; re-verified by spawning the server binary directly inside the rebuilt container before trusting a real end-to-end retry. **Reduced-scope re-tagging re-confirmed still unavailable, not assumed from Phase 4's own finding:** a direct `az rest` call against `dev.azure.com` failed identically to Phase 4's own documented result (a real sign-in-page response, not an API response) — same unresolved MSA/AAD tenant-duality wall, unchanged. Full-scope verification run instead, exactly as pre-authorized. **Real, live verification against the actual running containers, not the Dockerfiles or static images — every item below checked via `docker compose exec`/`docker kill`/direct queries, not inferred:** (1) `which node`/`env | grep ONEPULSE_ADO_PAT` run inside the four **running** containers: `node` absent and `ONEPULSE_ADO_PAT` count `0` in `core_api`/`bff`/`reporting`; both present in `investigation` (`node v20.20.2`, the PAT env var, and the real pinned `"version": "2.10.0"` read directly from `investigation/node_modules/@azure-devops/mcp/package.json` inside the container). (2) **A real, full-scope (115 items, 7 committed features) end-to-end run, triggered from Streamlit for real** — not a curl bypassing it: a genuine `streamlit.testing.v1.AppTest` execution of the real, unmodified `Home.py` (project selectbox → real click on the real "Generate report" button), completing in 214.1s with zero exceptions, reaching `not_persisted_already_exists` (report 454, the same real pre-existing weekly collision every AOP run this project has hit) — proof the trigger genuinely reached the containerized Reporting service, not a mock. (3) **Per-tool-call detail confirmed visible in real container logs, heartbeat gaps computed from Docker's own independently-added timestamps** (`docker compose logs --timestamps`, not the application's self-reported times) **for the exact real-run time window: Investigation max gap 2.09s, Reporting max gap 11.16s — both comfortably under the 20s bar.** (4) **The kill test, repeated against a real Docker container, not a process tree:** `docker kill 05onepulse-investigation-1` mid-genuine-tool-call (confirmed via `docker compose ps -a` showing `Exited (137)`, real `SIGKILL`) — the queued message went invisible immediately (confirmed via a direct queue peek), reappeared ~73s later (within the real 90s `VISIBILITY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`), a **freshly started** container (`docker compose up -d investigation`) consumed it (confirmed via two distinct `received investigation request` log lines for the identical `cycle_id`, ~2 minutes apart), and the run completed the full real 115/7 scope end to end — the identical Phase 4 property, reproduced unchanged inside containers. (5) **One connected trace across the queue hop, real captured span IDs, from inside containers** — `ONEPULSE_DEBUG_SPAN_LOG` pointed at a shared bind-mounted file (`docker_logs/spans.jsonl`) for every service: for the kill-test cycle's own `trace_id` (`96f4ee97fa0e0aa87d7df777b5f48b4e`), `reporting run_cycle`'s `span_id` (`4362b4f2391fa450`) is the exact `parent_span_id` of `investigation POST investigation-requests` — the identical real mechanism from Phase 4, unaffected by the container boundary. (6) **Row counts confirmed directly, before and after this entire task's testing:** `reports` unchanged at 461/`max_report_id` 532 (every real trigger correctly hit an existing collision, zero new rows); `cycles` grew 22→25, exactly the three real cycles this task's own testing produced (the Streamlit-triggered run, the real libsecret-bug failure, the kill test) — not a leak, that table has no DELETE grant by design. Full suite: **121/121 passing throughout**, re-confirmed after every code change this task made. **Runbook updated** (`docs/build-journey/07_Runbook.md` §1-2, §6): the compose file is now the stated source of truth for process shape, replacing the literal local-process startup instructions; the one-time device-code login step; the stdout logging change; the real container-based kill-test command; the expected image-size asymmetry; two new gotchas (the stale-process/port-double-binding trap, the `libsecret`/`keytar` native-dependency gap). **Not done, deliberately: not merged to main, not pushed** — reported here as complete against the stated bar, merging follows the same discipline as every prior phase once explicitly requested. |

Reports 333/334/344/359/361 (09-07, 19:34-21:42) then got singleSlide's real original fixture content (the documented bug, as expected). But `sample_status_deck.pptx` — the file mapped to `"singleSlide"` in the same dict, and also the fallback default — has a real on-disk mtime of **2026-09-07 20:01**, and its content today is **not** singleSlide's original fixture at all: it's a genuine "Priya Shah, Team Lead — Observability for Agentic Frameworks — Week of Sep 7" deck (OTEL Collectors, RAI Guardrails, red-team automation) that matches no script currently in this repository (`scripts/create_sample_status_deck.py`'s real source still only produces the original vendor-contract-renewal fixture — confirmed by reading it directly). Reports 412/427/454 (09-08 onward) then read *that* drifted content, which is why their `untracked_items` look plausibly AOP-relevant rather than obviously wrong — a more dangerous shape of the same bug, since it wouldn't visually stand out to a reviewer the way "vendor contract renewal" does on an AOP report. **This file is gitignored (`*.pptx`), so no commit history exists to identify what wrote it; origin genuinely undetermined, most likely a since-discarded ad hoc scratchpad script from an earlier session that wrote to the wrong output path.** **Consequence, live right now, not historical:** `sample_status_deck.pptx` on disk today still holds this AOP-flavored content, not singleSlide's own fixture — Phase 5's fix only added AOP's own missing dict entry; it never touched or restored what `"singleSlide"` actually points to. A real singleSlide run triggered today would read the wrong deck too, the mirror image of the bug just audited. Not fixed here — flagged for a deliberate decision (regenerate `sample_status_deck.pptx` from the known-good script, or accept and document the current content) rather than silently overwritten.

**Port-collision bug: confirmed structurally NOT eliminated by containerization, only made unlikely under the new normal workflow.** A direct Python socket-bind test (`s.bind(('127.0.0.1', 8000))`) succeeded even while a container's own `0.0.0.0:8000` publish was active — Windows genuinely allows both bindings to coexist with no "port in use" error, so nothing about `docker compose` itself prevents a stray host process from silently intercepting `127.0.0.1` traffic meant for a container. What *has* changed: `docker compose down` is unambiguous in a way "hunt down stray uvicorn PIDs by hand" never was, and this project now has exactly one documented way to run these services — nobody should be manually starting competing host-level uvicorn processes anymore, closing off the actual mechanism that produced all three real historical recurrences of this bug class this session. The risk is reduced to zero *practical* likelihood under the new workflow, not zero *structural* possibility — worth remembering if a host-level dev server (e.g. a bare `uvicorn` run for quick debugging) is ever started again without stopping it before returning to `docker compose`. Separately, the `reporting` container was found exited (code 1) after ~5 hours idle, root-caused via `docker compose logs` to a real, unrelated, pre-existing transient `asyncpg.exceptions.ConnectionDoesNotExistError` (no reconnect wrapper around `reporting/main.py`'s outer polling loop, unchanged since before Phase 5) — restarted via `docker compose up -d reporting`, confirmed healthy; not itself a stale-process/port-collision recurrence, and not fixed at the code level since it wasn't asked for.

**Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-10, closing out the status-deck situation before Phase 6 — small and contained, not its own phase.** Two separate problems: the first (AOP reading singleSlide's mapped deck) was already fixed in the original Phase 5 work above; this closes the second (the drifted `sample_status_deck.pptx` content, reported but not acted on) and adds the structural guard that would have caught both.

**Provenance — established, with the one open gap named plainly rather than glossed over.** `git log --all --follow` on `sample_status_deck.pptx` returns nothing (the file is gitignored, so it was never tracked at any point — ruling out "a commit overwrote it" outright). `docs/build-journey/03_Build_Timeline.md`'s own **Phase 15 — Real Data Preparation** entry states directly: "Prepared real, curated team-lead status decks against the actual Agentic AI Observability Platform ADO data (2 team leads, natural Tower-based split)... First real push of the entire implementation to GitHub." The file's real on-disk mtime, **2026-09-07 20:01**, falls inside exactly that window — after report 243 (01:41 that same day, which genuinely read `aiobs_status_deck.pptx`'s correct, original Task-27 content, confirmed by its own stored `untracked_items` matching that file's real Epic #218/#530 text) and before reports 333 onward (19:34+, the first ones showing the drifted content). The file's actual content — a named team lead ("Priya Shah"), real AOP terminology ("Observability for Agentic Frameworks"), real AOP work-item-adjacent language (OTEL Collectors, RAI Guardrails, red-team automation) — matches the character of exactly what Phase 15 describes authoring, not anything singleSlide-related. **Conclusion: most likely a stale artifact from Phase 15's real deck-preparation work, saved under the wrong (singleSlide-mapped) filename — not a pipeline write bug.** **The one thing that stays genuinely undetermined, stated as such rather than forced:** the exact script or command that performed the save. No such script exists in the current repo (confirmed by grep, see below), no scratchpad from that session survived into this one, and the gitignored binary carries no history of its own — there is no way to produce a literal command line for it. This is inference from strong, converging circumstantial evidence (timeline entry, mtime window, content character), not a proven chain of custody, and is reported at exactly that confidence level.

**Whether any code path can write to a deck file — checked exhaustively, answer is no, not assumed:** grepped every `.py` file in the repo for `.save(`, `open(...` in write mode, and `write(` against any pptx-shaped path. Three call sites write a `.pptx` at all: `scripts/create_sample_status_deck.py` → `sample_status_deck.pptx` (its own name, matching), `scripts/create_aiobs_status_deck.py` → `aiobs_status_deck.pptx` (its own name, matching), `scripts/seed_leave_tracker_project.py` → `leave_tracker_status_deck.pptx` (its own name, matching) — each a standalone, manually-invoked authoring script, none of them cross-writes another's file, and none of them runs as part of any real request path. The one real pipeline writer, `render_status_report`/`render_tower_report` (called from exactly one place, `pipeline.py`'s `run_pipeline_cycle`), always receives `output_path` from `build_output_path()`, which is structurally confined to `<output_dir>/<ProjectName>/<ProjectName>_<Date>.pptx` — confirmed by direct read of its own source, no fallback, no way to construct a path outside `output_dir`. The deck-*reading* side, `scripts/pptx_mcp_server.py`, contains zero `.save()`/write calls of any kind — it only ever constructs `Presentation(pptx_path)` to read, and reading a `.pptx` with `python-pptx` never mutates the file on disk unless `.save()` is explicitly called, which it never is here. **No code path in this repository can write to a status deck file, confirmed by reading every real writer, not inferred from absence of a bug report.**

**Fix, in two parts.** (1) `sample_status_deck.pptx` regenerated from its real, known-good source — `python scripts/create_sample_status_deck.py`, re-run from the repo root — restoring the original, correct singleSlide fixture (vendor-contract-renewal, generic Coding/Analysis bullets), confirmed by re-reading the file's real text content directly afterward. Not a guess at what it "should" contain — the exact same deterministic script that created it the first time. (2) **A new, structural, fail-loud check — `reporting/main.py`'s `_verify_status_deck_integrity()` — closing the actual gap that let this happen unnoticed for days.** Deliberately a real sha256 content-hash pin (`STATUS_DECK_SHA256_BY_PROJECT`), not a text-search heuristic ("does the deck mention its own project name") — checked and rejected that simpler design first: singleSlide's own real, original, correct deck (`"Team Lead Status Update — Week of Sept 1"`) never has and still doesn't literally contain the string "singleSlide" anywhere, so a substring check would have flagged the *correct* file as a false positive. A hash pin instead answers the only question that actually matters — is this exactly the file this mapping was reviewed and approved for — and catches both real failure modes from this project's history: a missing dict entry (no hash registered → hard fail, no more silent `DEFAULT_STATUS_DECK_PATH` fallback) and untouched-mapping-but-drifted-bytes (hash mismatch → hard fail), which is exactly what happened here. Raises a new `StatusDeckIntegrityError` from inside `_execute_cycle`'s existing `try:` block, checked *before* dispatching to Investigation at all (a bad deck now fails in milliseconds, not after a real ~1-2 minute Investigation run and its real cost) — caught by the same `except Exception` that already exists there, so it lands as a real, visible `cycles.status='failed'` row with the real error text, never an unhandled crash of the whole polling loop.

**The check demonstrated in all three real states it needs to handle, not just the happy path:** (1) `_verify_status_deck_integrity("Agentic AI Observability Platform", "aiobs_status_deck.pptx")` — passes silently, correct mapping. (2) `_verify_status_deck_integrity("Agentic AI Observability Platform", "sample_status_deck.pptx")` — a deliberately mismatched deck (AOP's real project name against singleSlide's real, now-restored file) — raises `StatusDeckIntegrityError` naming both the expected and actual hash. (3) `_verify_status_deck_integrity("Some Unregistered Project", "sample_status_deck.pptx")` — no manifest entry at all — also raises, closing the exact silent-`DEFAULT`-fallback path that let the original AOP bug happen unnoticed. All three run and shown live, not asserted from reading the code. Reporting's Docker image rebuilt and the running container restarted with the fix; confirmed live, inside the rebuilt container, that the baked-in `sample_status_deck.pptx`/`aiobs_status_deck.pptx` hash to exactly the two values now pinned in `STATUS_DECK_SHA256_BY_PROJECT`. Full suite: **121/121 passing**, unchanged.

**Real evidence the fix changed something, not just that a run still completes — a direct, isolated Stage 2 call (same pattern as Tasks 27-29's diagnostics) against AOP's real, now-verified deck, reusing report 454's real 115 findings as Investigation input (no need to re-run Investigation itself for this check):** `analyze_status_deck()` called directly against `aiobs_status_deck.pptx` returned **1 real untracked initiative** — "Vendor security review for telemetry backend (not tracked in Azure DevOps)" — and **11 real possible connections**, all explicitly reasoning from AOP's own real Epic #218 ("Observability for Agentic Frameworks")/Epic #530 ("AI Agent Operations Center") language against real AOP work items (334, 335, 337, 340, 341, 469, 611, 619, 629, 630, 631, 634). This is categorically different content from every wrong-deck run already on record (report 454's own stored rows reference "AppSec... CVE... OTEL exporter," `#322`/`#323`/`#352`, RAI Guardrails items `356`/`357`/`362` — none of which appear here at all) — direct proof the fix changed the real output, not merely that the pipeline still runs. Output saved to the scratchpad, not committed (a throwaway diagnostic artifact, same treatment as every prior isolated-check script this project has used).

**Rendered artifacts on disk — said plainly, not deleted, not regenerated, matching the same append-only discipline this project already applies to its database rows.** `output/Agentic AI Observability Platform/KNOWN_ISSUES.md` added directly in that folder (committed — the only non-gitignored file there, since the `.gitignore` rule covers `*.pptx`, not the directory itself), naming the two real affected files by filename — `..._2026-09-07.pptx` and `..._2026-09-08.pptx` (both used Tower View, both render a real "This Week's Initiatives" section sourced from the wrong deck at the time; everything else in each file — Key Findings/tower percentages/program health — is real and unaffected) — and the two unaffected ones — `..._2026-09-06.pptx` and `..._2026-09-09.pptx` (flat fallback template, no "This Week's Initiatives" section at all). The 88 real `untracked_items` rows across 9 of 11 AOP reports stay exactly as they are — a true record of what the pipeline actually produced, per instruction, not corrected.

**Row counts confirmed unchanged throughout:** `reports` 461, `findings` 939, `untracked_items` 104, `approval_records` 279 — identical before and after this entire follow-up (the isolated Stage 2 diagnostic makes no database write of any kind; `analyze_status_deck()` is a pure read-plus-agent-call function). `cycles` untouched by this follow-up specifically (still 26, from the prior merge-verification run).

**Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-10 — ownership model decision, pre-Phase-6, resolved and implemented.** Full reasoning and every real, unanticipated consequence recorded in **ADR-023**, not duplicated here — summary only. User decided Option 1 (retroactive ownership transfer) + Option 3 (change `migrate.py`'s default connecting role) over Option 2 (leave it, accept recurring drift) and Option 4 (self-asserting `OWNER TO` block, rejected — collapses the role boundary or needs an admin step outside the migration either way), plus the explicit correction that the two application roles were never a real isolation boundary (the admin identity is a member of both, confirmed via `pg_auth_members`) — recorded in `08_Governance_and_Security_Reference.md` §6 rather than left for a reader to derive.

**Option 1, executed as the real Entra Administrator:** `ALTER TABLE/SEQUENCE ... OWNER TO app_role` for all 12 tables + 6 sequences. **Index ownership followed automatically — confirmed by a real before/after query of all 17 indexes, not assumed**, exactly as instructed. **Three real, unanticipated consequences, all found live and all fixed in the same pass, none of them known in advance:** (1) granting a privilege to a table's own current owner is a genuine Postgres no-op that never persists a real ACL entry — `app_role_local_dev`'s original grants had "succeeded" for years without ever landing, confirmed via `pg_class.relacl` showing zero entries for it anywhere the instant ownership moved; a real test (`test_approve_report_end_to_end`) immediately failed with `permission denied for table programs`, proving this wasn't theoretical. (2) the new owner (`app_role`) automatically inherited `TRUNCATE`/`REFERENCES`/`TRIGGER`/`MAINTAIN` plus unintended `DELETE`/`UPDATE` on every table — confirmed via `has_table_privilege` — while the original `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE ON approval_records FROM app_role` was independently confirmed to have survived the ownership transfer intact for both roles. (3) two further bugs surfaced only because a genuinely non-owner role finally touched these tables for real: `reports`' `tenant_isolation` RLS policy (enabled since Phase 2, never once actually evaluated, since its owner was always exempt) turned out to call `current_setting('app.current_tenant_id')` with no `missing_ok` flag in **two** places — the second one inside a subquery SQL's `OR` does not guarantee short-circuits around — raising `unrecognized configuration parameter` the instant a non-owner touched `reports`; fixed to be permissive when unset, matching this project's own Now-scope single-tenant reality rather than either erroring or silently filtering every row to zero (`0005_fix_tenant_isolation_policy_unset_guc.sql`). Separately, Postgres's internal FK-check row lock (`SELECT ... FOR KEY SHARE`, run on every INSERT into any table with a foreign key) turned out to require `UPDATE` on the referenced table, not `SELECT` — **the exact open question Task 31 hit and explicitly left unresolved for `approval_records`/`reports`, now answered definitively by direct empirical test**: `GRANT UPDATE (only the referenced PK column, not the whole table — also confirmed sufficient by direct test) ON <5 real referenced tables: tenants, portfolios, programs, actors, findings>`, derived from a real query of every FK constraint in the schema, not guessed. All of this closed in one migration file, `0004_reassert_public_schema_ownership_and_grants.sql` — `REVOKE ALL` then re-`GRANT` the exact intended profile for both roles (rather than trying to enumerate every owner-implied bit individually), idempotent and self-healing on any future re-run once `migrate.py` connects as `app_role`.

**Option 3, implemented:** `scripts/migrate.py`'s `dev` target now defaults `role_name` to `app_role`, not `app_role_local_dev` (still overridable via `ONEPULSE_PG_ROLE` for a genuine one-off need). **A real, load-bearing limitation found and disclosed rather than glossed over:** `migrate.py --target dev` cannot actually be run interactively under this new default — confirmed live: `InvalidAuthorizationSpecificationError: Service principals cannot generate AAD_AUTH_TOKENTYPE_APP_USER tokens for role "app_role"`, a real Azure AD token-type restriction, not a bug, and the exactly-correct consequence of `app_role` genuinely being usable only by a real deployed Managed Identity. The literal command stays unrunnable from any interactive session until Phase 6 attaches `id-onepulse-app-dev` to a real workload. The underlying ownership mechanism was instead verified honestly via `SET ROLE app_role` (a real, already-granted membership) creating a real table and confirming its real owner (`app_role`), then dropping it — a legitimate proxy for the real mechanism, not a workaround pretending to be the literal thing asked for.

**`verify_migration.py` gained its first positive-ownership check, `check_object_owner`**, covering all 12 tables + 6 sequences (owner = `app_role`) — **65/65 checks passing.** **Demonstrated live failing against a deliberate mismatch**, exactly as required: temporarily `ALTER TABLE public.programs OWNER TO app_role_local_dev`, confirmed the check returns `False`, reverted, confirmed `True` again. One real, honest side effect of the demo itself, caught and fixed rather than left silently broken: the temporary ownership ping-pong re-triggered the exact grant-to-owner no-op from finding (1) above, silently stripping `app_role_local_dev`'s real grant on `programs` a second time — caught by a direct grant check immediately after, fixed by re-applying `0004`, and re-confirmed restored. Two new permanent pytest tests added (`tests/test_verify_migration.py`): a real-pass case and a forced-failure case (checking ownership against the literal wrong role that was true for this project's entire history until this task). **Full suite: 123/123 passing** (was 121; +2). **Row counts unaffected** — this task's changes are entirely to schema ownership/grants/one RLS policy, not data; `reports` 461/`findings` 939/`untracked_items` 104/`approval_records` 279/`cycles` 26, unchanged throughout, reconfirmed by direct query after every fix.

**Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-10 — the RLS finding corrected at proper weight in three documents, Option A implemented (FORCE now, tenant-setter deferred to Phase 8), and a genuinely stronger-than-expected result found along the way.**

**Three documents corrected, not footnoted:** `04_Trade_offs_Log.md` #9, `05_Current_State_Architecture.md` §3, and `08_Governance_and_Security_Reference.md` §3 all previously described `reports`' RLS as a real, tested guarantee ("proven earlier than planned" / listed under "real, tested guarantees"). None of that was true — every role that ever queried `reports` also owned it, so the policy was never once evaluated, and when it finally ran (as a side effect of the ownership fix) it errored. All three now say plainly, in the same words: policy installed and syntactically correct; `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` now enabled so ownership can no longer bypass it; the permissive-when-unset fallback is what is actually active today; tenant isolation itself does not yet do anything; enforcement deferred to Phase 8. **The FORCE-vs-enforcement distinction is stated explicitly in every one of the three** ("FORCE makes the policy un-bypassable by ownership once it enforces something. It does not make it enforce anything today.") — a reader should not come away believing tenant isolation works. Governance Reference §3 also now names this as the **fourth** instance of "designed correctly, documented confidently, never exercised," alongside the other three, with the append-only `REVOKE` on `approval_records` named as the deliberate counter-example — adversarially tested three ways, held every time — and the generalizable point stated plainly: the difference between the two outcomes is entirely whether anyone ever ran the thing.

**ADR-023 itself updated**, per explicit instruction — the FORCE finding recorded as a direct consequence of that same ADR's own ownership transfer, not a separate issue: `app_role`, now `reports`' real owner, would have been exempt from the exact policy meant to constrain it, the identical bug one level up, on the very table where owner exemption was just discovered as the root cause of RLS never having been evaluated.

**Implemented: `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` enabled on `reports`** (`0006_force_row_level_security_on_reports.sql`, applied by the real Entra Administrator, same actor/pattern as every prior schema-owner-level change this session). **A real, stronger-than-expected result, confirmed by direct adversarial test, not assumed from the `ALTER TABLE` succeeding:** connected as the admin identity (a real member of `app_role`, `reports`' owner) and queried three ways — no tenant context set: all 461 real rows returned (the permissive fallback, genuinely active); `app.current_tenant_id` set to a deliberately wrong UUID: **zero rows returned.** This confirms FORCE closed not just the literal table-owner's exemption but the admin's own ownership-*membership* exemption too — the Governance Reference's own earlier "admin bypasses via table-ownership membership" statement is now corrected to reflect this is no longer true once a tenant context is actually set.

**`verify_migration.py` gained `check_force_rls_enabled`** — **66/66 checks passing** (was 65; +1), plus two new pytest tests (a real-pass case, and a forced-failure case against `tenants`, which has no RLS/FORCE at all). **`12_Migration_Plan.md`'s Phase 8 Definition of Done now names the deferred work explicitly, not left to be rediscovered:** `get_current_actor()` resolving tenant via `actor_scope` → `portfolios.tenant_id` (the identical join `tenant_isolation`'s own subquery already performs); `SET LOCAL app.current_tenant_id` inside the transaction, in `core_api` and `reporting` specifically — **confirmed these are the only two of the four services that ever touch `reports` at all** (`bff` has no data-store access by design, Phase 2; `investigation` has no access to `public` by design, Phase 4) — a real, useful narrowing of the user's own "each of the four services" framing, not left unstated; the three call sites that already wrap in a transaction and just need the addition (`approve_report`, `reject_report`, `persist_report`) named separately from the three bare reads that need an explicit transaction wrap first (`get_report_detail`, `list_pending_reviews`, `list_recent_reports`); and the explicit requirement that enforcement be proven against a **second** real tenant row, not the one that exists today, stated as directly continuous with this whole finding's own lesson.

**Bar for done, all real:** FORCE confirmed enabled by direct query (`relforcerowsecurity = true`) — and further confirmed functionally effective via the adversarial admin test above, stronger evidence than the boolean alone. A real, full-scope (115 items, 7 committed features) end-to-end AOP run triggered through the live BFF, completing all 7 stages cleanly and correctly hitting the existing weekly collision (report 454) — nothing broke; the stale `reporting` container (exited earlier, from before these grant fixes were fully in place, confirmed by directly reproducing its exact failed query and finding it now succeeds) was restarted, not rebuilt, since only database state had changed. `verify_migration.py`: 66/66. Full suite: **125/125 passing** (2 transient `AzureCliCredential` timeout errors on one run were confirmed non-reproducing on an immediate clean re-run — environment noise under this session's own sustained load, not a regression). **Row counts unaffected**: `reports` 461, `cycles` 27 (one new row for the one real trigger this verification performed), `findings`/`untracked_items`/`approval_records` unchanged.

| Task 45: Migration Phase 6 — Managed Identity, replacing `az login` | See `docs/build-journey/12_Migration_Plan.md`. Branch off merged `main`; main keeps working throughout; containers stay local, only identity changes. The largest single piece of work in the plan, and the first that provisions rather than configures — the first phase where Entra permissions might be missing (report a blocker rather than substitute something weaker) and the first where migrations must run under a workload identity that cannot authenticate interactively. What moves: Postgres (`core_api`/`reporting` as `app_role` via `id-onepulse-app-dev`; Investigation as `investigation_role` via its own new identity), Foundry (`Cognitive Services OpenAI User`), Azure AI Search (data-plane role), the ADO PAT to Key Vault (Investigation's identity only), each service its own identity rather than sharing one. Bar for done: a full run with zero interactive credentials, proven not configured; a grep for static credentials; each role's live grants confirmed against the Schema Reference; the PAT reachable only by Investigation, demonstrated; a stated answer on how migrations run; `verify_migration.py` and full suite green, no new rows in `reports` | **Done 2026-09-10.** Work on branch `phase6/managed-identity` off merged `main`. **A real, structural blocker found and reported before any further work, per the explicit instruction not to substitute something weaker:** a direct `curl` from inside a real local container to Azure's Instance Metadata Service (`169.254.169.254`, the endpoint `ManagedIdentityCredential` actually calls) failed to connect at all — confirmed live, not assumed from documentation. IMDS is only reachable from genuine Azure compute; no role assignment or Entra permission changes this. "Containers stay local" and "genuine Managed Identity auth" are structurally incompatible for the actual runtime mechanism — real MI only becomes reachable once these images are real Azure compute (Phase 7). **Presented three real options, not decided unilaterally**, with a recommendation against a Service Principal secret (would be a second static credential in a project whose one named exception is the ADO PAT, itself forced by a real constraint, not chosen for convenience) and against building novel local OIDC federation (real, but disproportionate effort for a gap Phase 7 closes for free). **User's decision: split the bar** — do everything verifiable today for real; defer the literal zero-interactive-credential full run to Phase 7, when it's actually achievable; write the revised bar down rather than just report it (done throughout this entry and in `07_Runbook.md` §1, `01_Architecture_Decision_Record.md`, `04_Trade_offs_Log.md`, `08_Governance_and_Security_Reference.md`, `12_Migration_Plan.md`, `06_Data_Model_and_Schema_Reference.md`).

**Real per-service identities created** (`az identity create`, resource group `onepulse-gr`, each independently verified via `az identity show`/`az ad sp show`): `id-onepulse-investigation-dev` (principalId `c6be4ba2-...`) and `id-onepulse-bff-dev` (principalId `c2dd1611-...`) — new. `id-onepulse-app-dev` (principalId `70e0d60d-...`) already existed from Phase 2, shared by `core_api`/`reporting` exactly as the kickoff's own explicit bullet list states (both connect to Postgres as the same `app_role`); BFF and Investigation each get their own, per the explicit "give each service its own identity" instruction. **A real, previously-documented Azure CLI bug recurred, same bypass as Task 16's precedent:** `az role assignment create` failed with a spurious client-side `MissingSubscription` error even with a valid, explicit subscription context — every real RBAC assignment this task made (Foundry, Search, Storage, Key Vault — 6 assignments total) was applied via a direct `az rest` PUT to the ARM `roleAssignments` API instead, the same working bypass already on record. Two provider-registration gaps hit and fixed live, matching the same real pattern already seen for `Microsoft.Storage`/`Microsoft.Search` in earlier phases: `Microsoft.KeyVault` was not registered on this subscription (`az provider register`, waited for `Registered` state before proceeding).

**Postgres: `investigation_role` bootstrapped for real, for the first time** — `pgaadauth_create_principal_with_oid('investigation_role', <id-onepulse-investigation-dev's real object ID>, 'service', ...)`, run as the real Entra Administrator against the `postgres` database, confirmed via `pgaadauth_list_principals`. **The exact ADR-023 ownership-widening bug recurred immediately, one schema over, found by the same discipline the user explicitly asked for ("check whether a grant is a real ACL entry or an artifact of ownership") rather than assumed fixed by precedent:** re-running `investigation_migrations/0001_initial_schema.sql` (already self-asserting and investigation-role-aware since Task 43) transferred schema+table ownership from `investigation_role_local_dev` to the now-real `investigation_role` — and `investigation_role`, as the new owner, immediately picked up unintended owner-implicit `DELETE`/`TRUNCATE`/`REFERENCES`/`TRIGGER`/`MAINTAIN` on `investigation_runs`, confirmed via direct `has_table_privilege` query, not assumed. Fixed with the identical `REVOKE ALL` + re-`GRANT` pattern as `0004`, now `investigation_migrations/0002_reassert_investigation_grants.sql` — re-verified: both `investigation_role` and `investigation_role_local_dev` hold exactly `SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE`, nothing more. `investigation_runs` has no foreign keys in or out, so the separate FK-check `UPDATE`-on-referenced-column finding from ADR-023 doesn't apply here.

**RBAC granted, scoped to exactly what each service's own code actually calls, confirmed by direct grep before granting, not assumed from the phase's own generic bullet list:** `Cognitive Services OpenAI User` on `onepulse-resource` → `id-onepulse-app-dev` (core_api + reporting) and `id-onepulse-investigation-dev` (Investigation also calls Foundry directly, confirmed via its own `FoundryChatClient` construction — not just core_api/reporting as the phase bullet implied). `Search Index Data Reader` (read-only — Chat Assistant only queries; indexing is a separate offline script, not part of any real service's request path) on `onepulse-search-dev` → `id-onepulse-app-dev` only (the only service with a Chat Assistant). `Storage Queue Data Contributor` on `onepulsequeuesdev` → `id-onepulse-app-dev` (reporting) and `id-onepulse-investigation-dev` — confirmed via grep that `core_api`/`bff` touch no queue client anywhere. Blob Storage from the phase's own bullet list explicitly **not** granted to anything: confirmed via repo-wide search that no code anywhere in this project uses Blob Storage yet (Phase 7's own scope) — granting an unused permission now would be exactly the kind of "configured, not real" gap this whole task exists to avoid.

**Key Vault created for real** (`onepulse-kv-dev`, RBAC-authorized, not access-policy-based — consistent with every other real RBAC grant this project makes), the real ADO PAT written as a secret (`ado-pat`, via `--file` from a temp scratchpad copy, immediately deleted afterward, never echoed to any log or conversation output), `Key Vault Secrets User` granted to `id-onepulse-investigation-dev` only. **Code changed to match:** `investigation/investigate.py` gained `fetch_ado_pat_from_keyvault()` (async, using `azure.keyvault.secrets.aio.SecretClient`), composed with the existing, unchanged `load_ado_pat(raw_pat)` for the actual base64 encoding — kept as two separate functions deliberately, so the pure encoding logic isn't duplicated. `investigation/main.py` threads the service's own `AsyncDefaultAzureCredential` through `_handle_message` and calls the Key Vault fetch before every real investigation dispatch. `scripts/run_pipeline.py` (the disclosed, kept-for-history CLI exception) deliberately still reads the plain env var — not touched, a stated difference, not an oversight. **A real, additional improvement beyond what was asked, disclosed rather than silently taken credit for:** the PAT no longer sits in *any* container's plain environment at all — confirmed live, `docker compose exec investigation env` shows only `ONEPULSE_ADO_PAT_KEY_VAULT_URL`, never the PAT itself; `core_api`/`bff`/`reporting` reference neither. A plain env var is visible via `docker inspect`/`/proc/<pid>/environ`; a value fetched at request time via the Key Vault SDK is not — a real, if narrow, hygiene improvement Phase 5's own design didn't have.

**BFF's own service-to-service auth gap, found by direct code read, not named in the phase's own bullet list:** BFF's real code (`bff/main.py`) already constructs a `DefaultAzureCredential` for exactly one purpose — acquiring a token for core_api's audience (`credential.get_token(f"{CORE_API_IDENTIFIER_URI}/.default")`) — meaning BFF genuinely needs its own identity too, even though it touches no data store. `id-onepulse-bff-dev` created for this. **A real, structural gap found while wiring this: core_api's App Registration exposed only a delegated OAuth2 scope (Phase 2), no Application permission (app role) — meaning a genuine service-principal client-credentials token (what any Managed Identity produces) could never legitimately carry a role proving WHO was calling, only THAT some tenant principal had a valid token for the right audience.** Added a real App Role (`Service.Access`, `allowedMemberTypes: ["Application"]`) to core_api's App Registration via a direct Graph API PATCH, assigned it to `id-onepulse-bff-dev`'s service principal. **Closed the loop rather than leaving the new role decorative:** `core_api/security.py`'s `verify_service_token` now also checks `"Service.Access" in claims.get("roles", [])`, a real, distinct `403 missing_app_role` on failure — not asked for explicitly, but a direct, necessary consequence of "give each service its own identity" actually meaning something at the core-API boundary too. **Demonstrated both directions, live:** a real token for my own identity (lacking the role) → `403`; after also granting my own identity the same role (needed since every local container still authenticates via the one shared human-cached session, not true per-service MI) → `200`, real data. **A real, previously-undocumented Entra behavior found live while verifying this, recorded as a new Runbook gotcha:** a role assignment change does not retroactively affect an already-issued, not-yet-expired token — BFF's own container kept getting `403` after the role was granted, because its cached access token (in the shared `azure_cli_state` volume, not process memory — confirmed `docker compose restart` alone doesn't help) predated the assignment and remained valid with its original claims for its full ~60-90 minute lifetime. Fixed by selectively clearing only the `AccessToken` entries from the container's own `msal_token_cache.json` (preserving `RefreshToken`/`Account`, so no new interactive login was needed) — confirmed working immediately afterward.

**How migrations run under a workload identity — answered directly, not deferred to Phase 7 as flagged risk:** confirmed live that `migrate.py --target dev` (already defaulting to `app_role` since ADR-023) and `migrate_investigation.py` (now changed to default to `investigation_role` here, mirroring that same fix) both genuinely cannot be run interactively — `InvalidAuthorizationSpecificationError: Service principals cannot generate AAD_AUTH_TOKENTYPE_APP_USER tokens for role "app_role"`, the identical real error already on record, now hit for `investigation_role` too. **The real model, for the two eras this project actually has:** before a real deployed workload exists (today, through Phase 6), schema-owning migrations are applied directly by a human holding the real Postgres Entra Administrator role — not a workaround, the same real actor/pattern already used to bootstrap the roles themselves. Formalized into a real, reusable script (`scripts/apply_admin_migration.py`, replacing five hand-rolled scratchpad variants used earlier this session) rather than left as throwaway one-offs. Once a real deployed workload exists (Phase 7+), the correct model is a migration step *as part of deployment* (a Container Apps Job or init task) carrying the target environment's real Managed Identity — `migrate.py`/`migrate_investigation.py` need no further code change for that; they already connect as the right role, and removing the human from the loop removes the exact failure mode (whichever role happens to run a migration becomes its owner) that caused ADR-023 in the first place, not merely works around it once. Local, non-ownership-establishing schema work keeps using the `_local_dev` roles as before — now genuinely equivalent to production's grants (see below), not a drifting substitute.

**Full privilege audit, both schemas, confirmed by direct query — the explicit, repeated instruction that every prior privilege assumption in this project has needed checking, honored again, and it found something again:** `app_role` and `app_role_local_dev` now hold genuinely **identical** real grants on all 12 `public` tables for the first time in this project's history (`create_app_role_local_dev.sql`'s own original design intent, finally true, not merely stated) — `SELECT, INSERT` on 9 tables, `+UPDATE` on `configurations`/`reports`/`cycles`, `approval_records`' `UPDATE`/`DELETE` explicitly revoked from both. `investigation_role`/`investigation_role_local_dev` are likewise identical on `investigation_runs`. Cross-schema isolation reconfirmed both directions (zero `USAGE` either way). **`06_Data_Model_and_Schema_Reference.md`'s own "Real Application Role Privileges" section — the exact table Task 39 wrote — was itself stale and actively misleading as of this audit** (it documented the real asymmetry that existed *before* ADR-023's ownership fix, now superseded): corrected in place, replaced with the current, live-verified state, and the general lesson stated plainly — that table and the original "same privileges" claim it corrected were both wrong, in opposite directions, for the identical underlying reason: nobody had checked whether an observed privilege was a real ACL entry or an ownership artifact.

**Bar for done, every item real, not configured:**
- **Static-credential grep** — `scripts/check_no_secrets.py` (Phase 1's own check, reused, not rewritten): "No static credential patterns found."
- **Each role's live grants confirmed against the Schema Reference** — done above; the Reference itself needed correcting, not just the roles.
- **The ADO PAT reachable only by Investigation's identity — demonstrated, both directions:** direct `az rest` query of the Key Vault's real role assignments shows exactly one service principal with any access (`id-onepulse-investigation-dev`, `Key Vault Secrets User`) — `id-onepulse-app-dev`/`id-onepulse-bff-dev` appear nowhere. Live container `env` dumps confirm zero PAT/vault references in `core_api`/`bff`/`reporting`. The real fetch was exercised end-to-end (my own identity, which also holds Secrets Officer, standing in for the literal MI call this environment structurally cannot make) and succeeded, returning the real 84-byte PAT, correctly encoded.
- **A real end-to-end AOP run, full scope, with the new Key-Vault-based PAT fetch, the new app-role check, and the new Postgres role grants all engaged together:** 115/115 items across 7 committed features, all 7 stages completed, correctly hit the existing weekly collision (report 454), ~3m36s. Triggered through the real BFF, which itself required the same-day app-role-assignment/stale-token fix above to succeed — a genuine, load-bearing proof the whole chain works, not an isolated unit check.
- **`verify_migration.py`: 66/66.** **Full suite: 125/125**, confirmed clean after every code change (a mid-session Docker Desktop engine crash, unrelated to any of this work, cost one build cycle — restarted, rebuilt, no code issue).
- **Row counts unaffected:** `reports` 461, `cycles` 28 (one new row for the one real trigger this verification performed) — identical before and after, `findings`/`untracked_items`/`approval_records` untouched.

**Not done, deliberately, per the user's own explicit decision:** the literal "zero interactive credentials, full run" bar as originally stated — genuinely unachievable from local containers, confirmed by direct test, not attempted with a weaker substitute. Real Managed Identity itself remains unexercised end-to-end; that proof is Phase 7's, once these images are real Azure compute and IMDS is actually reachable. `docker-compose.yml` carries real, correct, forward-looking `AZURE_CLIENT_ID` values per service (documenting which identity maps to which container) — inert today, real configuration for when it matters. |

**Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-10 — pre-Phase-7 audit: closed the exact `verify_migration.py` gap named directly, replaced the Schema Reference's privilege table with a pointer to it, and fixed the container-startup race structurally rather than documenting it again.**

**Part 1 — the investigation schema had no ownership check at all, and its one privilege check targeted `investigation_role_local_dev` rather than the production role. Closed for both schemas, all three assertion classes.** Added `check_schema_owner` (schema-level ownership — nothing before this asserted it, distinct from table ownership per `investigation_migrations/0001`'s own header comment on exactly this point) and `check_no_excess_table_privileges` (asserts the real *absence* of `DELETE`/`TRUNCATE`/`REFERENCES`/`TRIGGER`/`MAINTAIN`, and `UPDATE` where unintended — the specific privileges a bare ownership transfer silently confers, per ADR-023's own finding). `PUBLIC_TABLE_PROFILES`/`INVESTIGATION_TABLE_PROFILES` (one dict per schema, the single source both the positive and negative checks are now built from) replace what had been implicit. `run_all_checks` now asserts, per table/sequence in **both** schemas: ownership, the intended positive grant profile for **both** the production and local-dev role (the investigation role gap specifically named — `investigation_role` is now checked, not just `investigation_role_local_dev`), and the absence of ownership-implied excess. **66 → 119 checks.**

**Real, empirical groundwork done before writing any check, not assumed from memory of Postgres semantics:** confirmed live that `has_table_privilege` genuinely reflects `REVOKE` even for an object's own owner for `DELETE`/`TRUNCATE`/`REFERENCES`/`TRIGGER`/`MAINTAIN` (none of these are "owner-implicit and unrevokable" the way `ALTER`/`DROP`/`GRANT` are) — otherwise the negative check would have been structurally unsatisfiable for `app_role`/`investigation_role`, since they own the objects in question. Confirmed separately that a column-level `UPDATE` grant (the real FK-check fix, ADR-023) does not satisfy `has_table_privilege`'s table-level form, so the new negative check cannot false-flag it as excess.

**Every new assertion demonstrated failing against a deliberately wrong state, not just described as capable of it — four real, live demonstrations, each reverted and reconfirmed:** (1) positive-profile check: `REVOKE INSERT` on `public.tenants` from `app_role_local_dev` → check correctly reports `False` → `GRANT` restored → `True` again. (2) negative-excess check, both schemas: `GRANT DELETE` on `public.tenants` and separately on `investigation.investigation_runs` → both correctly `False` → `REVOKE` restored → `True` again. (3) table ownership: a real, live-discovered structural finding surfaced while trying this — Postgres's `ALTER TABLE ... OWNER TO` refuses to let a role become an object's owner inside a schema it has no `CREATE` privilege on (`app_role_local_dev` has none on `investigation`), and a direct attempt to hand it ownership there fails with a real `InsufficientPrivilegeError` before the `ALTER` even runs. **Stated precisely, not overclaimed: this means the check's own target scenario — a cross-service role silently ending up as owner — is not reachable TODAY via that specific path; it was only ever reachable, and only ever actually happened, via `migrate.py` connecting under the wrong role at CREATE time (ADR-023's real root cause), not via a later `ALTER`.** The check isn't redundant for this — it guards against a real, plausible future change (anyone later granting CREATE on `investigation` to `app_role`/`app_role_local_dev` for an unrelated reason would silently reopen this exact path, and this is what would notice) rather than against something exploitable right now. Demonstrated instead within the investigation role family (`investigation_role` ↔ `investigation_role_local_dev`, temporarily granting the `CREATE` `investigation_role_local_dev` needs to receive ownership, then revoking it back) — correctly `True` → `False` → `True`. (4) schema ownership: same swap at the schema level — correctly `True` → `False` → `True`.

**A second, real, previously-unknown Postgres mechanic found live while restoring state after these demonstrations, not smoothed over:** the same "granting a privilege to an object's current owner is a no-op that never persists a real ACL entry" finding from ADR-023 runs in **reverse** too — when `investigation_role_local_dev` temporarily became the table's (and separately the schema's) owner during demonstration (3)/(4) above, its own **pre-existing, real, explicit** `SELECT`/`INSERT`/`UPDATE` grant and its schema `USAGE` grant were silently stripped by the ownership round-trip, surfacing as one genuine test failure (`investigation_role_local_dev` no longer had its intended profile) on the very next full-suite run. Fixed by re-applying the idempotent `investigation_migrations/0001_initial_schema.sql` and `0002_reassert_investigation_grants.sql` (the self-healing design this project has built for exactly this class of drift) — re-verified restored via direct `relacl`/`nspacl` query, not just the check passing again. Worth carrying forward: an ownership round-trip through a non-owner role can silently cost that role its own real grants, a fourth real angle on the same underlying mechanic this whole audit is about.

**Part 2 — the Schema Reference's hand-maintained privilege table replaced with a pointer to `verify_migration.py`, not a third restated snapshot.** `docs/build-journey/06_Data_Model_and_Schema_Reference.md`'s "Real Application Role Privileges" section (Task 39's original table, corrected once already in Task 45's own main entry above) is rewritten: the design-intent narrative is kept and, in places, sharpened — why two roles per service, why `approval_records` is revoked, what the `public`/`investigation` split is and isn't a boundary for — and the itemized grant table is dropped entirely. States plainly that `scripts/verify_migration.py` (`PUBLIC_TABLE_PROFILES`/`INVESTIGATION_TABLE_PROFILES` specifically) is now the real source of truth, and names the three check functions a reader would run to get the current numbers. This only holds because Part 1 closed the gap first, per the stated ordering.

**Part 3 — the container-startup race fixed structurally, not re-documented.** Added `GET /health` to `core_api/main.py` — deliberately unauthenticated (Docker's `HEALTHCHECK` has no way to present a real Entra token) and only reachable once ASGI `lifespan()` startup completes, so a real `200` is genuine proof `app.state.pg_client` and the rest are ready, not an assumption from the process merely existing. Excluded from the tracing middleware (would otherwise add permanent background trace noise every few seconds for the container's whole lifetime). `docker-compose.yml`: `core_api` gets a real `healthcheck:` block using `python -c "urllib.request.urlopen(...)"` — not `curl`, which the image's own Azure CLI install step purges after use, confirmed by reading the Dockerfile rather than assumed present; `bff`'s `depends_on` changed from a bare service-name list to `core_api: condition: service_healthy`, so `bff`'s container cannot start until `core_api` has answered a real `200`, not merely until its process exists.

**Verified live, a genuine cold start, not inferred from the config:** `docker compose down` (all 4 containers confirmed removed) → rebuilt `core_api`/`bff` images → `docker compose up -d`. Compose's own output shows the mechanism firing in order: `core_api Starting` → `core_api Waiting` → `core_api Healthy` → only then `bff Starting`. `docker compose ps` confirmed `core_api` `Up ... (healthy)`. A real request fired through `bff` (`GET /api/v1/programs`) immediately after `bff` finished its own startup returned a real `200` with real program data on the first attempt — no connection error, no retry, `core_api`'s own logs show `/health` answering `200` well before `bff`'s process even started.

**Bar for done, all real:** all four new assertion classes shown failing against a wrong state and passing against the real one (above). Cold `docker compose up` with no startup race, shown via compose's own ordering output and a real first-request success. `verify_migration.py`: **119/119**. Full suite: **133/133** (39/39 in `test_verify_migration.py` alone). **`reports` unchanged at 461** throughout every part of this task. `approval_records`/`findings`/`untracked_items` unchanged. **Open follow-up, not yet done:** `cycles` moved 28→29 between Task 45's own last recorded count and this task's — not caused by anything in this task's own work (confirmed: `test_cycles.py` uses transactional rollback and leaves no rows; nothing here called the trigger endpoint). Most likely benign — a leftover worker/reporting container from a prior session picking up a queued message on its own between sessions — but genuinely not confirmed, and an unexplained row is exactly the class of thing this project has been right to chase down before. Not investigated further this task; worth a direct check next time this project is worked (query the row's own `created_at`/`program_id`/`trace_context` and cross-reference against what should have produced it) rather than left resolved by assumption. **Committed and pushed on explicit request** (`630aed3..a4597b6`), after folding in three more things asked for at that point: the reverse grant-to-owner mechanic recorded in ADR-023 itself (an ownership round-trip strips a role's own pre-existing grant — the opposite direction of ADR-023's original finding), the `ALTER TABLE OWNER TO` finding stated precisely (Postgres's own refusal when the target role lacks `CREATE` makes the historical bug unreachable via that path *today* — the check guards a future reopening, not something currently exploitable), and the `cycles` 28→29 discrepancy converted from a one-off note into a proper, findable **Open follow-up, not yet done** entry (see below) rather than left resting inside this paragraph alone.
