**Real inventory checked before provisioning anything, not assumed:** existing resources in `onepulse-gr` are `centralus` except Key Vault (`eastus2`, pre-existing, cross-region reads are fine — no colocation requirement). `Microsoft.App`/`Microsoft.ContainerRegistry` were **not registered** on this subscription — the same recurring real provider-registration gap this project has hit for Storage/Search/KeyVault in earlier phases — registered and polled to `Registered` before proceeding.

**Provisioned, reported as created, in order:**
- **Postgres firewall rule** `AllowAllAzureServicesWithinAzureIps` (`0.0.0.0`-`0.0.0.0`, the real Azure sentinel meaning "any Azure-hosted caller," not scoped to just this environment). **Real, disclosed trade-off, not glossed over:** the Container Apps environment is not VNET-integrated, so there is no static/predictable egress range to scope a narrower rule to — the genuinely scoped alternative (a custom VNET + NAT Gateway with a static IP, then whitelisting just that IP) is real infrastructure this phase's own stated scope never listed (no VNET/NAT in the "what gets provisioned" bullet list) and was not built.
- **Log Analytics workspace** `onepulse-caenv-logs` (`centralus`, PerGB2018 — usage-based, no fixed cost).
- **Container Apps environment** `onepulse-caenv` (`centralus`, Consumption workload profile, no VNET), default domain `wonderfulbay-b92b8b1b.centralus.azurecontainerapps.io`.
- **Azure Container Registry** `onepulseacrdev.azurecr.io` (Basic, admin user disabled — pull is by Managed Identity `AcrPull` role assignment on all three real identities, not a static registry credential, matching convention #4). The real, recurring `az role assignment create` `MissingSubscription` client-side bug (Task 16's own precedent) recurred here too — bypassed via the same direct `az rest` PUT to the ARM `roleAssignments` API already established as the working path.
- **Four images built directly in ACR** (`az acr build`, no local `docker push`) — `onepulse-core-api`, `onepulse-bff`, `onepulse-investigation`, `onepulse-reporting`, tag `phase7` (`reporting` later rebuilt as `phase7-verify`, see below). **A real, live client-side bug found and worked around:** `az acr build`'s log-streaming crashed locally with `UnicodeEncodeError: 'charmap' codec can't encode...` (a Unicode character in the real build output this Windows shell's cp1252 console can't render) — the build itself kept running server-side regardless (confirmed via `az acr task list-runs`); worked around for the remaining three builds with `--no-wait`/`--no-logs` and polling instead of streaming.
- **Four container apps**, each with its own real user-assigned identity already provisioned in Phase 6 (`id-onepulse-app-dev` shared by `core_api`/`reporting`, `id-onepulse-investigation-dev`, `id-onepulse-bff-dev`) and `--registry-identity`/`--registry-server` pointed at the ACR (no admin credential, no static registry secret): `onepulse-core-api` (internal, target port 8000, `minReplicas 0`/`max 3`), `onepulse-investigation` (internal, target port 8200, `minReplicas 0`/`max 3`, real KEDA `azure-queue` custom scale rule on `investigation-requests` — see below), `onepulse-bff` (external, restricted — see below), `onepulse-reporting` (**no ingress at all, `minReplicas 1`/`max 1` — the real, disclosed exception the phase's own instructions anticipated finding**: `reporting`'s own outer loop is a plain Postgres `cycles`-table poll, `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, unchanged since Phase 3 — it is not triggered by an HTTP request or a queue message of its own, so nothing in the platform could ever wake it from a scaled-to-zero state; it must run continuously to ever notice a newly-queued cycle. A small, mostly-idle container, not a large one, but a real, permanent, non-zero cost — quantified below, not hand-waved).

**KEDA queue scaling on `investigation`, the one real design/CLI gap hit this phase:** the documented mechanism for identity-based (secretless) KEDA Azure Queue scaling in Container Apps (an explicit `identity` field on the scale rule) was tried at two different JSON nesting positions and two different API versions (`2024-03-01`, `2025-01-01`) and rejected both times with `Unknown properties identity in ContainerApp{Custom}ScaleRule are not supported` — a real, live-confirmed gap between this feature's documentation and what this subscription's current API actually accepts, not a syntax mistake. **The real, working fix, found empirically:** a bare `azure-queue` custom scale rule with **no** identity/auth block at all — Container Apps' own KEDA layer falls back to the container app's own already-attached user-assigned identity automatically when no explicit auth is specified, confirmed live: `investigation` correctly woke from zero and consumed a real queued message using exactly this configuration, no static connection string anywhere (the storage account itself has `allowSharedKeyAccess=false` since Phase 4 — a connection-string fallback was never on the table).

**`bff`'s ingress, done atomically so it was never public-and-unrestricted for any window, even briefly (the one config the kickoff named explicitly not to ship):** deployed first with `--ingress internal` (zero public exposure), Easy Auth configured while still internal, **then one single ARM PATCH set `external: true` and `ipSecurityRestrictions` together** — verified live via `docker compose`-style before/after query that `external` and the IP restriction landed in the same operation, never one without the other.

**A real, structural decision point surfaced and resolved, not glossed over: Container Apps' built-in Entra authentication (Easy Auth) for the `azureActiveDirectory` provider requires a real client secret — confirmed by reading the actual CLI parameter surface, no secretless/federated-identity path exists for it.** This is a genuine, disclosed exception to convention #4, reasoned through rather than silently taken: the platform performs its own server-side OAuth confidential-client exchange with Entra on behalf of a signing-in browser, which Entra requires a client credential to authorize — Managed Identity federates this project's own *outbound* service-to-service calls, not the platform's *inbound* sign-in flow, a structurally different problem than the one ADR-024 already reasoned through for Managed Identity itself. A new Entra app registration (`onepulse-bff-signin`) was created for real end-user sign-in (distinct from `onepulse-core-api`, which represents an API for service tokens, not an interactive sign-in principal); its client secret was generated once (`az ad app credential reset`) and stored only in the container app's own managed secret store, never echoed to any log or committed anywhere — the same discipline as every other real secret this project has ever handled. `--action Return401` (not `RedirectToLoginPage`) for unauthenticated requests — a browser redirect would break Streamlit's own plain HTTP calls, which remain this phase's real caller until Phase 9.

**Two real, live-discovered application bugs found and fixed while proving the deployed chain actually works, not assumed from a clean `docker compose` history:**
1. **`Cognitive Services OpenAI User` (Phase 6's own RBAC grant) covers real-time inference calls only — it does not include the Foundry project's connections-listing operation `enable_observability()` calls at startup.** Every service that calls it (`core_api`, `reporting`, `investigation`) crashed on first real deploy with `ClientAuthenticationError: (PermissionDenied) Principal does not have access to API/Operation`, `Application startup failed. Exiting.`, then crash-loop-backed-off — invisible from Phase 6's own verification, since that phase's checks ran under the shared human credential (broadly privileged), never the narrower real Managed Identity RBAC surface until this phase's own deployed containers first exercised it for real. **Fixed** by granting the real, current, resource-type-correct role — **`Foundry User`** (the role this Foundry resource's own role catalog explicitly recommends over `Azure AI Developer` for exactly this newer "Foundry project" resource type; a superset of control-plane read + data-plane access, so the older `Cognitive Services OpenAI User` grant is redundant, not conflicting, and was left in place rather than removed) — to `id-onepulse-app-dev` and `id-onepulse-investigation-dev`. **A second, real operational lesson found while confirming the fix:** a plain `az containerapp revision restart` was not sufficient — the replica was already in `CrashLoopBackOff` on its own exponential backoff schedule and kept retrying the *old* failure rather than picking up the just-granted RBAC; a genuinely fresh revision (forced via a harmless env-var bump) was needed to actually exercise the fix.
2. **A transient, non-fatal `mcp.server.mcpserver.exceptions.UnexpectedToolError: Error executing tool get_slide_text`** surfaced once during a real Status Update Analysis stage against the deployed `aiobs_status_deck.pptx` — the agent itself recovered and completed the stage correctly (`3 of 3 real tool call(s) resolved`, real untracked-initiative/possible-connection counts); reported here as a real, observed, non-blocking flake rather than silently omitted, consistent with this project's own standard for a transient tool-call hiccup that didn't actually break anything.

**Zero interactive credentials, demonstrated, not asserted:** `az containerapp exec` into `core_api` and running `az account show` fails with `Please run 'az login' to setup account` — confirmed live, and `properties.template.volumes` is empty on every app (no `azure_cli_state`-equivalent volume mounted anywhere in this deployment, unlike the local `docker-compose.yml` shape). `DefaultAzureCredential().get_token(...)` from inside the same container succeeds in **13ms** — real Managed Identity via IMDS, the thing Phase 6 could provision but never prove.

**Internal-only ingress, demonstrated by a real failed external attempt, not a config screenshot:** a real `curl` from this developer's own machine to `core_api`'s internal FQDN resolves DNS, completes a real TLS handshake against the environment's own public edge (`4.249.255.211`), and receives a genuine `404` — but the response body is Azure's own literal **"Azure Container App - Unavailable"** platform page (`content-type: text/html`), not this project's FastAPI application at all — proof the request never reached the container, contrasted directly against `bff`'s real external FQDN returning a genuine `401 Unauthorized` from its own live Easy Auth sidecar for the identical unauthenticated-request shape (a real app-level response, correctly gated) in the same test pass.

**The full authenticated chain proven end to end via direct HTTP, before the real Streamlit run:** a real Entra token for `onepulse-bff-signin`'s own `access_as_user` delegated scope (requiring, matching Phase 2's own precedent exactly, a real `identifierUris`/exposed-scope fix and a fresh `oauth2PermissionGrant` for the CLI's own service principal — the first token request returned an opaque non-JWT because no scope had ever been exposed) — validated live by Easy Auth's own sidecar logs (`numberOfSuccessfulBearerTokenAuthentications: 1`, the real decoded claims logged), then correctly proxied through to `core_api` and back with real data once the RBAC fix above was in place.

**The real bar-for-done end-to-end AOP run, triggered through Streamlit exactly as required — not a curl bypassing it:** `api_client.py` gained a small, disclosed, necessary addition (`_auth_headers()`, acquiring a real bearer token via this developer's own already-authenticated `az login` session — not a static token in `.env` — for every one of its 7 real `httpx.AsyncClient` call sites; a genuine no-op against a local, non-Easy-Auth `bff` when the new `ONEPULSE_BFF_SIGNIN_APP_ID` env var is unset, so this file works unchanged either way) — `.env`'s `ONEPULSE_BFF_BASE_URL` pointed at the real deployed FQDN. A real `streamlit.testing.v1.AppTest` run (project selectbox → real click on "Generate report") completed in **252.1 seconds with zero exceptions**, matching `reporting`'s own real log exactly: full 7-stage pipeline, `Code-enforced risk floor: PASS` on both the initial and revised draft, the one permitted revision firing correctly, `Overall status: RED`, and the correct terminal outcome `not_persisted_already_exists` (report 454 — the same real, pre-existing weekly collision every AOP run this project has hit all session).

**One connected trace across the queue hop, on real deployed compute, real captured span IDs from a live Application Insights query, not a claim:** `reporting run_cycle` (`id=58444dd90e2200e4`) is the exact `operation_ParentId` of `investigation POST investigation-requests`, both sharing the identical `operation_Id` — the same real mechanism Phase 4 proved locally, now reproduced unchanged across genuinely separate, independently-scaled Azure Container Apps instances rather than local Docker containers on one machine.

**`verify_migration.py` run from inside real deployed compute, connecting as the real `app_role` Managed Identity — not a laptop standing in for it:** `reporting`'s image gained the script (`COPY scripts/verify_migration.py`, a small, disclosed, deliberate addition solely to make this specific bar-for-done item literal rather than proxied), rebuilt as `onepulse-reporting:phase7-verify` (a strict superset of `phase7`, not redeployed until after the real E2E run completed, to avoid disrupting it mid-flight), deployed, and run via `az containerapp exec -g onepulse-gr -n onepulse-reporting --command "python scripts/verify_migration.py --target dev"`: **119/119 checks passed**, identical result to every local run this session, now genuinely produced by deployed compute.

**Real, measured cold-start numbers, not continued assumption — Migration Plan Phase 7's own explicit ask:** a real, precisely-timed (`curl -w '%{time_total}'`) first request through the full `bff` → `core_api` chain from a confirmed genuine zero-replica state on both (both Python-only images, chained — `bff`'s own cold start plus its own outbound call triggering `core_api`'s) took **55.69 seconds**. The Python-**and**-Node `investigation` image — measured via a fresh-revision creation-to-ready window rather than a second live KEDA-trigger test, given the time already invested this task — showed its own container process starting **17 seconds** after replica creation and confirmed actively polling its real queue within **50 seconds** of it: comparable to, not dramatically worse than, the two-Python-services-chained figure, despite carrying the Node runtime and its real `npm ci`'d dependency tree. **Real, concrete consequence for the Runbook's own pre-demo advice:** a demo that cannot tolerate roughly a minute's wait on the first click needs the existing "raise `minReplicas` to 1 before a demo, return it to 0 after" step (ADR-016) — now backed by a real number, not a guess.

**Row counts confirmed directly, before and after this entire task's real deployment testing:** `reports` unchanged at 461 (the one real E2E trigger correctly hit the existing weekly collision, zero new rows); `findings`/`untracked_items`/`approval_records` unchanged (939/104/279); `cycles` moved 29→30, exactly one new row for the one real trigger performed — not a leak, that table has no DELETE grant by design.

**Real cost, calculated from the live Azure Retail Prices API for `centralus` (not recalled from memory or marketing pages), reported as a genuine financial consequence, not discovered later on a bill:**
- **Azure Container Registry, Basic:** $0.1666/day × 30 ≈ **$5.00/month** (plus negligible real image-storage cost, a few images well under 2GB total) — matches the Migration Plan's own pre-existing cross-cutting estimate exactly.
- **Log Analytics:** $2.76/GB ingested, pay-as-you-go, no fixed cost — real volume for a low-traffic dev/demo system's console-log output is genuinely unknown without a real billing cycle; estimated at a few dollars/month, flagged as the one real unknown in this report rather than forced to a number.
- **Container Apps Consumption — the real, new, ongoing cost driver, and the one number most worth reading carefully:** Consumption pricing distinguishes **active** vCPU usage ($0.000024/vCPU-second) from **idle** vCPU usage ($0.000003/vCPU-second, 8x cheaper) — memory is the same rate either way ($0.000003/GiB-second). `reporting`'s forced `minReplicas: 1`, running near-continuously idle (a polling loop, not constant real work), computes to roughly **$21.71/month** net of the subscription's own free monthly grant (180,000 vCPU-seconds/360,000 GiB-seconds) — not the ~$72/month a first, cruder calculation using only the active rate would have implied; getting the idle/active distinction right mattered directly to the number reported here. `core_api`/`bff`/`investigation` all still scale to zero and cost only for real active-request windows — expected to stay small for this system's real usage pattern, largely absorbed by whatever free-grant headroom `reporting`'s own idle baseline doesn't already consume.
- **Real, approximate total new monthly run rate this phase adds: roughly $30-40 USD/month** (dominated by `reporting`'s forced-always-on footprint, ≈$22, plus ACR ≈$5, plus Log Analytics and the other three services' real-but-small active usage, ≈$5-10) — **against the ~CA$35 baseline named in the kickoff, this is a real, meaningful new addition, not a rounding error, and it is driven almost entirely by the one `minReplicas: 1` exception this phase's own instructions anticipated needing.** Recommend checking Azure Cost Management after a real 30-day billing cycle for ground truth rather than treating this calculation as final; a follow-up rightsizing of `reporting`'s CPU/memory (it is a lightweight poller, not compute-bound) is a real, low-risk way to reduce this further and was not done this task, given the time already spent — flagged as a real, available next step, not silently left undiscoverable.

**Full suite: 133/133 passing** (only `api_client.py`, `reporting/Dockerfile`, and the Runbook changed locally — confirmed via `git status`; no `onepulse_common` logic touched). **Runbook updated** (`docs/build-journey/07_Runbook.md`): a new §7 covering how to deploy (real `az acr build`/`containerapp update` commands, no IaC yet — a real, disclosed scope gap, not an oversight), how to reach it as Streamlit, and how to warm it before a demo with the real measured numbers above; §6 gained three new gotchas (the `Cognitive Services OpenAI User`-vs-`Foundry User` gap and its crash-loop-backoff trap, the Easy-Auth-requires-a-real-secret structural finding, both written up in full). **Not done, deliberately: not merged to main, not pushed** — reported here as complete against the stated bar, merging follows the same discipline as every prior phase once explicitly requested. |

**Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-10 — four things requested before the merge: the cold-start guidance strengthened, a new ADR for the Easy Auth secret, a fourth Governance Reference privilege-assumption instance, and a report (not a fix) on how far `reporting` is from being queue-driven.**

**1. Runbook §7's warm-up guidance rewritten from a conditional nicety to a required step**, opening with the real consequence ("a real stakeholder watches a blank screen for the better part of a minute") ahead of the mechanism, and correcting this project's own repeated prior assumption in the same edit: the Python-and-Node `investigation` image is not "the slow one" — its real measured cold-start (ready within 50s of replica creation) is comparable to, not dramatically worse than, the two-Python-services-chained 55.7s figure, despite carrying the Node runtime. Cross-referenced against §2's own, still-correct "real, expected asymmetry" note (image *size*, not startup time) so the two don't get conflated on a future read.

**2. ADR-025 added**, giving the `bff` Easy Auth client secret the same weight ADR-024 gave the ADO PAT rejection, not a disclosure footnote: states plainly why Managed Identity structurally cannot substitute here (it federates this project's own outbound calls; Easy Auth's inbound OAuth exchange has no "this project's own service" identity on either side of it), explicitly does not reopen or weaken ADR-024's own SP-secret rejection (accepting one exception where no live alternative exists is consistent with, not in tension with, rejecting one where Managed Identity already does the job), and states what would change if it ever became avoidable (a certificate-based provider, deferred not rejected). ADR-018 gained a short cross-reference to it. **Governance Reference §1/§4 restructured**: §1's count updated from "one explicitly-tracked, time-boxed exception" to two — one time-boxed (ADO PAT), one permanent by the nature of the mechanism (Easy Auth secret). §4 split into §4a (ADO PAT, original content preserved verbatim) and §4b (the Easy Auth secret, same four-part structure: what it is / why unavoidable / what bounds it / what would change / current status).

**3. Governance Reference §6 gained a fourth privilege-assumption instance**, alongside the three ownership-bypass cases already on record: `Cognitive Services OpenAI User` covering real-time inference but not the Foundry project's own connections-listing control-plane call `enable_observability()` makes at startup — explicitly distinguished from instances 1-3 ("not an ownership bug... the identical *shape* of failure one layer up") and stating plainly what made it non-obvious: the crash-loop came from this project's own dual-telemetry setup at process startup, ahead of any real request, not from anything in the actual investigation/reporting workload the RBAC grant was designed around.

**4. Item 4, report only, not implemented — read `reporting/main.py` and `core_api/main.py`/`cycles.py`'s trigger path directly before answering, not assumed from the architecture description above.** `reporting`'s outer loop (`main()`) is a plain `while True` calling `claim_next_queued_cycle` (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` against the `cycles` table) on a 2-second `asyncio.sleep` when idle — nothing queue-shaped triggers it; its own, separate `findings-ready` queue consumption is real but only fires reactively, mid-cycle, while awaiting a specific already-known `cycle_id` a cycle it already claimed is waiting on. `core_api`'s trigger endpoint (`POST /api/v1/programs/{programId}/reports` → `cycles.create_cycle`) does a plain `INSERT ... status='queued'` and returns — no message is published anywhere today.

**Assessed as a small, bounded change, not a real redesign — reusing already-proven pieces rather than inventing new ones:** `onepulse_common/queues.py` (`get_queue_client`/`send_json_message`/`parse_message`/`is_poison`/`deadletter`, `MAX_DEQUEUE_COUNT`) is already generic, built for Investigation but with zero Investigation-specific code in it — reusable as-is for a new `report-cycles` queue. `investigation/main.py`'s own queue-consumer loop (receive → dispatch → lease-renewal → delete-on-success, Phase 4) is the exact shape `reporting/main.py`'s outer loop would need to become instead of its DB poll — a pattern to copy, not one to invent. The KEDA `azure-queue` custom scale rule already proven on `investigation` (no identity block, falls back to the container's own attached MI) applies to `reporting` unchanged. Concretely: `core_api`'s trigger endpoint gains one `send_json_message` call after the existing INSERT (carrying `cycle_id`, `program_name`, `requested_by_actor_id`, a fresh `traceparent` — the identical envelope shape `_dispatch_investigation_request` already uses); `reporting/main.py`'s `while True: claim_next_queued_cycle(); sleep(2)` is replaced with a `receive_messages` loop matching Investigation's; `claim_next_queued_cycle`'s `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` claim semantics become unnecessary (the queue's own visibility timeout is the concurrency guard, the same role it already plays for Investigation) but the row itself stays, since `cycles` is still the real status table Phase 3 built.

**The one real property worth deciding on deliberately, not a blocker:** a redelivered `report-cycles` message (worker killed mid-run, same Task 42/43 kill-test shape) would re-run the whole cycle from scratch, including re-dispatching Investigation — safe, not wasteful, because Investigation's own `investigation_runs` store is already idempotent by `cycle_id` (`ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`, Phase 4) and `persist_report`'s `UNIQUE(program_id, week_of)` backstops against double-persisting — but it does mean a crash after Investigation was real work (a real Foundry/ADO cost) but before persistence would pay that cost again on redelivery. This is the same accepted trade-off Investigation's own design already made for itself (Phase 4's kill test explicitly proved and welcomed this property), not a new risk category — but worth naming plainly since it wasn't asked to be evaluated, only reported. Rightsizing `reporting`'s CPU/memory (flagged as available but not done in the main Task 47 entry) is a smaller, independent lever that doesn't require this redesign and could be done first or instead.

**Conclusion, stated as asked: a small change, not a real one** — mechanically it is "copy Investigation's already-proven consumer-loop pattern onto reporting, add one queue, add one publish call," with no new library, no new Azure resource type, and no unresolved design gap; the one thing genuinely worth a deliberate decision is accepting that a killed run gets fully redone (including its real Foundry/ADO cost) rather than resumed — not whether the mechanism itself is buildable. Not implemented, per instruction — this is the report the user asked to decide from.

**Full suite re-run after items 1-3's documentation-only changes: 133/133 passing, unchanged** (`git status` confirms only `01_Architecture_Decision_Record.md`, `07_Runbook.md`, `08_Governance_and_Security_Reference.md` — no application code touched by any of items 1-4). |

Record it as an ADR. Bar for done: `reporting` at `minReplicas: 0` with KEDA queue-depth scaling, deployed and confirmed; a real full-scope AOP run end to end on deployed compute through the new path; the kill test on `reporting` (Phase 4 proved this for Investigation, never for Reporting); redelivery proven — not asserted — to skip re-dispatching Investigation when already complete; cold start measured now that `reporting` starts from zero, Runbook updated if warming changes; revised cost figure against the ~CA$35 baseline; full suite and `verify_migration.py` green | **Done 2026-09-10/11.** Implementation matches the small, bounded shape Task 47 predicted — copied Investigation's already-proven consumer-loop pattern (lease renewal at 45s/90s, poison dead-lettering, delete-only-after-terminal), no new library, no new Azure resource type. **The resume design, the one real piece of new thinking, as ADR-026 records it:** rather than adding a second "was Investigation dispatched" flag to `cycles` (rejected — a second source of truth that could drift from Investigation's own real record, the same class of risk this project's Governance Reference has repeatedly found and corrected elsewhere), `reporting` asks Investigation directly, every time, via the same real `GET /internal/investigations/{cycle_id}` route it already uses to fetch results (a 404 means dispatch; a real body means resume) — Investigation's own `investigation_runs` table was already keyed on `cycle_id` with `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` (Phase 4), so this needed exactly one new function (`_resolve_investigation_result`), not a second tracking mechanism. `libs/onepulse_common/onepulse_common/queues.py` gained `REPORT_CYCLES_QUEUE`/`REPORT_CYCLES_POISON_QUEUE`; `cycles.py`'s `claim_next_queued_cycle` (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`) was retired outright and replaced with `get_cycle_for_execution` (idempotent across redelivery, preserves `started_at`) — the queue's own visibility-timeout lease is now the concurrency control, same as it already is for Investigation. `core_api/main.py`'s trigger endpoint publishes a thin `{cycle_id}` message right after its existing `cycles` INSERT. Full local test suite: 134/134 (was 133 — `claim_next_queued_cycle`'s two tests replaced with three covering `get_cycle_for_execution`, including the real idempotency-across-redelivery case). **Real deployment, KEDA scale rule, two real bugs found and fixed, both disclosed in ADR-026:** (1) `az containerapp update --scale-rule-*` CLI flags produce a stray, present-but-empty `"auth": []` — unlike an absent key, this breaks Managed-Identity auto-fallback; fixed via a direct ARM PATCH omitting `auth` entirely, matching Investigation's own proven shape byte-for-byte. (2) A more serious, real, live-discovered platform issue: even with the ARM shape corrected, `reporting` stayed pinned at 1 replica for hours — **checked, not assumed, against the alternative explanation the user explicitly raised (legitimately busy, not stuck):** `report-cycles`/`investigation-requests` both confirmed genuinely empty (`approximate_message_count=0`, a count that includes invisible/leased messages) and Investigation's own live SDK-level HTTP logs confirmed it performing a real empty poll at the exact moment checked — direct proof of idleness. Investigation's own already-proven scale rule was independently confirmed showing the identical intermittent `KEDAScalerFailed: error parsing azure queue metadata: no connection setting given` on the same real cadence — a genuine, disclosed Azure Container Apps platform flake in the KEDA-to-Managed-Identity resolution path, affecting both queue-scaled services equally, not a defect in this task's config (both independently confirmed correct). **With that confirmed rather than assumed, work proceeded on everything not gated by it, per the user's explicit direction (Option 1 of an `AskUserQuestion`):** a real full-scope AOP run (115 items/7 features) triggered through the deployed `bff`, completing end to end through the new path (`core_api`→`report-cycles`→`reporting`→`investigation-requests`→`investigation`→`findings-ready`→`reporting`→render/persist) in 2m59s, correctly hitting the pre-existing weekly collision (`not_persisted_already_exists`, report 454). **The kill test took five real attempts to find a working hard-kill mechanism for this platform, each failure itself a real, disclosed finding:** `az containerapp exec --command "kill -9 1"` doesn't reach the real app process (the target cycle completed uninterrupted; a follow-up harmless `ps aux` via the same mechanism produced the identical generic disconnection error, proving the error is exec-session teardown noise, not evidence of a real kill); `az containerapp revision restart` performs a rolling restart, not a hard kill (a new replica spins up alongside the untouched original, which keeps running); `--max-replicas 0` is rejected outright by the CLI (`[1,1000]` only). **The real, working mechanism: `az containerapp revision deactivate`/`activate`** — confirmed live to tear every replica down within ~15-20s and bring them back under normal KEDA control. The fifth real attempt (tighter 8s polling, deactivating the instant Stage 1 showed done) caught the cycle genuinely mid-flight during Self-critique's own revision call — confirmed via the cycle's own status (`running`, `finishedAt: null`, live stage-5 note `"revising for tone"`) and zero replicas, not assumed from the deactivate call merely succeeding. The `report-cycles` message was confirmed still present and invisible; after reactivating, its lease expired and it was redelivered to a freshly claimed replica, reaching a real terminal state ~3.5 minutes later. **Redelivery proven — shown, not asserted — not to re-dispatch Investigation, two independent ways:** reporting's own log shows the literal resume-path text (`"Investigation — already completed for this cycle, resuming ... (no re-dispatch)"`) with Stage 1 taking a real, measured **0.15 seconds** on redelivery versus 85-110s for every fresh dispatch this session; Investigation's own independent log for the identical `cycle_id` shows exactly ONE `received investigation request` line and exactly ONE `cycle ... completed: 115 finding(s)` line despite two separate reporting execution attempts, with two `GET /internal/investigations/{cycle_id}` calls (200 OK each) — one per attempt, the second being the observed resume check. **Cold start measured via a disclosed proxy** (the same `revision deactivate`/`activate` mechanism, since KEDA's own scale-from-zero stayed blocked by the platform flake): a genuinely fresh replica (confirmed by pod name) claimed a real message **~21.6 seconds** after its own creation (~27.4s from message-publish) — faster than either of Phase 7's own `bff`→`core_api` (55.7s) or `investigation` (~50s) figures, despite carrying the bulk of the real business logic. Runbook §7 updated: `reporting`'s own measured cold start recorded, and its warm-up need stated as a real, different action from the other three services (a throwaway report-cycle trigger, not a `curl`, since nothing HTTP-shaped reaches a queue-driven service). **Revised cost, same Azure Retail Prices methodology as Task 47:** removing the ~$21.71/month forced-idle line item (the dominant term in Phase 7's ~$30-40/month total) brings the revised estimate to roughly **$10-18/month** against the ~CA$35 baseline — with an explicit, honest caveat that this assumes the KEDA scaler eventually behaves the way Investigation's own historical pattern shows it can; for as long as the platform flake keeps a queue-scaled app pinned at 1 replica, it bills at the old always-on rate regardless of correct `minReplicas: 0` configuration. **Full suite: 134/134** (5 tests showed transient `AzureCliCredential`-related errors under this session's own heavy concurrent `az` CLI load during the kill-test attempts — confirmed non-reproducing on an immediate individual re-run, the same class of environment noise already documented in Task 45). **`verify_migration.py`: 119/119.** **Row counts confirmed directly:** `reports` unchanged at 461/`max_report_id` 532 (every real trigger this task correctly hit an existing weekly collision, zero new rows) — `cycles` grew by the real number of cycles this task's own testing triggered (7 real triggers: the full-scope run, 4 completed-before-teardown kill attempts, the successful kill test, and the cold-start test), not a leak, that table has no DELETE grant by design. **Open follow-up, not yet done (as of 2026-09-11): `reporting` has not been directly, visually confirmed reaching a KEDA-triggered zero-replica state.** Its configuration is confirmed correct (byte-for-byte matching Investigation's own proven rule, independently re-verified via direct ARM query), and the blocking mechanism was checked against the real alternative explanation (legitimately busy, not stuck — queue depths and Investigation's own live poll logs confirmed genuine idleness) before concluding it's a real, external, intermittent Azure platform flake affecting both queue-scaled services identically. That conclusion is accepted, but deliberately kept open rather than settled here: "config is correct and the platform isn't cooperating" is also what this would look like if something subtler were wrong on this project's own side, and the difference is only resolved by actually observing `reporting` reach zero replicas under normal KEDA control, not by the strength of the evidence ruling out one alternative. **Concrete next check, not a vague "revisit later":** run `az containerapp replica list -g onepulse-gr -n onepulse-reporting --query "length([])" -o tsv` (and the same for `onepulse-investigation`) next time this project is worked, with no manual `revision deactivate`/`activate` in between — if either shows `0` under normal operation, the flake has cleared and this closes; if both are still pinned at `1` after a similarly extended, undisturbed observation window, that itself is worth a second look rather than continuing to attribute it to intermittent flakiness indefinitely. **Direct consequence for the revised cost figure: the ~$10-18/month estimate above is a projection, not an observed result** — it holds only once `reporting` is actually confirmed reaching zero replicas in normal operation; until then, real spend could still track closer to Phase 7's original ~$30-40/month if `reporting` stays effectively pinned at 1 replica by this same platform behavior. Full ADR-026 write-up, including all five kill-test attempts and the platform-flake investigation trail, in `docs/build-journey/01_Architecture_Decision_Record.md`. |

| Task 49: Migration Phase 8 — real reviewer identity, the Owner/Visitor role model, and RLS finally enforcing | See `docs/build-journey/12_Migration_Plan.md` Phase 8 and ADR-018. Branch `phase8/identity-and-roles`, off merged `main` — this one goes on a branch, not direct to `main`, since it ends by opening public ingress and a half-finished state on `main` during that is the position not wanted. The phase the whole migration has been building toward — everything before it moved code; this one makes the system usable by someone other than the operator. **First, seed a real second tenant** (pure Postgres — tenants/portfolios/programs/actor_scope, a few seeded reports; no second ADO project) — with one tenant, a policy that filters correctly and one that silently matches everything produce identical results, and this project has already found four guarantees designed correctly, documented confidently, never exercised; a fifth via single-tenant "verification" was explicitly to be avoided. **Then identity:** `get_current_actor()` as a real FastAPI dependency on every `core_api` route, resolving role and scope from the database, never accepting `actor_id` from the client, **never cached** — revoking an `actor_scope` row must take effect on the very next request. **The no-actors-row path is the common case:** a clean 403, not a crash. **RLS, finally enforcing:** `SET LOCAL app.current_tenant_id` (FORCE already on since pre-Phase-6) in `core_api` and `reporting` only, proven against the real second tenant, not one convenient row. **Roles, enforced entirely in `core_api`, no Streamlit UI change:** Owner (generate/approve/see everything in scope) vs Visitor (view/chat within scope, no generate) — a hidden button is usability, not security. **FR-11's rate limit**, buildable now because Phase 4 already threads the requesting actor through the queue envelope; say whether NFR-6's usage ledger is built too. Bar for done, all real: a visitor scoped to tenant A cannot see tenant B's reports through the report list, the chat assistant's retrieval filter (matters most — filtering after retrieval means the model already saw it), and a SAS request; an authenticated user with no `actors` row gets a clean 403; a visitor cannot trigger a run; forging a different `actor_id` is rejected, shown as a real request and a real error; the rate limit enforced, demonstrated by exceeding it; full suite and `verify_migration.py` green, deployed environment still working. **Opening public ingress is the last step, only after everything above passes, reported as a distinct action with its own confirmation.** | **Done 2026-09-10/11.** Full design/reasoning/every finding in ADR-027 — summary here. **Seeding, `scripts/seed_phase8_test_data.py`:** a real fictional second tenant ("Meridian Health"), its own portfolio/program, two real reports with real findings, a real Tenant-B Owner actor scoped to it, plus a real Tenant-A Visitor actor — and, a real gap found while writing this, a backfilled `actor_scope` row for the pre-existing Tenant-A stand-in reviewer, which had none at all before this task (would have 403'd under the new dependency). **`get_current_actor()`** (`core_api/security.py`): resolves `actors.entra_object_id` → role, then the real tenant via `actor_scope` → `portfolio_id`/`program_id` → `portfolios.tenant_id` — the IDENTICAL join `tenant_isolation`'s own policy performs, deliberately not `actors.tenant_id` directly, so the two can never structurally disagree — plus the real set of authorized `program_id`s (the program-granular half of scope tenant-only RLS can't express). Resolved fresh every request via its own short-lived pool acquisition, no caching of any kind. **RLS wiring, six functions:** `approve_report`/`reject_report`/`persist_report` already transactional, just gained the tenant-set call; `get_report_detail`/`list_pending_reviews`/`list_recent_reports` were bare reads, now wrapped in a real transaction (`list_recent_reports` changed from opening its own connection to taking `conn`/`tenant_id`/`authorized_program_ids`, its only real caller being `core_api`'s own route). `persist_report` resolves its own tenant directly from `program_id` — `reporting` is a queue consumer with no actor of its own, and "which tenant owns this program" doesn't need one. **Two real bugs found live, both fixed:** (1) `SET LOCAL x = $1` is not valid SQL over a bind parameter at all (`asyncpg` raises `PostgresSyntaxError` on the first real call) — fixed everywhere via `SELECT set_config('app.current_tenant_id', $1, true)`. (2) A more serious, more consequential one, found only because the seed script was the first real workload to call `set_config` twice on the same pooled connection: `current_setting(x, true)` returns real `NULL` only the very first time a session ever references a custom GUC — after that, even once the transaction that set it commits and the LOCAL value reverts, it returns `''`, not `NULL`. Migration 0005's own permissive-when-unset fix (`IS NULL`) was therefore only correct for a connection's first tenant-scoped query ever — every real deployed connection pool (`min_size`/`max_size` > 1, i.e. every one of `core_api`'s/`reporting`'s real processes) would eventually hit `''::uuid` and error instead of falling back permissively. Fixed in **migration 0008**: `NULLIF(current_setting(...), '') IS NULL`, both places the check appears. **Roles:** `actors.role`'s CHECK widened (migration 0007) to add `'owner'`/`'visitor'` alongside the three legacy values — no new column; `onepulse_common/roles.py`'s `is_owner_role` treats every legacy value as Owner-tier (an exclusion check, `role != 'visitor'`), so no historical row needed touching. `_require_owner` gates exactly two routes (trigger, approve/reject) and nowhere else. **The real SAS/Blob mechanism, built now because this phase's bar required it — ADR-021 designed this in Phase 3/4 planning, neither Phase 4 nor Phase 7 ever built it:** new `onepulse_common/blob_storage.py` (`upload_report_blob`/`issue_download_sas`, real user-delegation SAS — signed by Managed Identity, never an account key), a new `reports` blob container on the existing `onepulsequeuesdev` storage account, `Storage Blob Data Contributor`+`Storage Blob Delegator` RBAC granted to `id-onepulse-app-dev` (and, for local testing, the admin identity) via the established `az rest` PUT bypass for the recurring `az role assignment create` `MissingSubscription` bug. New `GET /api/v1/reports/{reportId}/download` — authorization (RLS + program-membership) fully before issuance, matching ADR-021's own stated constraint; a report with no real `blob://` URI (every one rendered before this phase) gets a real, honest 404, not a broken link. Deliberately NOT a full pipeline migration onto Blob Storage — every existing `file://` report is untouched. **The real, mandatory chat retrieval filter LLD 2.3/ADR-022 always required, finally built:** `hybrid_search` gained `authorized_program_ids` (`search.in(program_id, ...)` OData), passed unconditionally from the chat route's own `get_current_actor` resolution. **Rate limit:** a plain count against `cycles.requested_by_actor_id`/`created_at`, rolling 24h window, 2/day, applied to every Owner-tier actor (not narrowed to the literal legacy `portfolio_lead` value — a real, avoided gap). `usage_ledger` (NFR-6, real cost-based governance) stays real, installed, and deliberately unbuilt — a materially larger, separate concern this rate limit doesn't need. **Every bar item proven live, with real requests, both directions where a positive control matters:** no-actors-row → real `403 {"error":"no_access",...}` (corrected from the prior code's 401 — the caller IS authenticated, just not authorized). Actor-id forgery → attempted via an extra `actorId` field in the real `reject` body (`RejectRequest` gained `model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")`) → real `422 extra_forbidden`, not silently dropped. Visitor triggering → real `403 visitor_cannot_generate_or_approve`. **Cross-tenant isolation, three separate real proofs, each with both a negative and a positive control:** report list (Tenant-A Visitor's own 100-row listing never contains a Meridian row; explicitly requesting Meridian's `programId` → real 404; Tenant-B Owner correctly sees both real Meridian reports). Chat retrieval filter — **the one that matters most, proven the strongest way available:** the identical question asked as Tenant-A Visitor got "I could not find any mention of a Meridian patient-records migration... unrelated singleSlide items only" (zero citations — the model never saw the content at all, not a post-hoc filter), asked as Tenant-B Owner got a fully grounded, correctly cited answer from the real seeded findings. SAS download — 404 for Tenant-A Visitor against Tenant-B's report 997; a real, working SAS URL for Tenant-B Owner against the same report, independently confirmed by fetching the real blob content through it directly (no `core_api` involved) via a plain `curl`. Rate limit — two real triggers `202`, a real third within the same 24h window `429 rate_limit_exceeded`. **The provisioning path, written down (Runbook §8) and exercised for real, not just asserted:** this session's own real signed-in Entra object id (`20f87799-...`, decoded from a real `az account get-access-token` JWT) had no `actors` row at all — a real request through the DEPLOYED `bff`, behind real Container Apps Easy Auth (not a synthetic header), correctly got the real `403 no_access` — then a real `actors`+`actor_scope` row was inserted, and the identical identity's next real request through the same deployed `bff` succeeded (`200`, real program list). **`bff/main.py` gained real Easy Auth identity extraction** (`_resolve_entra_object_id`, decoding the real `X-MS-CLIENT-PRINCIPAL` header Container Apps injects, falling back to the local-dev stub only when that header is genuinely absent) — this is what made the provisioning proof above possible against real deployed compute, not a simulation. **Deployed, verified working:** `core_api`/`bff`/`reporting` images rebuilt (`:phase8`) and redeployed; a real full local + real deployed smoke test (chat query, reports list, a real trigger reaching `running` status) both succeeded post-deploy. **Full suite: 147/147** (was 134; +11 `tests/test_phase8_identity_and_roles.py`, +2 `tests/test_verify_migration.py` for the migration-0008 policy-text check). **`verify_migration.py`: 120/120** (was 119; +1, `check_policy_definition_contains` proving the real 0008 `NULLIF` fix is present, not merely that a policy named `tenant_isolation` exists — the latter would have passed identically for the live-broken 0005 shape). **Row counts, reported honestly, not asserted as unchanged (this task's own bar required new second-tenant data, unlike every prior phase):** `reports` 461→464 (997/998 real Meridian, 999 a real Tenant-A SAS-test report — all three deliberate, attributable, disclosed); `tenants` 1→2; `actors` 1→4; `actor_scope` 0→4; `cycles` 38→41 (the real rate-limit-test triggers). Runbook updated: §6 gained both real `SET LOCAL`/GUC gotchas; new §8 covers the real provisioning recipe, `seed_phase8_test_data.py`, and how to test as a specific identity with or without a browser. |

**Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-11, before ingress — two real asks, one of which overturned the session's own first hypothesis.** User approved the fixture-row cleanup (real schema column, not text matching, plus deleting whatever's cleanly deletable) and separately asked whether the admin identity's newly-discovered ability to delete from `approval_records` — surfaced while scoping that cleanup — had decayed the append-only guarantee since ADR-023's ownership transfer, with an explicit instruction: make `verify_migration.py` assert it, demonstrate the assertion failing first, then bring fix options before applying anything.

**The causal hypothesis given to the user in the moment was wrong, and was corrected before anything went into permanent documentation.** The plausible-sounding story — `app_role` now owns `approval_records` (ADR-023), the admin is a member of `app_role`, therefore inherited DELETE — was checked directly and disproven: `has_table_privilege('app_role', 'approval_records', 'DELETE')` is `false`; the explicit `REVOKE` genuinely holds for both application roles, unchanged. The real mechanism, found by checking the admin's own role name directly: `azure_pg_admin` (every Entra Administrator on this Postgres server, including this project's own operator) is a member of PostgreSQL's built-in `pg_write_all_data` role, which grants `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE`/`TRUNCATE` on every table in every schema unconditionally — a privilege that exists via role membership, never as a `pg_class.relacl`/`information_schema.role_table_grants` row, confirmed live: a direct `REVOKE DELETE, UPDATE ON approval_records FROM azure_pg_admin` runs with no error (nothing to revoke) and changes nothing. This most likely predates this project's own schema entirely — a property of the Postgres server since provisioning, not something ADR-023 or any other decision here caused. **The user was told the wrong story first and the correction is recorded plainly, not quietly folded in** — full account in ADR-028.

**Why three prior adversarial tests of this exact guarantee (Task 31) never found it:** every one of them authenticated as `app_role_local_dev` (the correct, established discipline for testing what the *application* can do) or read the ACL layer directly. None tested the raw administrator identity. The guarantee is real and rigorously proven for the identity it was built to constrain; it was never tested against a different identity that bypasses it by an unrelated, structural mechanism.

**`verify_migration.py` now asserts it directly and honestly fails, exactly as instructed — not smoothed over.** A new `check_real_privilege_denied` (using `has_table_privilege`, which sees ACL, ownership, *and* predefined-role membership) is asserted against `azure_pg_admin` on `approval_records`; `check_privilege_revoked` (the pre-existing check, `information_schema`-based) is kept for what it's still correct about — the ACL layer — with its own docstring now naming the exact blind spot. Demonstrated live: `120/121` before this follow-up's other checks, `121/122` after adding the two new column/policy checks from the fixture work, with the one new `azure_pg_admin` check the sole, expected, disclosed failure. Three new tests (`tests/test_verify_migration.py`): the real pass for `app_role_local_dev`, the real (currently failing, honestly asserted) case for `azure_pg_admin`, and a control proving the function can report a genuine pass elsewhere, not hardcoded.

**Real fix options investigated, not applied — a decision to bring back, per instruction.** `pg_write_all_data`'s grant is confirmed un-revokable at the per-table level (tested directly, see above). Four real options recorded in ADR-028: accept and precisely re-scope the guarantee's own claim (recommended candidate, not yet chosen); a `BEFORE DELETE OR UPDATE` trigger, which — checked, not assumed — fires for every role including `pg_write_all_data` members and cannot be disabled by `azure_pg_admin` without table ownership or superuser (neither of which it has), making it a real, ACL-independent enforcement layer; reducing `azure_pg_admin` membership (mitigates blast radius, doesn't close the structural gap); real-time auditing (`pgaudit`, not installed, heavier lift). Governance & Security Reference §2's own unqualified "has survived... and held" claim corrected to state precisely which identity class the guarantee covers and which it structurally cannot.

**The fixture-row cleanup, both approved options plus one more found along the way.** A real `reports.is_test_fixture BOOLEAN` column (migration 0009) — not a text match — backfilled for the exact 440 rows matching `test_human_governance.py`'s own historical marker; `list_recent_reports`, `list_pending_reviews`, and `ingest_reports_to_search.py`'s two fetch functions all now exclude it unconditionally, closing the same real ingestion gap Task 39/40 already flagged, in the same pass. **Checking for anything the exact-text match would miss (rather than declaring done) found two more**, both self-describing as test artifacts in their own `executive_summary` (`report_id=21`, `'CLI end-to-end test report'`; `report_id=95`, `'Fixture row for testing the new single-page Reject popover flow (Task 19)'`) — plus this same session's own `report_id=999` (the Phase 8 SAS positive-control test report), the identical category of "not something a visitor should see as real output." Migration 0010 marks all three explicitly by `report_id`, deliberately not by a date-range heuristic (a genuinely real future-dated report, like the already-existing `report_id=532` at a real future Monday, must stay visible). Verified live: `list_recent_reports` now returns exactly 19 rows, every one a real, plausible 2026-dated report — confirmed clean by direct inspection of the full result set, not sampled. **Option 1 (deleting the 179 rows with no `approval_records` reference) hit a real, new privilege wall, disclosed rather than pushed through:** a zero-match `DELETE FROM reports` succeeds for the admin identity, but a real, non-empty delete fails with `permission denied for table untracked_items` — isolated to a single real row, confirmed independent of scale. Root cause: `pg_write_all_data` grants `DELETE` but not `REFERENCES`/`TRIGGER`, and Postgres's real FK-integrity check (verifying no `findings`/`untracked_items` row references the `reports` row being deleted) needs something from that category once a match is genuinely possible — confirmed via `has_table_privilege('azure_pg_admin', 'untracked_items', 'REFERENCES') = false`. **Deliberately not resolved by granting more privilege**, given the whole point of this same follow-up was scrutinizing admin privilege scope, not expanding it further to push through a tidiness task — reported as a real, open blocker instead. Net effect: the 261 always-stuck rows plus these same 179 (440 total, plus the 3 individually-marked ones) remain in the table but are now completely absent from anything a visitor or the RAG index would ever see — the actual, stated problem is fully solved; only the literal row-count reduction (a "nice to have," not the real ask) is deferred.

Migrations 0009/0010 both required the same real, already-documented workaround (Task 41's own gotcha): any `UPDATE` re-validates every `CHECK` constraint regardless of which column changed, so both drop and re-add `reports_week_of_is_monday` (`NOT VALID`) around their own backfill rather than fail on the fixture rows' real non-Monday dates. Full suite: **150/150** (was 147; +3 for `check_real_privilege_denied`). `verify_migration.py`: **121/122** (was 120; +2 new checks, `reports.is_test_fixture` column exists and the 0008 policy-text check already counted — the `azure_pg_admin` failure is the one honest, expected, documented exception). `reports` row count unchanged at 464 (the fixture rows were reclassified, not deleted, per the disclosed Option 1 blocker). **Not done, deliberately, per explicit instruction: no fix applied to the `approval_records` gap, and the IP restriction on `bff` stays in place** — both await the user's decision.

**Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-11 — the trigger built, adversarially verified, and the framing corrected a second, more precise time.** User chose Option 2 (the trigger) over Option 1 (re-scoping the guarantee's own documented claim): "it closes the gap rather than describing it."

**A real, live-discovered second consequence of ADR-023's own "REVOKE ALL then re-GRANT exact intended profile" fix, found while building this:** `app_role` — the real, current owner of `approval_records` — does not itself hold `TRIGGER` privilege on its own table (`has_table_privilege('app_role', ..., 'TRIGGER')` is `false`, since ADR-023's REVOKE ALL stripped even the owner's own default at-creation grant of it). `CREATE TRIGGER` genuinely needs that ACL bit; ownership alone wasn't sufficient. Resolved with the minimal-footprint form, inside the same migration: `SET ROLE app_role` (session-level, `gopi` is a real, confirmed member — no interactive AAD auth needed), `GRANT TRIGGER` → create the trigger → `REVOKE TRIGGER` again — `app_role`'s own documented, minimal profile is unchanged before and after; only the trigger's own existence persists. Migration `0011`: `reject_approval_records_mutation()` (a trivial `plpgsql` function that unconditionally `RAISE EXCEPTION`s, naming the real operation and calling role) as `approval_records_append_only`, `BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE ... FOR EACH ROW`.

**Adversarial verification, the same rigor Task 31 applied to the application roles, now applied to the one identity never covered — with one real, disclosed methodology mistake caught before drawing any conclusion:** the first test attempt used a deliberately nonexistent `report_id` as a "safe" probe — `FOR EACH ROW` triggers don't fire on zero matched rows, so that attempt vacuously "succeeded" and proved nothing. Redone against a real, existing `approval_records` row: connected as `gopi@gopdhagmail.onmicrosoft.com`, a real `UPDATE` and a real `DELETE` both raised `approval_records is append-only: {OP} is not permitted (role=gopi@...)`, and the real row was independently re-queried and confirmed byte-for-byte unchanged. Confirmed the trigger doesn't interfere with legitimate use: the full `test_human_governance.py` suite (real `approve_report`/`reject_report`, both `INSERT` into `approval_records`) re-run unchanged and passing — the trigger is scoped to `UPDATE`/`DELETE` only. Both proofs are now permanent tests, not one-off checks: a new `admin_conn` fixture + `test_approval_records_append_only_holds_against_the_real_admin_identity` in `test_human_governance.py`, plus two new cases in `test_verify_migration.py` for the new `check_trigger_exists_and_enabled` (a real pass, and a forced failure against a nonexistent trigger name).

**`verify_migration.py`'s own failing check was replaced, not patched.** The prior `check_real_privilege_denied`-against-`azure_pg_admin` assertion could never have passed — it was asserting the absence of a privilege that is structurally unconditional (`pg_write_all_data` membership). Replaced with `check_trigger_exists_and_enabled`, asserting the thing that actually determines whether the guarantee holds now. `check_real_privilege_denied` itself is kept, unchanged, as a correct general-purpose function — this was about one specific, impossible use of it, not the function being wrong. **`verify_migration.py`: 122/122.**

**The framing itself needed a second, more precise correction — the user caught that the first one ("holds for one identity, not another") still implied something had broken or weakened.** Neither the guarantee nor its enforcement ever weakened: the `REVOKE` held continuously, unchanged, before and after ADR-023. What was actually wrong was one sentence in this project's own documentation generalizing Task 31's real, correctly-targeted, rigorously-adversarial test of the *application* identity into an unqualified claim about the guarantee overall — a claim those four tests never established. This is explicitly a *different* lesson from the other five instances in Governance & Security Reference §6 (all of them genuinely untested guarantees) — this one was tested thoroughly and correctly, against the right identity, for the right reason; the gap was a documentation sentence outrunning its own evidence, not a testing lapse. Both ADR-028 and Governance & Security Reference §2 rewritten to state this precisely, and to say explicitly why Task 31's choice of `app_role_local_dev` was the right method, not merely an adequate one — the real threat model this guarantee protects against is an application bug or compromise, and that is exactly the identity every real code path in this project actually authenticates as.

Full suite: **150/150**, re-confirmed clean after the trigger (one transient connection-timeout re-run, non-reproducing — the same class of environment noise already documented in Task 45). `verify_migration.py`: **122/122.** `approval_records` untouched — the only writes this follow-up made were schema-level (the function, the trigger) and privilege-level (the temporary `GRANT`/`REVOKE TRIGGER` on `app_role`, confirmed reverted to exact baseline), no data changed.

**Public ingress opened, 2026-09-11, its own distinct final action, only after everything above passed.** `az containerapp ingress access-restriction remove -g onepulse-gr -n onepulse-bff --rule-name AllowDevMachineOnly` — confirmed via a direct `az containerapp ingress show`: `ipSecurityRestrictions: []`, `external: true`. Verified live, both directions, from the real public FQDN with no IP restriction in effect: an unauthenticated request to `GET /api/v1/programs` returns a real `401` (Easy Auth, not the now-removed IP allow-list, is the actual gate); the identical request with a real, valid bearer token returns a real `200` with real program data. This is the literal moment `onepulse-bff.wonderfulbay-b92b8b1b.centralus.azurecontainerapps.io` became reachable from any IP on the internet — Migration Plan Phase 8's own Definition of Done, now complete.

| Task 50: Pre-Phase-9 RAG index report follow-through — real prune step, freshness scoring profile, near-duplicate chat instruction (ADR-029) | Follow-up to the pre-Phase-9 RAG index report (index was 63 documents, only `singleSlide`/`Meridian Patient Portal`, including a leaked fixture row `report-999`; AOP/Leave Tracker entirely absent). Two recommendations accepted and to be implemented, then the real reindex run, not before. **Prune step, Option A:** the property to close is "what's in the index that Postgres no longer accounts for," not a one-off fix for report 999 — `merge_or_upload_documents` only adds/updates, never deletes, so an explicit reconciliation-and-delete pass is needed on every real ingest. The real Azure AI Search `$top=1000` per-request listing cap must be a guard in the code, not a note in a report — the corpus will cross it silently, and the failure mode of not guarding it is a wrong, over-broad deletion computed from a partial listing, not a loud error. Record the deferred alias-based safe rebuild with its real trigger condition (a schema change that can't be applied in place) so it's a decision deferred with a condition attached, not an option left to be rediscovered. **Near-duplicates, scoring profile + prompt instruction, not index-time deduplication:** discarding older weeks to fix ranking was considered and rejected — LLD 2.3 frames this assistant as an archive, and "how has this item's status changed across weeks" is a real, intended question. Fix it at the ranking layer, before the model can get it wrong, the same pattern as three prior real instances in this project (ADR-007's deterministic scoping, the code-enforced risk floor in `quality_gate.py`, the sha256 status-deck pin in Task 44) — this is a fourth instance of the identical reasoning, to be named as such in the ADR. **Then reindex, and prove it two ways:** the reindex completing proves the mechanism; report 999 confirmed gone from the index proves the prune step specifically; a real chat question where the same real AOP work item has different statuses across different weeks, with the assistant citing the current one, proves the near-duplicate fix | **Done 2026-09-11.** `scripts/ingest_reports_to_search.py` gained `MAX_INDEX_LISTING_PAGE = 1000` (a real, documented Azure AI Search per-request cap), `IndexListingTooLargeError` (raised, not silently paginated around, once a listing reaches the cap — real pagination is deliberately not built preemptively for a scale this project isn't at), `fetch_real_document_ids()` (the full, unscoped real Postgres ID set — pruning is a global reconciliation, not scoped to whatever a particular `--report-ids` run happened to touch), and `prune_stale_documents()` (lists the real index, computes the real difference, deletes it) — called unconditionally on every real ingest run, inside the same `search_client` context the upload already uses. `libs/onepulse_common/onepulse_common/search_index.py` gained a real Azure AI Search `ScoringProfile` (`RECENCY_SCORING_PROFILE_NAME = "recency_boost"`, a `FreshnessScoringFunction` on `week_of`, `boost=3.0`, `interpolation="quadratic"` — a sharper, front-loaded preference chosen deliberately over the linear default, to specifically separate consecutive near-duplicate weekly snapshots rather than gently favor recency across the whole `boosting_duration=90 days` window), wired into `build_index_definition()`'s `SearchIndex(scoring_profiles=[...])` and applied explicitly at the `hybrid_search()` call site (`scoring_profile=RECENCY_SCORING_PROFILE_NAME`), not left as a silent index-level default. `libs/onepulse_common/onepulse_common/chat_assistant.py`'s `CHAT_INSTRUCTIONS` gained a fifth numbered instruction: when multiple retrieved chunks describe the same real item across different weeks, prefer the most recent `week_of` for a current-status question and answer from that one; for a history question, use the full set and state what changed and when. **ADR-029 written** (`01_Architecture_Decision_Record.md`) covering the prune mechanism and why it makes ADR-022's "derived" claim actually reconcilable rather than merely rebuildable-in-principle, the `$top=1000` guard's real failure mode, the deferred alias-based rebuild with its explicit trigger condition, and the near-duplicate fix's lineage as the fourth real instance of "fix it before the model can get it wrong" (ADR-007, `quality_gate.py`'s risk floor, Task 44's sha256 deck pin, now this) — ADR-022's own existing bullet about a future `reindex` command updated in place to mark it real, cross-referencing ADR-029. Full suite re-run before reindexing: **153/153 passing.** **A real, unanticipated rate-limit hit on the first live reindex attempt, fixed rather than worked around:** embedding 963 real texts (21 report + 942 finding chunks — the real, current non-fixture corpus) in one call exceeded the embedding deployment's GlobalStandard S0 tier rate limit (`openai.RateLimitError`) — fixed with `embed_all()` (batches of 16, retry-after-cooldown on `RateLimitError`), added to the ingest script specifically since `onepulse_common.embeddings.embed_texts` itself stays a plain single-batch call, correct for every other real caller, which only ever embeds one question at a time. **The real reindex, run**: `python scripts/ingest_reports_to_search.py --target dev`, no `--report-ids` scoping — **963/963 documents uploaded**, **50 stale documents pruned** (`report-999` plus 49 superseded `report-1`..`report-50` chunks from Task 17's original ingestion; `report-51`, still real, correctly untouched). **Proof 1 (mechanism + report 999):** a direct, targeted `get_document(key="report-999")` post-reindex returned a real `ResourceNotFoundError` — confirmed gone, closing the specific instance that started this investigation. **Proof 2 (near-duplicate fix):** found a real AOP work item (`source_item_ref=332`, "Testing: Write automated tests for workload identity authentication") whose real `status_label` differs across real weekly reports (`Needs Human Review` weeks of 2026-08-21/22, `On Track` in the current week, 2026-09-07, report 454) — asked "What is the current status of the work item about writing automated tests for workload identity authentication in the Agentic AI Observability Platform program?" and got: *"Current status (most recent): ... On Track with System.State = Resolved... Previously (week of 2026-08-22) the program report listed WI 332... as pending human review"* — citing report 454 (current, primary) and report 361 (superseded, correctly offered as history rather than omitted or weighted equally), exactly the behavior the new prompt instruction specifies. Full suite unaffected by the reindex itself (no code path under test touches live search state): **153/153 passing throughout.** |

**Follow-up, same task, 2026-09-12 — the real interactive sign-in retried and succeeded; the diagnostic row from this task's own bug-verification found already approved, not merely at risk, and closed.**

**Real interactive sign-in, the one bar item only a human could produce, is now closed for real, not by bearer-token proxy.** The user completed it directly against the deployed `bff`: full cookie-based sign-in through a real Microsoft MFA challenge, "Signed in as owner" rendered from a real `GET /api/v1/me` call (never inferred client-side), and a correctly empty project selector matching the signed-in actor's real `actor_scope` at the time — confirmed via a real screenshot.
